EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

3 
 
 
3 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
On January 31, 2019, the New York City Housing Authority 
(“NYCHA”) committed to implementing essential reforms to improve 
housing conditions for NYCHA residents through an agreement (the 
“HUD Agreement” or “Agreement”) with the U.S. Attorney’s Office for 
the Southern District of New York (“SDNY”), the U.S. Department of 
Housing and Urban Development (“HUD”), and the City of New York 
(the “City”). As part of the terms, a monitor was appointed to oversee 
and evaluate adherence to the Agreement. After the first monitor 
completed a five-year term, on February 28, 2024, the current co-
Monitors (“Monitors”), Neil Barofsky and Matthew Cipolla of Jenner & 
Block LLP (“Jenner”), began their five-year term. This is their second 
annual report and seventh overall. 
In their prior reports, available on their website, the Monitors 
detailed their assessment of NYCHA’s compliance with the HUD 
Agreement, including NYCHA’s successes and challenges. In this 
report, and others going forward, the Monitors continue to provide 
regular 
updates 
on 
NYCHA’s 
progress 
in 
meeting 
those 
requirements. This report includes annual data for January 31, 2025, 
to January 31, 2026, the seventh year of the monitorship, unless 
otherwise noted.  
As detailed further in this report, NYCHA has continued to 
build on the progress described in the Monitors’ previous reports and 
should be commended for those efforts; at the same time, however, 
several important areas for improvement remain, with NYCHA’s 
progress regressing in certain areas. Full compliance with the HUD 
Agreement will require NYCHA to address these challenges while 
also continuing its efforts at cultural and organizational reform. It also 
needs to build upon its efforts to improve the delivery of services to 
residents, but to accomplish these objectives, NYCHA must have 
sufficient resources, both for operational expenses as well as for 
capital improvements to its aging buildings and infrastructure. 
Support from the federal government is critical, given that 
approximately two-thirds of NYCHA’s funding comes from federal 
grants. But contemplated federal budget cuts could result in NYCHA 
losing as much as $100 million in its federal support starting next year. 
No matter the gains in efficiency that NYCHA achieves through the 
reforms described and contemplated in this report, such a large 
reduction in funding would slow progress and lead to performance 
declines as staffing constraints, deferred maintenance, and reduced 
program capacity widen the gap between NYCHA’s current 

4 
 
4 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
performance and its commitments under the HUD Agreement. 
NYCHA’s residents, of course, would be the ones who would suffer 
the most, and for their benefit, the Monitors strongly encourage the 
federal government to maintain sufficient financial support for 
NYCHA.   
As with past reports, the Monitors again recognize and thank 
those who are working to improve NYCHA and help it fully meet its 
obligations under the HUD Agreement, including the constructive 
and collaborative approach taken by NYCHA’s leadership and staff; 
the vital feedback received from NYCHA residents and resident 
leaders who have taken time to engage with the Monitors; the 
cooperation of the leadership of Teamsters Local 237; and the 
expertise and engagement from SDNY and HUD officials and other 
stakeholders. 
Property Management 
NYCHA committed in the HUD Agreement to various 
obligations related to heat, elevators, pests and waste, lead, mold, 
and inspections. Further detail concerning these topics is contained 
in Section I of this report. As described further in that section, NYCHA 
has achieved signiﬁcant milestones, including:  
• 
Pests. In Year 7, NYCHA signiﬁcantly improved its 
response 
times 
to 
pest 
complaints 
for 
mice, 
cockroaches, and bed bugs, and is rapidly approaching 
compliance with the HUD Agreement requirements. 
NYCHA responded to 87% of these pest complaints 
within seven calendar days in Year 7, which although 
short of the 90% HUD Agreement requirement, still 
represents 
a 
22 
percentage 
point 
increase 
in 
performance from the prior year. Similarly, NYCHA 
responded to 97% of these pest complaints within ten 
calendar days in Year 7, short of the 100% requirement 
but an 11 percentage point increase from last year. 
NYCHA also continues to meet its rat complaint 
response rate requirement, responding to 98% of rat 
complaints within two business days and 99% within ﬁve 
calendar days. Also, for the ﬁrst time, NYCHA began 
publishing estimates of the rat, mouse, cockroach, and 

5 
 
 
5 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
bed bug populations for each development as required 
by the HUD Agreement.  
 
• 
Lead. Over the last year, NYCHA has attempted X-Ray 
Fluorescence (“XRF”) testing in all units known or 
presumed to have lead-based paint and completed XRF 
testing for approximately 97% of these units. XRF testing 
is essential for identifying lead paint hazards early and 
guiding their safe removal to protect residents’ health. 
NYCHA remains on track to meet its HUD Agreement 
obligation to abate 50% of apartments in need of 
abatement by 2029, assuming that NYCHA remains at 
or above its average rate of abatement over the last 
three years.  
 
• 
Mold. In Year 7, NYCHA made improvements in 
addressing the incidence of mold, an area that has long 
proved challenging. NYCHA met HUD Agreement 
remediation timeframes for mold conditions 29% of the 
time, which although still far short of the HUD 
Agreement requirement of 95%, represents signiﬁcant 
improvement from its 9% compliance rate in Year 6. 
Average remediation time, which is a key predictor of 
NYCHA’s ability to continue to improve on its response 
rate, also improved dramatically, dropping from an 
average of 120 days in Year 4 to 12 days in Year 7, a 90% 
improvement. Timely work plan printing improved as 
well. In Year 7, NYCHA provided residents with written 
work plans—documents explaining how mold issues and 
their root causes would be addressed—within ﬁve 
business days of a veriﬁed complaint 90% of the time, 
short of the 95% requirement but up signiﬁcantly from 
55% in Year 6.  
 
• 
Elevators. Although a year late, NYCHA completed its 
obligation to replace or modernize at least 275 
elevators, with 109 replacements completed in 2025. 
NYCHA also maintained its in-compliance status for the 

6 
 
6 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
second year in a row by restoring service within ten 
hours for 89% of unplanned elevator car outages. 
 
• 
Inspections. In 2025, an increasing number of NYCHA 
developments passed HUD inspections. The agency 
passed 64 of 89 inspections (72%), a signiﬁcant 
improvement over 2024, when NYCHA passed only 
46% of 138 inspections. NYCHA also increased the 
reach of its annual self-inspections, completing them in 
95% of occupied apartments, up from 91% the previous 
year, although still short of the 100% requirement. These 
improvements reﬂect NYCHA’s continued focus on 
ensuring safer, well-maintained living conditions for 
residents.  
 
• 
Heat. NYCHA has remained on track to comply with the 
obligations requiring restoration of heat to apartments 
affected by central heating outages: ﬁrst, it restores heat 
to apartments affected by central heating outages 
within an average of 12 hours; and second, it restores 
heat in apartments within 12 hours for 85% of the time 
for such outages. The agency also completed its 
obligation to address 497 boilers a year sooner than the 
Agreement required by replacing 198 boilers itself and 
transferring 698 boilers to PACT partners, who replaced 
462 of them, subject to veriﬁcation by the Monitors. 
At the same time, as detailed further in the report, there are 
several notable areas that require continued focus, including the 
following: 
• 
Mold. The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to address 
mold growths and the underlying causes, such as leaks, 
within set timeframes at least 95% of the time. As noted 
above, although NYCHA has improved its performance, 
it is still well short of these requirements. Moreover, the 
severity of conditions remains a concern: 85% of mold 
complaints involve Large Mold Growths—areas of visible 
mold covering ten or more square feet. The combined 
mold and leak backlog—over 65,000 open work orders—
continues to be a persistent challenge to NYCHA timely 
addressing mold and leak work orders. 

7 
 
 
7 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
• 
Heat. Although not a standalone requirement of the 
HUD Agreement, a key part of NYCHA’s ability to 
provide 
consistent 
heating 
service 
to 
NYCHA 
apartments is to limit the number of instances where 
residents are impacted by a development-wide heating 
service interruption—an unplanned heating outage. 
During the ﬁrst half of the 2025-2026 heating season, 
there have been 307 unplanned central heating outages, 
up from 206 during the ﬁrst half of last season. These 
unplanned outages have affected 61% of NYCHA's 
occupied apartments, as compared to 54% as of this 
time last season. 
 
• 
Lead. Remediating lead-based paint hazards is essential 
to keeping residents safe from serious health risks. 
NYCHA continues to work through a backlog of paint 
remediation work orders. Of 19,835 remediation work 
orders identiﬁed in December 2024, NYCHA has 
completed 9,412 (47.5%) and cancelled 610 (3.1%), 
leaving 9,813 still open. NYCHA reports that it has 
attempted to address 99% of these remaining work 
orders, but many remain unresolved because NYCHA 
cannot access the apartments or additional work is 
needed.  
• 
Inspections. 
NYCHA 
must 
continue 
to 
make 
improvements to its self-inspections process in order to 
more quickly remediate health and safety concerns and 
improve its performance on HUD inspections. The 
Monitors analyzed the deﬁciencies that HUD identiﬁed 
in its inspections and found numerous categories that 
NYCHA could and should have caught in its self-
inspections, including missing smoke detectors and 
non-GFCI outlets in kitchens and bathrooms. 
• 
Elevators. After improving year-over-year from Year 3 to 
Year 6, NYCHA's performance on most elevator-related 
obligations stayed the same or declined in Year 7. Most 
notably, NYCHA restored service after unplanned 
elevator outages in an average of 6.5 hours per outage, 
which was 16% worse than its average of 5.6 hours last 
year. 

8 
 
8 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
• Pests. NYCHA tracks estimated pest populations to 
monitor building conditions and prioritize pest control 
efforts. In Year 7, NYCHA’s estimated pest populations 
increased from Year 6, with a 19% increase for rats, 39% for 
mice, 21% for bed bugs, and 4% for cockroaches. While 
the Monitors recognize that overall NYCHA has reduced 
the estimated populations of pests since the outset of the 
monitorship, with a 31% decrease for rats, 58% decrease 
for mice, 80% decrease for bed bugs, and 69% decrease 
for cockroaches since Year 1, the Monitors nevertheless 
highlight these recent increases in the estimated pest 
populations so that they can be promptly addressed and 
NYCHA can continue its work to further improve pest 
management at its developments. 
 
The Monitors continue to closely observe NYCHA’s activity in 
all these areas, and the others detailed further in this report, and 
continue 
to 
work 
collaboratively 
with 
NYCHA 
on 
further 
improvements.  
Organizational Change 
NYCHA committed under the HUD Agreement to change its 
management, organizational, and workforce structure in a manner to 
ensure sustained compliance with the Agreement’s requirements. 
That commitment is embodied in the Transformation Plan, which 
contains both broad principles for improvement and specific 
initiatives intended to improve NYCHA’s processes and performance.  
Further detail concerning these topics is included in Section II 
of this report. That section outlines the current status of the 47 
initiatives that NYCHA proposed in its Transformation Plan.   
Among the highlights of that progress, NYCHA has continued 
to develop and improve its training programs, including by 
improving maintenance worker training, an initiative that will be 
critical to improving both worker efficiency and the overall culture of 
service at NYCHA. In the last year, NYCHA, with support from the 
Monitors, launched the Maintenance Worker Muster Overhaul 
Program, which focuses on strengthening daily maintenance 
operations through the development of critical skills and the fostering 
of a culture of accountability, continuous improvement, and 
professionalism. Alongside the new Maintenance Worker Musters, 

9 
 
 
9 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
NYCHA established the Maintenance Workers Recognition Program, 
which is designed to highlight and elevate the essential contributions 
of NYCHA’s frontline workforce in a manner that reinforces 
organizational values and promotes a positive workplace culture.  
NYCHA has also launched what it calls its “Learning Leaders” 
program, which aims to improve training and leverage NYCHA’s 
existing workforce by identifying and equipping high-performing 
employees to deliver training to others in the organization. Selected 
through a competitive screening and training process, 42 employees 
have been trained to become Learning Leaders and were tasked with 
delivering a course called “Be a Customer Service Superhero,” which 
covers topics such as active listening, de-escalation, empathy, and a 
service-oriented mindset. This Learning Leaders process allows 
NYCHA to expand training capacity beyond the existing full time 
training staff who work for its Learning and Development department 
and leverage the institutional trust earned by NYCHA’s highest-
performing workers. As of this date, 986 employees have completed 
the “Be a Customer Service Superhero” training taught by Learning 
Leaders. NYCHA plans to train a total of 1,200 employees through 
this program in the coming months, and the majority of its workforce 
by the end of 2027.  
For NYCHA to continue to improve as an organization, it must 
have modernized Information Technology systems to maximize 
efficiency and accountability, and NYCHA has undertaken several 
major IT projects as part of its transformation. However, over the last 
year, these projects continued to experience delays despite 
significant investment of time and resources. For example, NYCHA 
has faced significant challenges in its effort to replace its outdated 
Resident Case Management System (“RCMS”). In this report, the 
Monitors provide observations regarding the failures of that process, 
and recommendations for how it should be improved. NYCHA is 
hiring new IT leadership and has committed to sharing the Monitors’ 
observations with candidates to ensure they are prepared to take 
action to complete the ongoing IT overhauls as quickly as possible.  
NYCHA and the Monitors have also collaborated on several HR 
initiatives in Year 7, including developing a performance evaluation 
process and improving employee onboarding. Over the last year, 
NYCHA completed its first performance evaluations for managerial 
employees with 97.6% participation and has begun incorporating 
Monitor recommendations for the next cycle. The development of 

10 
 
10 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
nonmanagerial evaluations has proven to be slower, though NYCHA 
is developing a pilot program which is expected to begin in early 
2026. The Monitors are also assisting NYCHA with improving 
deficiencies in its employee onboarding process. Improving the 
onboarding, training, evaluation, and accountability of NYCHA 
employees will be a necessary component in NYCHA’s efforts toward 
full compliance with the HUD Agreement. 
Capital Expenditures 
In Year 7, the Monitors continued to track NYCHA’s 
management of City Capital Action Plan (“CCAP”) funding under the 
HUD Agreement. Following the Monitors’ First Annual Report 
(published in April 2025), NYCHA received an additional $200 million 
in CCAP funds from the City for fiscal year 2026, bringing the total 
funding under the Agreement to $1.6 billion.  
Spending under the Agreement increased steadily throughout 
Year 7. At the start of Year 7, NYCHA had spent approximately $300 
million of its CCAP funds and had an approximately $1.1 billion 
remaining under contract. Over the course of Year 7, NYCHA spent 
$583 million and entered into $462 million in new contracts. As of 
January 31, 2026, NYCHA has spent a total of approximately $880 
million, with approximately $1 billion remaining under contract.1  
In February 2026, SDNY and HUD approved an amendment to 
the CCAP (the “2026 Amendment”) that reallocates $50 million from 
waste management capital projects to heating and plumbing 
initiatives. 
The 
2026 
Amendment 
reduces 
NYCHA’s 
CCAP 
commitments for waste yards and interior compactors and 
accelerates the timeline for completing the remaining waste 
management projects from 2030 to the end of 2028. The reallocated 
funds will support heating system improvements and a new Waste 
Plumbing Initiative (“WPI”) that will replace severely deteriorated 
waste plumbing lines and renovate kitchens and bathrooms in 
approximately 300 apartments, addressing persistent leaks and mold 
that cannot be resolved through surface-level repairs alone. The 
impacts of the 2026 Amendment on the waste management and 
mold and leaks capital programs are discussed further in Sections I.3 
and I.4 of this report. 
The Monitors’ ongoing review of CCAP projects includes 
tracking project progress, conducting site visits, examining delays, 

11 
 
 
11 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
evaluating the resulting operating impact, and regularly meeting with 
NYCHA’s Asset & Capital Management team. 
Monitors’ Budget and Stakeholder Engagement 
Section III of this report describes in detail the $19,902,453.90 
budget for work on the monitorship and also summarizes the 
Monitors’ engagement with residents and other stakeholders in Year 
7. 
* * * 
 
Pursuant to Paragraph 28 of the HUD Agreement, NYCHA does 
not yet meet the criteria for termination. Further detail is set forth in 
the remainder of the report.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
I.1 
Heat
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to improve heating 
conditions for NYCHA residents through a variety of measures, such 
as (1) replacing a specified number of boilers, (2) establishing 
systems for responding to apartments affected by in-apartment 
heating failures, (3) investigating failures to timely restore heat, (4) 
notifying residents of heating outages, (5) monitoring the 
temperature in apartments, and (6) reducing the duration and 
number of heating service interruptions experienced by residents.2 
The section below provides an update on NYCHA’s progress with 
respect to outstanding heating-related obligations under the 
Agreement for the first half of the 2025-2026 heating season (from 
October 1, 2025 to January 31, 2026). 
A.
Mitchel Houses
On October 1, 2025, the chimney connected to the boiler
room providing heat and hot water to Mitchel Houses collapsed.3 
There were no reported injuries or fatalities from the collapse. As 
discussed in the Monitors’ December 2025 Report, this collapse 
required temporary relocation of some residents and interruption of 
gas service to all residents in the development. 4  Multiple city 
agencies, including the Fire Department of the City of New York, the 
Department of Buildings, and the Department of Investigation, are 
investigating the cause of the chimney collapse. NYCHA has 
represented that it is cooperating fully with these investigations. The 
Monitors will share findings from these investigations once they are 
available.  
B.
NYCHA’s Boiler Replacements (Requirement No. H1)
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to “replace or address
approximately 500 boilers by 2026,” and it allows for some to be 
replaced by NYCHA and some to be replaced by PACT partners.5 
Under the parties’ interpretation of this provision, so long as at least 
200 boilers are transferred to and addressed by PACT partners, the 
remaining boiler replacements could occur either at NYCHA 
developments or at properties transferred to PACT partners. 
Accordingly, NYCHA completed its boiler replacement 
obligation by December 31, 2025, as follows: 
In-Apartment Heating 
Failures: Conditions 
where the heating 
system inside an 
apartment does not 
function properly due to 
issues with in-apartment 
components such as 
convectors, distribution 
pipes or related parts, 
like clogged traps, that 
require cleaning to 
restore proper heating. 
An in-apartment heating 
failure can impact a 
single room in the 
apartment or the entire 
apartment. 
Heating Outage: A 
failure of the central 
heating system that 
prevents the system from 
delivering heat to 
multiple apartments. It 
does not include 
instances when heat is 
not being provided to an 
individual apartment, 
such as when a single 
radiator malfunctions. 
Heating Service 
Interruptions: This term 
consists of heating 
outages and in-
apartment heating 
failures. 

16 
 
16 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
• 
Replaced 
198 
boilers 
at 
NYCHA-managed 
developments;6 and 
• 
Transferred 698 boilers to PACT partners, who have 
thus far replaced 462 of those boilers, and repaired 
another 20.  
In other words, because a total of 660 boilers have been 
replaced, which includes the minimum of 200 boilers that had first 
been transferred to PACT partners, the Monitors deem this obligation 
to be complete, subject to the Monitors’ verification of PACT partners’ 
boiler replacements. 
Boiler replacements are important to NYCHA’s efforts to 
improve its heating service for residents. In addition to the propensity 
of older boilers to break down because they are being used beyond 
their life expectancy, newer boilers offer greater reliability because 
they use modern controls, improved materials, and higher-efficiency 
technology, which reduces stress on equipment and lowers the 
likelihood of boiler-related heating service interruptions.7  
While it is commendable that NYCHA completed its obligation 
to replace or address boilers a year early, continued investment in 
heating infrastructure is vital for NYCHA to improve its performance 
on heating-related obligations. NYCHA must continue replacing 
boilers because of its aging infrastructure, discussed further in 
Section I.1.C.3. To that end, in 2026, NYCHA plans to complete 
replacement of an additional 104 boilers. The HUD Agreement also 
requires NYCHA to evaluate heating infrastructure needs beyond 
boilers, such as “replacement of underground heating distribution 
systems,” and to “take appropriate action” to improve the heating 
system. 8  According to NYCHA, it addresses distribution system 
needs through three approaches: 
• 
Building distribution upgrades—such as condensate 
tank replacements, steam leak repairs, zone valve 
installations, and Building Management Systems 
improvements—alongside boiler replacement projects 
when funding allows;  
• 
Investing in full underground steam pipe replacement 
at targeted developments through capital projects 
Heating Season: The 
heating season runs from 
October 1 in one year to 
May 31 in the next year. 
This is the period when 
resident-occupied 
buildings in New York 
City are required to be 
provided with heat from 
a central heating system.  
Heating System: The 
full set of equipment and 
infrastructure required to 
deliver heat to occupied 
apartments. This end-to-
end system includes 
central generation 
equipment (such as 
boiler plants), 
distribution components 
(including tank rooms, 
pipes, risers, and valves), 
and in-apartment 
equipment (such as 
convectors and 
radiators). A failure or 
condition requiring 
servicing within these 
components can affect 
the delivery of heat to 
apartments.  
Building Management 
Systems: The 
computerized controls 
that provide automation, 
remote monitoring, and 
remote control for 
building mechanical 
systems such as boiler 
plants.  

17 
 
 
17 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
managed by NYCHA’s Asset & Capital Management 
Division (“A&CM”); and 
• 
Conducting operational repairs to building distribution 
systems 
across 
developments 
through 
NYCHA’s 
Heating Management Services Department (“HMSD”), 
including vacuum tanks, underground steam lines, and 
zone valves, with priority given to developments with 
the highest heat complaint volumes.9    
Investing in heating distribution systems will improve heating 
service delivery, but NYCHA requires at least $4 billion in additional 
funding to fully repair or replace existing distribution lines. The 
Monitors are evaluating the scope and adequacy of NYCHA’s 
distribution system investments to assess whether NYCHA is taking 
“appropriate action” to address its developments’ broader heating 
infrastructure needs as required by the HUD Agreement.  
C. 
Mandatory Minimum Indoor Temperature Violations 
(Requirement Nos. H6-H8) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to ensure apartments 
stay above the mandatory minimum temperatures required by the 
New York City Code during the heating season.10 Beginning with the 
2024-2025 heating season, the Agreement sets limits on instances 
when an apartment’s temperature falls below the mandatory 
minimum indoor temperature. 11  The Agreement specifies that 
NYCHA’s compliance with these obligations will be assessed 
according to procedures agreed upon in an Action Plan.12 
As discussed in the Monitors’ September 2025 Report, 
because NYCHA cannot currently provide reliable in-apartment 
temperature data, the Monitors use an interim methodology that 
tracks 
occupied 
apartments 
affected 
by 
heating 
service 
interruptions. 13 A key limitation is that this methodology does not 
indicate whether apartments experienced temperatures below the 
legal limit. 14  For example, it is possible that in some cases the 
temperature in an apartment that experiences a heating interruption 
for a limited period of time does not fall below the mandatory 
minimum temperature. The Monitors use this methodology here to 
describe heating equipment performance for the 2025-2026 heating 
season as of January 31, 2026.  
Mandatory Minimum 
Temperature: From 
October 1 to May 31 (the 
“Heating Season”), 
during the day (6:00 a.m. 
to 10:00 p.m.), 
apartments must be at 
least 68°F when the 
outside temperature is 
below 55°F, and at least 
62°F at night (10:00 p.m. 
to 6:00 a.m.) regardless 
of the outside 
temperature. 

18 
 
18 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
This heating season, NYCHA began implementing a 
procedure to measure in-apartment temperatures when responding 
to verified heating complaints, which involves the agency’s staff 
taking temperature readings when they arrive at, and then later 
depart from, an apartment. The Monitors are evaluating whether this 
procedure produces reliable temperature readings that can be used 
to assess NYCHA’s compliance with its obligations under the HUD 
Agreement. The Monitors will provide a comprehensive assessment 
of this methodology in future reporting. Once this methodology has 
been approved, the goal will be for NYCHA to incorporate the 
finalized methodology into an Action Plan by the end of the year.

Agreement requires NYCHA to ensure that no occupied apartment 
experiences more than three instances when temperatures fall below 
the legal minimum.
21  As discussed above, without reliable

occupied apartments were repeatedly affected by heating service 
interruptions, as indicated by NYCHA’s repair and maintenance

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT

3. 
This 
Season’s 
Increase 
in 
Heating 
Service 
Interruptions 
NYCHA residents have experienced a notable increase in 
heating service interruptions this heating season, especially in the 
number of unplanned heating outages, as compared to the last 
season during the same period. As of the first half of this heating 
season, NYCHA developments have experienced 307 unplanned 
outages, which is significantly higher than the 206 unplanned 
outages during the first half of last heating season and roughly equal 
to the 305 unplanned outages during the first half of the 2021-2022 
heating season.22  

25 
 
 
25 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
NYCHA and the Monitors have been looking into potential root 
causes for the increase in unplanned heating outages this season, as 
this information could guide NYCHA’s maintenance and investment 
efforts during the annual overhaul to mitigate the number of heating 
service interruptions in future heating seasons. To do so, the Monitors 
have reviewed outage work order data alongside information about 
outside temperature, field observations, and capital projects, and 
compared data from this heating season and the last heating season. 
Preliminary observations are included below, though as of this report, 
isolating definitive root causes for the increase has proven difficult 
given the number of variables affecting heating system performance. 
The Monitors will share additional findings in future reporting. 
Because 
heating 
demand 
fluctuates 
with 
outside 
temperatures, which may impact performance, the Monitors’ analysis 
begins with the Heating Degree Days (“HDDs”) for the 2024-2025 
and 2025-2026 heating seasons. Colder temperatures equate to 
higher HDDs, which means heating demand increases to keep 
apartments warm. During the first half of the 2025-2026 heating 
season, there were 2,705 HDDs, an increase of approximately 16% as 
compared to the first half of the 2024-2025 heating season. 23 As 
noted in the table below, this heating season has been colder than 
the last heating season so far, and every month this season has been 
colder than the corresponding month last season. 
Annual Overhaul: 
Annual overhaul refers to 
NYCHA’s yearly 
preventive and 
corrective maintenance 
performed on heating 
service equipment 
starting around February 
and finishing by 
October, with most of 
the work taking place in 
the summer. The annual 
overhaul involves 
performing maintenance 
on all heating equipment 
(e.g., cleaning, 
lubricating, and 
replacing worn 
components), 
conducting thorough 
equipment inspections 
to identify all needed 
repairs and 
replacements, and then 
completing this work. 
Heating Degree Days 
(“HDDs”): A measure of 
how cold it is over a 
period of time, 
calculated based on the 
number of degrees the 
daily average 
temperature falls below 
a set baseline (typically 
65°F). Higher HDD 
values indicate greater 
demand for heat. 

26 
 
26 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
To help interpret NYCHA’s performance, the Monitors 
assessed HDDs alongside counts of planned and unplanned outages 
for the 2024-2025 and 2025-2026 heating seasons. 24  The graph 
below suggests that colder outside temperatures, especially earlier 
in the heating season, are associated with an increase in the number 
of unplanned heating outages. Just as each month of this heating 
season has been colder than the corresponding month during last 
heating season, there have been more unplanned outages each 
month of this season as compared to the corresponding month 
during last season. 
 

27 
 
 
27 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
NYCHA has represented that colder temperatures limit its 
ability to perform planned maintenance or repairs during the heating 
season, and its aging heating infrastructure is more susceptible to 
failure without this ongoing maintenance. The Monitors sought to 
quantify the correlation between age of the boilers and heating 
performance, but NYCHA does not track the age of its boilers or other 
heating assets comprehensively. The Monitors have noted through 
field inspections that several of NYCHA’s boilers are beyond their 
useful life, and that limited sample testing suggests hundreds more 
across NYCHA’s portfolio are likely in similar condition.25  
The Monitors also analyzed NYCHA’s heating outage work 
order data, specifically the reported cause and repair codes, to 
explore whether particular issues with NYCHA’s heating system could 
account for the increase. Thus far, the Monitors have not found trends 
in this data to account for the increase in unplanned outages this 
season, as compared to last season, and the analysis is ongoing. 
Notably, the most common repair code this season has been flame 
failure, which occurs when the flame inside a boiler extinguishes such 
that the boiler is no longer producing heat.26 However, flame failure 
has been the most common repair code in nearly every prior heating 
season as well, suggesting it does not account for this season’s 
increase. 
NYCHA’s preparation for the current heating season was very 
similar to its preparation for last heating season, if not slightly better. 
As discussed in the Monitors’ December 2025 Report, NYCHA’s 
annual overhaul prepares heating equipment for the heating 
season.27 Prior to the 2025-2026 heating season, NYCHA completed 
all preventive maintenance work orders and inspections, and of the 
highest priority corrective maintenance work orders, NYCHA 
completed 66% by October 1 and 97% by October 31. 28  As in 
previous years, because of limited staffing and resources, NYCHA 
was unable to complete all lower priority corrective maintenance 
work orders during the 2025 annual overhaul, and some of NYCHA’s 
boilers remained offline on October 1, though NYCHA considered all 
developments to be heat ready before October 1 because they had 
working boilers in place. However, there were fewer outstanding 
corrective maintenance work orders and fewer boilers offline to start 
this season as compared to last season.29  
NYCHA and the Monitors will continue to investigate potential 
factors contributing to the increase in heating service interruptions, 
Corrective 
Maintenance: 
Corrective maintenance 
consists of repairing or 
replacing damaged or 
malfunctioning 
components such as 
burners, pumps, valves, 
control systems, and 
piping; performing 
hydrostatic tests to 
detect leaks; and 
restoring full operational 
capacity to ensure safe 
and reliable boiler 
function. 
Preventive 
Maintenance: For 
heating, preventive 
maintenance consists of 
cleaning, lubricating, 
adjusting, repairing, and 
replacing worn 
components, and 
ensuring equipment and 
mechanical areas are in 
satisfactory operating 
condition. 
Cause and Repair 
Codes: The standardized 
codes used in NYCHA's 
Maximo system to 
categorize the cause of a 
heat outage (cause code) 
and the work performed 
to resolve it (repair 
code). Accurate coding 
helps NYCHA identify 
patterns, target 
preventive maintenance, 
and track the 
effectiveness of repairs. 

28 
 
28 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
including heating outages, as the heating season continues, and 
these findings will guide NYCHA’s preparations for the next heating 
season. Regardless of the ultimate causes of this season’s increase, 
NYCHA’s aging infrastructure means it will need to continue 
replacing boilers at a significant pace beyond what the HUD 
Agreement required. NYCHA’s current capital plan includes 
replacing 104 more boilers and continuing to assess whether the 
distribution systems at those developments need upgrades as well. 
NYCHA also plans on moving away from “in-kind boiler 
replacements”—replacing an aging boiler with a new boiler of the 
same type and fuel source—where possible, because in-kind 
replacements are more costly and perpetuate NYCHA’s reliance on 
fossil fuels, so it is exploring alternative heating solutions such as 
electrification. The Monitors will work with NYCHA to evaluate how to 
best deploy its capital resources to address these ongoing 
infrastructure needs. 
D. 
Heat Restoration Obligations (Requirement Nos. H6, H9, 
H11) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to restore heat in a 
timely manner when an apartment loses heat. Since its inception, the 
Agreement has required NYCHA to restore heat to affected 
apartments, on average, within 12 hours per heating service 
interruption. 30 Beginning with the 2024-2025 heating season, the 
Agreement requires NYCHA to restore heat to affected apartments 
within 12 hours for 85% of heating service interruptions, and to 
restore heat to affected apartments within 24 hours for all heating 
service interruptions.31  
Although the heat restoration obligations apply to both 
heating outages, which affect multiple apartments, and in-apartment 
heating failures, the Monitors did not report on in-apartment heating 
failures until the Monitors’ September 2025 Report because no 
agreed-upon methodology was in place. In that report, the Monitors 
presented NYCHA’s compliance with the HUD Agreement 
obligations for restoration times following in-apartment heating 
failures for the first time, using the methodology developed by 
NYCHA and the Monitors to measure restoration times based on 
residents’ verified heating complaints. 32  The Monitors use this 
methodology here to describe NYCHA’s response to in-apartment 
heating failures for the 2025-2026 heating season as of January 31, 
2026. 
Heat Ready: Heat ready 
refers to NYCHA’s 
assessment that the 
boilers at a development 
are capable of providing 
sufficient heat to the 
residents of the 
development in the 
upcoming heating 
season. Because many 
developments have 
boiler rooms with more 
than the necessary 
number of boilers, not all 
boilers must be 
operational in a 
development’s boiler 
room before NYCHA 
considers the 
development to be heat 
ready.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
1. 
Twelve-Hour 
Average 
Heat 
Restoration 
(Requirement No. H9) 
Beginning with the 2019-2020 heating season, the HUD 
Agreement requires NYCHA to restore heat to affected apartments in 
an average of 12 hours per heating service interruption.33 
Heating Outages. As of the first half of the 2025-2026 heating 
season, NYCHA is once again on track to meet this requirement, as 
illustrated in the graph below. NYCHA has met this requirement in 
every heating season of the monitorship since its inception.  
As of January 31, 2026, NYCHA has restored heat to 
apartments affected by heating outages in an average of 6.9 hours 
this season, 0.4 hours faster than during the same period last season.  
 
In-Apartment Heating Failures. By contrast, for in-apartment 
heating failures, NYCHA has not yet met this requirement for an entire 
heating season. However, NYCHA has improved year-over-year for 
the past few heating seasons, including during the first half of the 
current season. 
As of January 31, 2026, NYCHA has restored heat to 
apartments affected by in-apartment heating failures in an average of 
12.3 hours this season, 2.7 hours faster than during the same period 
last season.  

30 
 
30 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
NYCHA attributes its improvements in restoring heat to 
apartments affected by in-apartment heating failures to operational 
changes in its response to resident complaints. Specifically, NYCHA’s 
heating operations unit, HMSD, began daily management meetings 
to review and address open work orders, targeting those that were 
put in the night before, and HMSD directed more experienced 
employees to travel between developments to assist the employees 
on site at a development when those buildings experience a spike in 
resident heat complaints.

E. 
Heating Failure Investigations (Requirement No. H14) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to investigate and 
report on instances when it takes more than 12 hours to restore heat 

34 
 
34 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
to a particular apartment. 36  NYCHA’s Environmental Health and 
Safety (“EH&S”) Department conducts root cause failure analysis 
investigations for heating outages that impact multiple apartments 
lasting more than 12 hours and issues recommended corrective 
actions. Beginning this heating season, EH&S will also conduct 
investigations of certain in-apartment heating failures that last longer 
than 12 hours.37 The Monitors are collaborating with EH&S to develop 
a methodology to identify which in-apartment heating failures EH&S 
will investigate. 
During the first half of the 2025-2026 heating season, NYCHA 
had 21 heating outages that lasted longer than 12 hours for which 
EH&S will conduct root cause failure investigations.38   
From these investigations during prior heating seasons, EH&S 
has issued findings and made recommendations for corrective 
action. Of the more than 400 recommendations made by EH&S in 
prior heating seasons, as of January 31, 2026, 20 remain open.39 The 
Monitors will provide more details on any open recommendations 
remaining after the conclusion of the heating season. 
F. 
Electronic Temperature Monitoring (Requirement Nos. H4, 
H5, H15) 
As of January 31, 2025, NYCHA met the HUD Agreement 
requirement to install temperature sensors in at least 30% of the 
apartments in 44 developments with modernized BMS.40 NYCHA is 
continuing its efforts to install BMS and temperature sensors at 
additional developments, consistent with its 2019 Heat Action Plan.41 
Although NYCHA has made real-time temperature data from 
sensors available to personnel responsible for heating, as well as to 
the Monitors, HUD, and SDNY, as the HUD Agreement requires, 
NYCHA is not publishing this data on its website due to concerns 
about the accuracy of sensor readings.42 For this reason, the Monitors 
have not deemed NYCHA to be in full compliance with these 
requirements.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
I.2 
Elevators 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to (1) reduce the 
frequency and duration of occasions when all of the elevator cars in a 
bank are not in service (“no-service conditions”);43 (2) reduce the 
frequency and duration of outages for all elevator cars; 44 (3) give 
prompt notice about elevator car outages and collect data about 
them; 45 (4) replace a set number of elevators; 46 (5) install remote 
elevator monitoring systems; 47  and (6) establish schedules for 
cleaning elevators and other planned elevator car outages that limit 
the inconvenience caused to residents.48 The section below provides 
an update on NYCHA’s progress with respect to outstanding 
elevator-related obligations under the Agreement for Year 7 of the 
monitorship (from February 1, 2025 to January 31, 2026).  
The Monitors will continue to track and support NYCHA’s 
efforts to complete its outstanding obligations. The HUD Agreement 
obligations that NYCHA met prior to, or were superseded before, 
Year 7 may be found in Appendix A. 
A. 
Elevator Replacements (Requirement Nos. E1, E2) 
The HUD Agreement required NYCHA to replace 275 
elevators in its buildings by December 31, 2024.49 NYCHA met this 
obligation in the fourth quarter of 2025, completing construction on 
109 elevators in 2025, for a total of 277 elevator replacements as of 
December 31, 2025.  
The HUD Agreement also required NYCHA to transfer 150 
additional elevators to third-party management through the PACT 
program by December 31, 2024, and for PACT developers to replace 
those elevators “as needed.”50 NYCHA represented that it transferred 
at least 150 elevators to PACT developers by August 31, 2024. The 
Monitors have verified that NYCHA’s PACT partners modernized or 
replaced at least 150 elevators.  
In 2026, NYCHA plans to complete another 22 elevator 
replacements and begin the process for an additional 205 elevator 
replacements. As of January 31, 2026, NYCHA manages 2,877 total 
elevators but will continue to transfer some elevators to third-party 
management as more developments transition to PACT partners. 
. 
No-Service Condition: 
The HUD Agreement 
defines “no-service 
conditions” to include 
only a situation where all 
elevators are out of 
service at one building. 
However, NYCHA, HUD, 
and SDNY have agreed 
that it is consistent with 
the purpose of the 
Agreement for “no-
service conditions” to 
also include a situation 
where all elevators that 
serve an apartment are 
out of service, which 
generally means all 
elevators in one bank are 
out of service. For 
example, a single 
building may have one 
bank of elevators that 
serves the northern half 
of the building, and a 
second bank of elevators 
that serves the southern 
half of the building. If all 
of the elevators that 
serve the northern half of 
the building stop 
working, that would be 
considered a no-service 
condition because the 
apartments on the 
northern half of the 
building cannot be 
accessed by elevator, 
even though other 
elevators in the building 
continue to function. 

44 
 
44 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
B. 
Low Voltage Outages 
Low voltage conditions may cause elevators to shut down 
automatically to prevent unsafe operation. This past summer, Con 
Edison imposed voltage reductions, one type of low voltage 
condition, in certain sectors of the electrical grid serving New York 
City to help avoid blackouts during heat waves. These voltage 
reductions caused elevator outages at many NYCHA buildings. 51 
Although the HUD Agreement does not exclude outages due to low 
voltage conditions (“low voltage outages”) from NYCHA’s elevator-
related obligations, the Monitors have provided data that illustrates 
the extent to which low voltage conditions impact NYCHA’s elevator 
performance. As a general matter, low voltage outages have 
contributed to a decline in NYCHA’s performance for Year 7. When 
low voltage outages are excluded from the data, for the most part 
NYCHA either matched its previous performance or its performance 
declined a small amount, with the average duration of unplanned 
outages as a notable exception. 
The impact of low voltage conditions on residents can be 
mitigated by installing Automatic Voltage Regulators (AVRs). AVRs 
are devices installed on existing elevators that stabilize incoming 
electrical voltage, allowing elevators to continue operating safely and 
reliably despite fluctuations in power supply. 52 The installation of 
AVRs on existing elevators helps reduce low voltage outages, 
including those caused by voltage reductions. As discussed in the 
Monitors’ September 2025 Report, NYCHA previously installed AVRs 
for 13 elevators, and those elevators have experienced fewer low 
voltage outages. 53 Since that report, NYCHA identified funding to 
purchase and install AVRs for up to 21 more elevators and plans to 
first install nine AVRs already in its possession beginning in April 
2026. As of the date of this report, NYCHA has not yet started the 
process for purchasing the 12 additional AVRs.  
Only a small fraction of NYCHA’s elevators will receive AVRs 
under projected funding, with NYCHA prioritizing buildings based on 
the number of low voltage outages experienced, the number of 
residents in the building, the extent of residents’ need for elevator 
service (including the proportion of senior and mobility-impaired 
residents), and the height of the buildings. While voltage reductions 
typically affect dozens of elevators in specific areas, it is difficult to 
predict which areas will be affected each year. Nearly 600 buildings 
were affected by low voltage outages during Year 7 of the 
Modernization:  
Modernizing elevators 
refers to replacing key 
equipment and 
electronics and 
refurbishing the elevator 
cabs. 
Low Voltage 
Conditions: Low voltage 
conditions refer to when 
an elevator is not 
receiving enough 
voltage to continue 
operating. Many 
elevators (including 
NYCHA’s) cannot 
operate at lower 
voltages. Elevators need 
a steady supply of 
electricity to run safely. 
When the electrical 
voltage drops too low, 
the elevator’s motor 
does not have enough 
power to move the 
elevator car, and the 
safety systems 
automatically shut the 
elevator down to prevent 
damage or unsafe 
operation. Low voltage 
conditions can occur 
from widespread (e.g., a 
grid goes down) or local 
(e.g., a transformer 
blows) electrical issues. 
Low voltage conditions 
generally require an 
electrician to resolve the 
issue at the source of the 
problem. In responding 
to all outages, NYCHA 
mechanics will take 
voltage readings to 
confirm if the outage is 
due to low voltage 
conditions. 

45 
 
 
45 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
monitorship. The Monitors estimate that it would cost NYCHA 
approximately $45 million to install at least one AVR unit on one 
elevator in each of these buildings. 54 NYCHA represented that it 
would cost well over $100 million to install AVRs for all NYCHA’s 
elevators.  
C. 
No-Service Conditions (Requirement Nos. E7, E8, E15, 
E16, E20, E22) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to reduce how often 
and for how long residents face no-service conditions. The 
subsections below provide an update on NYCHA’s obligations to (1) 
reduce the frequency of no-service conditions in elevator banks with 
multiple elevators; (2) reduce the duration of all no-service 
conditions; and (3) avoid scheduling planned outages that will result 
in a no-service condition during Agreement-restricted hours 
(between 6:00 a.m. and 9:00 a.m. and between 4:00 p.m. and 8:00 
p.m.), and provide at least 24 hours’ advanced notice of planned 
outages to residents. 
1. 
Frequency of No-Service Conditions (Requirement 
Nos. E7, E8) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to reduce the frequency 
of no-service conditions in elevator banks with multiple elevators. In 
NYCHA developments, there are 686 elevator banks with multiple 
elevators.55 
a. 
No More Than One No-Service Condition Per 
Year (Requirement No. E7) 
Beginning in Year 5, the HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to 
ensure that at least 85% of multi-elevator banks have no more than 
one no-service condition per year.56 In Year 7, NYCHA did not comply 
with this obligation because only 45% of its multi-elevator banks 
experienced no more than one no-service condition, a decrease in 
performance by six percentage points from Year 6. 
Voltage Reductions:  
During heat waves, 
demand for electricity 
spikes as the use of 
cooling devices, such as 
fans and air conditioners, 
increases. If demand 
exceeds available 
capacity, equipment 
powering the electrical 
grid can overheat and 
fail, leading to blackouts. 
Reducing the voltage 
provided to the grid 
reduces overall 
electricity use, which 
lowers the risk of 
equipment failure or 
blackout. 

46 
 
46 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, 51% of NYCHA’s multi-elevator banks experienced no more 
than one no-service condition in Year 7, a decrease in performance 
by one percentage point from Year 6. 
b. 
No More Than Three No-Service Conditions 
Per Year (Requirement No. E8)  
Beginning in Year 3, the HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to 
ensure that no multi-elevator banks have more than three no-service 
conditions per year. 57 In Year 7, NYCHA did not comply with this 
obligation because only 73% of its multi-elevator banks experienced 
no more than three no-service conditions, a decrease in performance 
by three percentage points from Year 6. 

47 
 
 
47 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
 
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, 76% of NYCHA’s multi-elevator banks experienced no more 
than three no-service conditions in Year 7, a decrease in performance 
by one percentage point from Year 6. 
2. 
Duration of No-Service Conditions (Requirement 
Nos. E15, E16) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to reduce the duration 
of all no-service conditions, regardless of whether they occur in multi-
elevator banks or single-elevator banks.58 
a. 
Four-Hour No-Service Resolution Obligation 
(Requirement No. E15) 
Beginning in Year 5, the HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to 
resolve 85% of no-service conditions within four hours of learning of 
them.59 In Year 7, NYCHA did not comply with this obligation because 
it resolved only 73% of no-service conditions within four hours, a 
decrease in performance by one percentage point from Year 6.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
  
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, NYCHA resolved 77% of no-service conditions within four 
hours in Year 7, an increase in performance by two percentage points 
from Year 6. 
b. 
Twelve-Hour 
No-Service 
Resolution 
Obligation (Requirement No. E16) 
Beginning in Year 5, the HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to 
resolve all no-service conditions within twelve hours, with limited 
exceptions. 60 In Year 7, NYCHA continued to substantially comply 
with this obligation because it resolved 98% of no-service conditions 
within twelve hours, though this was a decrease in performance by 
one percentage point from Year 6. 

49 
 
 
49 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, NYCHA resolved 99% of no-service conditions within twelve 
hours in Year 7, matching its performance in Year 6. 
3. 
Timing of No-Service Conditions and Providing 
Advance Notice to Residents (Requirement Nos. 
E20, E22) 
To minimize disruptions to residents during specified morning 
and evening hours, the HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to avoid 
scheduling planned outages that will result in no-service conditions 
between 6:00 a.m. and 9:00 a.m. and between 4:00 p.m. and 8:00 
p.m., with limited exceptions.61 Additionally, the Agreement requires 
NYCHA to provide residents with at least 24 hours’ advanced notice 
of planned outages via robocall, postings in the development, and 
postings on NYCHA’s website.62 
NYCHA does not comply with these requirements, as it 
provides notice to residents for some but not all outages. In 
particular, residents have reported that NYCHA frequently shuts 
down elevators with minimal notice and during the HUD Agreement-
restricted hours, most often for preventive maintenance.63 According 
to NYCHA, since 2019, its elevator mechanics have been required to 
post written notice in buildings at least 24 hours before taking an 
elevator offline for preventive maintenance.64 Nevertheless, residents 
have continued to report that such postings are not happening. 

50 
 
50 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
NYCHA has noted that because mechanics must prioritize resolving 
unplanned elevator car outages, preventive maintenance schedules 
are frequently interrupted or modified, making advance postings 
difficult. While the Monitors acknowledge the benefits of preventive 
maintenance 
and 
the 
challenges 
in 
scheduling 
preventive 
maintenance while responding to unplanned outages, the notice 
requirements exist to protect residents from the hardship of 
unannounced outages, and the Monitors continue to discuss with 
NYCHA ways to meet these requirements.  
NYCHA has continued to reduce the number of planned 
outages, including preventive maintenance outages, resulting in no-
service conditions during HUD Agreement-restricted hours. In Year 
7, 1,528 no-service conditions resulted from planned outages, 
including preventive maintenance outages, during Agreement-
restricted hours, which are 474 fewer no-service conditions than in 
Year 6. 
 
NYCHA began piloting a process for scheduling preventive 
maintenance and notifying residents by robocall at least 24 hours in 
advance at South Jamaica Houses on March 17, 2025.65 Seeing early 
success with scheduling and notifying residents of preventive 
maintenance, NYCHA expanded this pilot to nearby Baisley Park 
Houses on November 17, 2025, and International Tower on 
November 24, 2025. The Monitors will share findings from this pilot 
in future reporting.

51 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
D. 
Elevator Car Outages (Requirement Nos. E10, E12, E17-
E19) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to reduce how often 
and for how long elevator cars experience service disruptions. The 
subsections below provide an update on NYCHA’s obligations to (1) 
reduce the frequency of unplanned elevator car outages; and (2) 
reduce the duration of elevator car outages. 
1. 
Frequency of Unplanned Elevator Car Outages 
(Requirement Nos. E10, E12) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to reduce the frequency 
of all unplanned elevator car outages, regardless of whether they 
occur in multi-elevator banks or single-elevator banks.  
a. 
No More Than Eight Unplanned Outages 
(Requirement No. E10) 
Beginning in Year 5, the HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to 
ensure that at least 85% of elevator cars have no more than eight 
unplanned outages per year.66 In Year 7, NYCHA did not comply with 
this obligation because only 67% of its elevator cars did not 
experience more than eight unplanned outages, a decrease in 
performance by three percentage points from Year 6. 
 

52 
 
52 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, 69% of NYCHA’s elevator cars did not experience more than 
eight unplanned outages in Year 7, a decrease in performance by two 
percentage points from Year 6. 
b. 
No More Than Twelve Unplanned Outages 
(Requirement No. E12) 
Beginning in Year 5, the HUD Agreement also requires NYCHA 
to ensure that no elevator car has more than twelve unplanned 
outages per year. 67  In Year 7, NYCHA did not comply with this 
obligation because only 82% of its elevator cars did not experience 
more than twelve unplanned outages, a decrease in performance by 
one percentage point from Year 6. 
 
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, 83% of NYCHA’s elevator cars did not experience more than 
twelve unplanned outages in Year 7, a decrease in performance by 
one percentage point from Year 6. 
2. 
Duration of Elevator Car Outages (Requirement Nos. 
E17-E19) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to reduce the duration 
of elevator car outages as well. 

53 
 
 
53 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
a. 
Percentage Reduction in Outage Duration 
(Requirement No. E17) 
The HUD Agreement has required NYCHA to reduce the 
duration of service outages by 10% in Year 1 of the monitorship and 
to then reduce the average outage duration by a rate set by the 
Monitors.68 Before Year 7, NYCHA and the Monitors agreed to target 
a 10% reduction in average duration of unplanned outages from Year 
6 to Year 7. In Year 7, NYCHA resolved unplanned elevator car 
outages in an average of 6.5 hours, which is 16% higher than its 
average of 5.6 hours during Year 6. 
 
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, NYCHA resolved unplanned elevator car outages in an average 
of 5.7 hours in Year 7, which is 10% higher than its average of 5.2 
hours during Year 6. 
Contributing to the decline in performance were low voltage 
outages, discussed in more detail above. However, even when low 
voltage outages are excluded from the data, NYCHA’s performance 
on this obligation still declined a notable amount. After improving 
every year from Year 3 to Year 6, NYCHA took 16% longer on average 
to resolve unplanned elevator car outages this year than during Year 
6 and, when excluding low voltage outages, it took 10% longer on 
average, falling well short of the agreed-upon goal to reduce average 
duration by 10%.  

54 
 
54 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
NYCHA and the Monitors have been assessing potential root 
causes for the increase in unplanned outage duration in Year 7 to 
guide potential changes in investments or operations to improve 
performance. According to NYCHA, the voltage reductions in 
summer 2025 had cascading effects on operations—the volume of 
simultaneous low voltage outages diverted NYCHA mechanics away 
from responding to other, concurrent outages and performing 
preventive maintenance. The Monitors’ analysis demonstrated a 
measurable impact on NYCHA’s response to outages that occurred 
concurrently with low voltage outages. 69 However, even excluding 
low voltage outages and concurrent outages, NYCHA’s average 
duration of unplanned outages still increased from Year 6 to Year 7. 
This may reflect the downstream effects of deferred preventive 
maintenance, though it is difficult to isolate the impact of deferred 
maintenance from other factors that may have contributed to the 
increase. 
NYCHA also represented that its elevators required more 
significant repairs this year, resulting in more excessively long 
outages. The Monitors found that, excluding low voltage outages, 
there were 61 unplanned elevator car outages over 100 hours in Year 
7, compared to 25 in Year 6, and these additional outages 
contributed notably to the increase in average outage duration.70 As 
NYCHA’s elevator infrastructure continues to age and replacement 
parts become harder to source, excessively long outages are likely to 
become more common.
71  Continued investment in elevator 
replacements and consistent preventive maintenance are vital to 
improving and maintaining the quality of NYCHA’s elevator assets, 
thereby reducing the burden on residents. 
Taken together, the Monitors’ analyses identify low voltage 
outages, their cascading impact on concurrent outages, and 
excessively long repair outages as the likely root causes of the 
increase in average outage duration from Year 6 to Year 7.72 These 
findings point to the need for continued AVR investment and 
accelerated elevator replacements. NYCHA and the Monitors will 
continue to explore other potential contributing factors as well to 
guide further improvement. 
To improve elevator service for residents, NYCHA is also taking 
steps to strengthen its operations. First, in Year 7 NYCHA began a 
four-year elevator mechanic apprenticeship program, and the first 
class from this program completed their first year. Upon completion 

55 
 
 
55 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
of the program, apprentices will be certified as mechanics, increasing 
the staffing pool and ensuring continuity as older mechanics retire. 
Second, as discussed in the Monitors’ April 2025 Report, NYCHA and 
the Monitors collaborated on developing a wrench time dashboard 
to improve the capacity and efficiency of its elevator repair and 
maintenance operations. This tool, which leverages NYCHA’s existing 
data, will help NYCHA optimize the deployment of employees and 
resources. During the fall of 2025, NYCHA began to pilot this 
dashboard with a group of its mechanics, and NYCHA plans to 
expand its use in the upcoming year. In addition to these initiatives, 
NYCHA continues to collaborate with the Monitors on leveraging 
data to increase the capacity and efficiency of its operations. 
b. 
Ten-Hour Outage Obligation (Requirement 
No. E18) 
Beginning in Year 5, the HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to 
resolve 85% of unplanned elevator car outages within ten hours of 
learning of them.73 In Year 7, NYCHA complied with this obligation 
for the second year in a row because it resolved 89% of unplanned 
elevator car outages within ten hours, though this was a decrease in 
performance by two percentage points from Year 6. 
 
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, NYCHA resolved 92% of unplanned elevator car outages within 
ten hours in Year 7, matching its performance in Year 6. 

56 
 
56 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
c. 
Eighteen-Hour 
Outage 
Obligation 
(Requirement No. E19) 
Beginning in Year 5, the HUD Agreement also requires NYCHA 
to resolve all elevator car outages within eighteen hours, with limited 
exceptions. 74  In Year 7, NYCHA substantially complied with this 
obligation because it resolved 98% of all elevator car outages within 
eighteen hours, though this was a decrease in performance by one 
percentage point from Year 6. 
 
Excluding the low voltage outages from this year and previous 
years, NYCHA resolved 99% of all elevator car outages within 
eighteen hours in Year 7, matching its performance in Year 6. 
E. 
Remote Elevator Monitoring (Requirement No. E5) 
The HUD Agreement required NYCHA to establish continuous 
remote monitoring in 70% of its elevators by January 31, 2024. 75 
While it did not meet this deadline, NYCHA reported that it installed 
Remote Elevator Monitoring Systems (“REMS”), technology 
capable of continuous remote monitoring, to 73.7% of its elevators as 
of October 31, 2024.76 As of January 31, 2026, NYCHA reports that it 
has installed this technology in 76.7% of its elevators.77  
During 
their 
ongoing 
verification 
of 
NYCHA’s 
REMS 
installations, the Monitors identified a material connectivity issue with 
NYCHA’s server-based REMS devices. At least 600 of NYCHA’s 
Remote Elevator 
Monitoring Systems:  
REMS provide 
continuous real-time 
monitoring by tracking 
elevator performance 
through sensors that 
detect faults and 
generate fault codes. 
The local Elevator Motor 
Room (“EMR”) receives 
the fault codes in a fault 
log, then transmits the 
codes to either physical 
servers at the central 
office of NYCHA’s 
Elevator Service and 
Repair Department 
(ESRD) for older REMS 
devices (“server-based”), 
or to a cloud-based 
monitoring system for 
newer REMS devices 
(“cloud-based”), where 
the data can be accessed 
and used to coordinate 
responses, generate 
work orders, and 
monitor elevator 
performance offsite. 

57 
 
 
57 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
approximately 2,200 REMS devices are not transmitting information 
from the local EMRs to ESRD’s central office as expected. Although 
mechanics are able to access and review fault logs generated by 
REMS devices in the local elevator motor rooms, this transmission gap 
limits ESRD’s ability to monitor the impacted elevators offsite. For 
example, connected REMS devices immediately generate alerts for 
high-priority fault codes, prompting ESRD dispatchers to generate 
outage work orders, while those without connectivity cannot. 78 
NYCHA is actively investigating this issue and is considering 
upgrades to avoid similar problems in the future. The Monitors will 
continue to evaluate whether this connectivity issue affects NYCHA’s 
compliance with this obligation as NYCHA’s remediation efforts 
progress.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
I. 3 
Pests & Waste 
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA must (1) estimate and 
report on the size of the pest population at each development and 
reduce those pest populations by specific percentages over time;79 
(2) respond to residents’ pest complaints within prescribed 
deadlines; 80  (3) apply timely and effective pest management 
techniques informed by Integrated Pest Management (“IPM”) 
practices; 81 (4) increase cleanliness at developments by improving 
waste management practices;82 and (5) upgrade specified pest and 
waste management infrastructure.83 The section below provides an 
update on NYCHA’s progress with respect to pest- and waste-related 
requirements under the Agreement as well as pest- and waste-related 
capital projects for Year 7. 
A. 
Pest Population Reporting and Reduction Requirements 
(Requirement Nos. P7–P11)  
Pursuant to the HUD Agreement, the Monitors, in consultation 
with NYCHA, established reasonable protocols (the “Protocols”) for 
NYCHA to estimate the pest populations at each development on a 
quarterly basis. 84 Using the Protocols, NYCHA must publish those 
estimates on its website so stakeholders, including residents, can see 
whether NYCHA is reducing pest populations.85 The Agreement also 
requires NYCHA to meet certain pest population reduction targets.86  
The following subsections provide an update on (1) NYCHA’s 
publishing of the pest population estimates on its website; (2) 
NYCHA-wide pest population estimates for Year 7; and (3) NYCHA’s 
progress in meeting the HUD Agreement’s pest reduction target 
requirements.87  
1. 
Publish Pest Population Estimates  
As discussed in the April 2025 Report, the Monitors, in 
consultation with NYCHA, developed Protocols to establish 
reasonable estimates of the presence of pests.88 These estimates do 
not count the actual number of pests, as that is not possible. Instead, 
the estimates are approximations of the pest presence at NYCHA’s 
developments based on residents’ pest complaints, and as adjusted 
for over- and underreporting of pests.  
Pests: Under the HUD 
Agreement, the term 
“pests” refers to rats, 
mice, cockroaches, and 
bed bugs. 
Integrated Pest 
Management (“IPM”): 
An environmentally 
friendly, commonsense 
approach to pest control. 
Unlike traditional pest 
management, which 
involves the routine 
application of pesticide, 
IPM focuses on the 
prevention of pests and 
uses pesticide only as 
needed. It involves 
multiple forms of pest 
controls and has a four-
tiered approach: (1) 
identify pests and 
monitor progress, (2) set 
action thresholds, (3) 
prevent pests, and (4) 
control pests. 

66 
 
66 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
Under the HUD Agreement, once the Monitors establish the 
Protocols, NYCHA is required to use the Protocols to develop and 
publish reliable pest population estimates for each development at 
least quarterly. 89 NYCHA continues to publish the pest population 
estimates 
for 
each 
development 
on 
its 
website 
at 
https://www.nyc.gov/site/nycha/residents/pest-population-
estimates.page. 
NYCHA is working to establish a process where it will be able 
to calculate the pest population estimates on its own. The Monitors 
currently supply NYCHA with the estimates for publication on the 
agency’s website and will continue to do so until NYCHA has 
established its own process. NYCHA has advised that the difficulty of 
finding NYCHA staff with experience coding in the required 
programming language prevented the agency from establishing the 
calculation process in Year 7. NYCHA currently projects that it will 
establish this process by the end of the third quarter of Year 8. 
2. 
Estimate Pest Populations   
The charts below illustrate the NYCHA-wide pest population 
estimates for each year beginning with the year before the 
monitorship began (which is denoted as “Year 0”). As set forth below, 
in Year 7, NYCHA’s developments experienced increased estimated 
populations of rats, mice, and bed bugs, with the estimated 
population for cockroaches remaining roughly flat, as compared to 
the prior year. As discussed in prior reporting, the pest population 
estimates are calculated using several factors, and the Monitors note 
that the Year 7 increases in the estimates were not solely the result of 
increases in pest complaints made by residents. Other contributing 
factors were that residents more accurately identified pests in their 
complaints, and that better-trained NYCHA pest inspectors were able 
to detect more unreported pests than in previous years. In other 
words, it is distinctly possible that it was better pest detection, as 
opposed to a greater pest presence, that materially drove the 
increases in Year 7. This is a key component for IPM, which requires 
accurate data to inform effective pest control management. 
Rats. The pest population estimate for rats was 440 for Year 7, 
up 19% from 370 in Year 6. For both years, the estimated rat 
populations were equal to the number of verified rat complaints by 
residents. This is because when a rat is present in an apartment, 
residents report it, so there is no need to adjust the number. In other 

67 
 
 
67 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
words, the pest population estimate of 440 means that exterminators 
responded to 440 complaints where they determined that there was 
a rat in the apartment.  
 
Mice. The pest population estimate for mice was about 14,300 
for Year 7, up about 39% from Year 6.  
 
Cockroaches. The pest population estimate for cockroaches 
was nearly 51,800 for Year 7,90 up about 4% from Year 6.91   

68 
 
68 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
Bed Bugs. The pest population estimate for bed bugs was 
about 1,900 for Year 7, up about 21% from Year 6.  
 
To the extent that higher estimated pest populations reflect 
underlying increases in the actual pest populations (as opposed to 
NYCHA improving its ability to detect the presence of unreported 
pests during its undercount inspections), the Monitors’ consulting 
urban entomologists have observed that there are many factors that 
contribute to such increases, but it is not possible to accurately 

69 
 
 
69 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
determine the extent to which any particular factor led to the increase. 
Some of those factors are within NYCHA’s control. For example, when 
garbage remains on NYCHA grounds without being containerized, it 
serves as a food source for pests. See Subsection E on NYCHA’s 
efforts to containerize trash below. Likewise, some developments 
have outer walls that do not extend deeply into the ground, meaning 
rats can burrow into NYCHA crawlspaces that have not been 
appropriately sealed off. NYCHA’s Pest Management Department 
(“PMD”) has begun to survey the crawlspaces and basements at 
NYCHA so they can begin work to address those issues. Where there 
are gaps or holes in NYCHA walls, internal or external, NYCHA is 
working to fill them to prevent pests from entering.  
Other factors that increase the pest populations are outside of 
NYCHA’s control. For example, as the Monitors’ consulting urban 
entomologists have previously noted, NYCHA’s rat population is 
influenced by rat populations and conditions outside of NYCHA’s 
developments.
92  For instance, rats can travel into NYCHA 
developments from burrows outside of NYCHA’s properties, which 
limits NYCHA’s ability to address the root causes of the rat population 
increase.  
3. 
Reduce Pest Populations 
The HUD Agreement required NYCHA to reduce pest 
populations by certain percentages by the end of Year 393 and by the 
end of Year 5.94 As discussed in the April 2025 Report, NYCHA met 
the pest reduction targets for mice, cockroaches, and bed bugs in 
Year 6.95  
The reduction requirements are calculated by comparing the 
estimated pest population of the past year with the estimated pest 
population at the beginning of the monitorship. Despite the recent 
increase in pest population estimates this year, NYCHA continued to 
meet some of the pest reduction targets for cockroaches, mice, and 
bed bugs because those pest population estimates are substantially 
lower compared to the pest population estimates for when the 
monitorship started. 
Rats. For rats, by the end of Year 3, NYCHA was required to 
reduce the rat population by 50%, and by the end of Year 5, by an 
additional 50%.
96  This means that NYCHA was required to 
cumulatively reduce the rat population by 75% by the end of Year 5. 

70 
 
70 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
NYCHA did not meet the reduction requirements for rats in prior 
years.97 As depicted in the chart below, NYCHA began substantially 
improving during Year 6. 
 
By the end of Year 7, the percent reduction for rats was 31%, a 
decrease in performance by 11 percentage points from the prior 
year, meaning the reported rat population was higher than last year. 
As illustrated above, NYCHA did not satisfy the rat reduction target of 
75% (or the lower Year 3 target of 50%). 
Mice. The percent reduction for mice was 58%, a decrease in 
performance by 12 percentage points from last year, meaning the 
reported mouse population was higher than the end of Year 6. 

71 
 
 
71 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
NYCHA is still in partial compliance with the mouse reduction 
targets. NYCHA continues to meet the first reduction target of 40% 
but fell out of compliance with the second reduction requirement of 
70%.  
Cockroaches. The percent reduction for cockroaches was 69% 
as of the end of Year 7, which is one percentage point lower as 
compared to Year 6. As illustrated in the graph below, NYCHA 
continues to meet the Year 3 reduction requirement of 40% and fell 
out of compliance with the Year 5 reduction obligation of 70%. 
 

72 
 
72 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
Bed Bugs. The percent reduction for bed bugs was 80%, down 
three percentage points from last year, but NYCHA remains in 
compliance with both reduction obligations. 
 
To address the increasing pest populations at NYCHA, PMD 
plans to categorize developments by level of pest activity and 
prioritize performing pest treatments at developments with higher 
levels of pest activity.98 This will include using pest control methods 
that address building-wide pest issues, in addition to treating pests 
on a complaint-by-complaint basis.99 As well, to the extent resources 
allow, PMD plans to increase its preventive work orders, to reduce 
pest populations proactively. The Monitors’ consulting entomologists 
have advised that the industry standard would be to complete four 
preventive work orders for every complaint NYCHA receives. PMD is 
working toward that industry standard and aims to fill 20 vacant 
exterminator positions to meet it. Some of those vacancies may be 
filled by graduates of the exterminator training course for residents 
taught 
by 
NYCHA’s 
Resident 
Economic 
Empowerment 
& 
Sustainability program. Filling the vacancies would help PMD meet 
the industry standard ratio for exterior and common areas, in addition 
to residents’ apartments. PMD and the Monitors’ consulting urban 
entomologists also believe that NYCHA should hire a bricklayer and 
plasterer to seal holes in basements and apartments following pest 
treatment, especially for rats. NYCHA has estimated that PMD would 
need a budget increase of $400,000 to staff those positions.

B. 
Respond to Resident Pest Complaints (Requirement Nos. 
P14–P15) 
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA must respond to resident 
pest complaints in apartments within certain timeframes depending 
on the pest.100 The subsections below provide an update on NYCHA’s 
progress meeting these requirements.  
1. 
Respond to Rat Complaints (Requirement No. P14)  
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to respond to 90% of all 
rat complaints within two business days, and to 100% of rat 
complaints within five calendar days. NYCHA has continued to satisfy 
these obligations.101  
 NYCHA responded to 98% of all rat complaints within two 
business days in Year 7, one percentage point higher than last year.  
 
NYCHA responded to 99% of resident rat complaints within 
five calendar days during Year 7, the same as compared to last year. 
The Monitors commend NYCHA for continuing to timely respond to 
rat complaints in Year 7.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
2. 
Respond to Other Pest Complaints (Requirement 
No. P15)  
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to respond to 90% of 
other pest complaints within seven calendar days and 100% of other 
pest complaints within ten calendar days.102  
NYCHA responded to 87% of other pest complaints within 
seven calendar days during Year 7, 22 percentage points higher than 
last year. The Monitors applaud this significant improvement, putting 
NYCHA close to satisfying this HUD Agreement requirement. The 
Monitors note that although the obligation to respond to 75% of 
other pest complaints within seven days has been superseded by the 
90% requirement as illustrated below, NYCHA surpassed the 75% 
requirement for the first time in Year 7, an important milestone.  
Other Pests: Under the 
HUD Agreement, the 
term “other pests” refers 
to mice, cockroaches, 
and bed bugs.

within ten calendar days in Year 7, 11 percentage points higher than 
last year. The Monitors also commend this meaningful improvement,
