SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
C. 
Address Pest Complaints from Residents with Health 
Conditions in an Expedited Manner (Requirement No. P17) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to respond to pest 
complaints in apartments occupied by residents whose health 
conditions may be caused or exacerbated by exposure to pest 
infestations (“Pest Sensitive Units”) and to apply pest control methods 
in Pest Sensitive Units in an expedited manner.103 
The First Monitor and NYCHA agreed that to satisfy this 
requirement, NYCHA would address pest complaints in Pest Sensitive 
Units more quickly than in other apartments.  
NYCHA responded to pest complaints in Pest Sensitive Units 
more quickly than to other apartments in Year 7, continuing to meet 
the HUD Agreement requirement. NYCHA also significantly 
improved its response time compared to last year.  
 
Similarly, NYCHA applied pest control methods more quickly 
in Pest Sensitive Units than in other apartments in Year 7, meeting the 
HUD Agreement requirement, as it has done beginning in Year 3.  
Apply: Under the 
definition agreed to by 
the First Monitor and 
NYCHA, this is the time 
from the moment a 
complaint is verified, to 
the moment that NYCHA 
staff completes the initial 
work order for inspection 
and initial treatment. 
Typically, the same 
NYCHA staff member 
who responds to the 
initial work order applies 
the initial pest control 
treatment during the 
same visit. Application of 
pest control methods 
should take, on average, 
45 minutes.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
NYCHA has consistently addressed pest complaints from residents 
with health conditions in an expedited manner. Additionally, NYCHA 
has significantly improved response times to all units starting in Year 
5. 
D. 
Implement 
Integrated 
Pest 
Management 
Practices 
(Requirement Nos. P16, P18–P19)  
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to consistently and 
comprehensively implement IPM practices. 104 Specifically, NYCHA 
must (1) apply effective pest control in a timely manner; (2) 
incorporate industry-standard IPM into building operations in all 
NYCHA developments; and (3) send staff appropriately trained in IPM 
to respond to all pest complaints.105   
As discussed in prior reporting, NYCHA continues to train its 
staff and implement IPM techniques, leading to improved 
compliance and services for residents.106 But more work remains. To 
fully comply with the HUD Agreement’s IPM requirements, NYCHA 
must ensure that all staff consistently follow the Pest Standard 
Procedure (“Pest SP”) and effectively apply IPM techniques. The 
subsections below provide an update on NYCHA’s progress on the 
IPM requirements and effectively apply IPM techniques. The 
subsections below provide an update on NYCHA’s progress on the 
IPM requirements. 
Pest Standard 
Procedure (“Pest SP”): 
The Pest SP provides 
instructions to NYCHA 
staff on how to 
implement and comply 
with preventative and 
responsive measures to 
control pest infestations. 
It also creates protocols 
to protect the health of 
residents, employees, 
and vendors when 
removing and reducing 
pest activity using IPM 
techniques. 

78 
 
78 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
1. 
Apply Effective Pest Control Methods in a Timely 
Manner (Requirement No. P16) 
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA must apply effective pest 
control methods within seven calendar days of verifying the presence 
of a pest.107 NYCHA timely applied pest control methods in Year 7, 
maintaining the same performance compared to last year. This means 
NYCHA applied pest control methods within seven days of an 
exterminator visiting the apartment to respond to a resident’s 
complaint. However, as discussed further below, NYCHA does not yet 
apply effective pest control methods during every visit to a resident’s 
apartment. Although NYCHA is improving, it must still work to further 
improve the quality of its application of pest control methods in order 
to meet this requirement.  
 
2. 
Implement and Apply Integrated Pest Management 
Techniques  
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to incorporate IPM 
practices into building operations at all developments and to send 
staff appropriately trained on IPM techniques to respond to all pest 
complaints.108  
The Monitors’ consulting urban entomologists have observed 
that PMD has substantially improved since the start of the 
monitorship, increasing its management and organizational skills, 

79 
 
 
79 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
critical for proper IPM. For example, PMD is making important strides 
toward coordinating their pest management activities to address pest 
populations comprehensively, instead of on a complaint-by-
complaint basis. This includes evaluating buildings with high pest 
populations to determine where pests enter the building (through 
sewers or crawl spaces, e.g.) and working to block those access 
points. Accomplishing these improvements requires PMD to create a 
series of work orders at a single development that addresses the 
specific pest issues at the development, then follow up to ensure each 
work order is completed. 
Throughout Year 7, NYCHA made meaningful progress 
toward meeting its IPM-compliant pest management training 
requirements, though significant work remains. PMD invested 
substantially in staff expertise and training infrastructure. PMD’s in-
house entomologists—including a second entomologist hired mid-
year—play a central role in developing and updating staff trainings 
and analyzing pest-related data to identify gaps in the application of 
pest control methods. PMD also conducted a program evaluation of 
its December 2024 Snap Trap training, which directly informed 
improvements to subsequent trainings. PMD’s recent bed bug 
training presentation met industry standards and was accompanied 
by meaningful hands-on instruction, including demonstrations and 
microscope work, reflecting a growing commitment to substantive, 
skills-based training. The Monitors’ consulting entomologists have 
found PMD’s training program and materials to be IPM-compliant, 
including the general Pest SP training and trainings for specific pests, 
and believe these ongoing efforts will lead to more widespread and 
consistent compliance with the Pest SP and IPM techniques. As a 
result, PMD’s exterminators have improved and expanded their pest 
control techniques, such as exclusion methods, which involve sealing 
gaps and holes that pests can use to enter buildings or residents’ 
apartments. This helps prevent pests from accessing food and 
shelter. 
NYCHA has also expanded its accountability and quality 
assurance efforts. EH&S is working to increase the scope of its quality 
assurance reviews of PMD exterminators’ work, providing an 
additional layer of oversight to ensure that field practices align with 
IPM standards. The Monitors commend PMD’s openness to outside 
evaluation of its exterminators and methods, as well as its 
Snap Trap: A 
mechanical trap for 
rodents with a spring-
activated mechanism. 

80 
 
80 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
demonstrated commitment to implementing changes in response to 
that feedback. 
Despite this progress, the quality of pest management services 
remains uneven across developments, and not all exterminators 
consistently apply IPM-based practices effectively in the field. This is 
due in part to inconsistent supervision of exterminators in the field 
and to the challenge of changing the habits of exterminators with 
decades of experience in non-IPM pest management. A persistent 
and significant obstacle is exterminators’ difficulty communicating 
with residents about the need for apartment access—an issue the 
Monitors’ consulting urban entomologists have continued to flag 
throughout Year 7. When exterminators are unable to explain the 
purpose of inspections, address resident concerns, or overcome 
objections to entry, access rates suffer and IPM methods cannot be 
comprehensively applied across all units in a building. Additionally, 
when exterminators do gain entry, they sometimes focus only on the 
rooms and pests that residents included in their complaint, instead of 
inspecting the entire apartment for all pest types.  
To address these challenges, NYCHA has committed to a suite 
of 
targeted 
initiatives. 
To 
improve 
exterminators’ 
resident 
communication skills and increase access rates, PMD is providing 
management 
and 
leadership 
training 
for 
Supervisors 
of 
Exterminators (“SOEs”) to help them more effectively manage, coach, 
and hold staff accountable. SOEs are also working through a roster of 
digital leadership courses developed collaboratively with NYCHA’s 
Learning and Development Department. In addition, PMD has 
launched a mentorship program pairing experienced exterminators 
with newer colleagues to provide individualized coaching and hands-
on guidance. To ensure exterminators inspect all rooms in 
apartments for all pest types, PMD is emphasizing full-apartment 
inspections in its Year 8 trainings and updating its inspection forms to 
require documentation of the pests found in each room. Finally, to 
encourage overall consistency across exterminators, PMD hosts 
borough-wide meetings twice a year and offers bonuses to 
exterminators who meet certain quality benchmarks. The Monitors 
believe these initiatives, taken together, have the potential to 
meaningfully improve exterminator performance in the field—but 
their ultimate effectiveness will depend on consistent implementation 
and follow-through. 

81 
 
 
81 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
The Monitors will continue to provide subject matter and 
technical expertise to NYCHA and support NYCHA’s efforts to deliver 
pest management services in accordance with the Pest SP and IPM-
based practices. 
3. 
Address Common Area Pest Complaints  
IPM practices require NYCHA to eliminate pests’ access to 
food, water, and shelter across developments, including common 
areas such as lobbies and stair halls. As discussed in prior reporting, 
in Year 6, NYCHA, HUD, and SDNY agreed that NYCHA must respond 
to common area pest complaints in a timely manner,109 and in Year 7, 
those specific timeframes were established. Specifically, NYCHA 
must (1) respond to 90% of rat complaints in internal common areas 
within two business days, and to 100% of complaints within five 
calendar days; (2) respond to 100% of rat complaints in external 
common areas within ten calendar days; and (3) respond to 100% of 
other pest complaints in internal and external common areas within 
ten business days.110 For rat complaints in high-traffic areas, NYCHA 
must respond in an expedited manner.111   
NYCHA responded to 80% of rat complaints in internal 
common areas within two business days in Year 7. For context, the 
graphic below also sets forth NYCHA’s performance historically, 
using work orders from prior years, recognizing that the parameters 
of this obligation were first established during the second quarter of 
Year 7 (as is the case with respect to all of the other response times 
discussed in this subsection).  

82 
 
82 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
NYCHA responded to 89% of rat complaints in internal 
common areas within five calendar days in Year 7.  
 
NYCHA responded to 98% of rat complaints in external 
common areas within ten calendar days in Year 7. 

83 
 
 
83 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
NYCHA responded to 98% of other pest complaints in internal 
and external common areas within ten business days in Year 7. 
 
In Year 7, NYCHA and the Monitors agreed to the definition of 
the terms “high-traffic areas” and “expedited manner.” “High-traffic 
areas” include playgrounds and parking lots. “Expedited manner” 
means that NYCHA will respond to 90% of high-traffic area rat 
complaints within two business days and to 100% of high-traffic area 
rat complaints within five calendar days.

area rat complaints, and NYCHA responded to one of them (20%) 
within two business days. To improve their response time, NYCHA 
has begun assigning exterior high-traffic area rat complaints to the 
specialized rat team. 
 
During Year 7, NYCHA responded to 60% of high-traffic area 
rat complaints within five calendar days.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
E. 
Improve Waste Management (Requirement No. P11)  
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to inspect grounds and 
common areas at every development for cleaning and maintenance 
needs and to correct such conditions at least once a day (“Waste 
Management Requirement”). 112 In particular, NYCHA must ensure 
that trash in grounds or common areas of each NYCHA development 
is collected and either removed from the premises or stored in a 
manner that prevents access by pests at least once every 24 hours.113  
To facilitate compliance with this requirement, NYCHA 
committed to several capital projects aimed at improving its waste 
management capabilities. 114  These projects include building new 
waste yards and trash collection systems and replacing and 
upgrading the agency’s waste collection and hauling equipment.115  
The subsections below provide an update on (1) data from 
NYCHA’s waste inspections; (2) NYCHA’s compliance with the Waste 
Management Requirement, as verified by the Monitors’ inspection 
team; and (3) the agency’s pest and waste management capital 
projects.   
1. 
Data from NYCHA’s Waste Inspection 
NYCHA continues to measure compliance with the Waste 
Management Requirement through its Clean Compounds initiative, 
which requires consolidations to submit daily photographs of certain 
areas on development grounds and for grounds supervisors to 
submit attestations to NYCHA’s Waste Management Department 
(“WMD”). To validate the consolidations’ self-reported compliance, 
WMD launched Scorecard 1.5, a reporting system for Waste 
Management inspectors to perform inspections of consolidations’ 
common areas and grounds. In connection with Scorecard 1.5, WMD 
launched a new reporting initiative to share the results of the 
inspections with development caretakers. WMD now immediately 
shares a list of observed deficiencies that require corrective action 
with the respective development and instructs the consolidation to 
resolve the issues. WMD then provides the consolidation a score, 
together with information regarding the basis for the score, based on 
the inspectors’ assessment of interior and exterior locations. 
WMD has shared the results of its Scorecard 1.5 inspections 
and Clean Compounds daily photo verifications with the Monitors, 
Consolidation: A group 
of developments 
managed by the same 
property management 
office.  

86 
 
86 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
and the Monitors will begin reporting on developments’ scores at a 
high level to illustrate NYCHA’s progress in meeting the Waste 
Management Requirement. The results of Scorecard 1.5 and the 
Clean Compounds verifications are not the only components of the 
Waste Management Requirement. High scores alone do not establish 
NYCHA is in full compliance with the Waste Management 
Requirement. Instead, they show NYCHA’s efforts in working toward 
full compliance, and the Monitors hope to see the scores increase 
over time.  
For Scorecard 1.5, developments receive an overall grade 
from 1.0 to 5.0, with a 5.0 being the highest score a development can 
receive. Inspectors evaluate whether there is garbage in the common 
areas and grounds, including on the sidewalks, greenspaces, parking 
lots, elevators, or interior hallways and stairwells. Inspectors also 
check whether the recycling and compactor areas are well-
maintained, including whether they are clean or have damaged 
doors, walls, or ceilings. Each area of a development receives an 
individual score, and those scores are averaged and then adjusted to 
create the development’s total score from 1.0 to 5.0. 
In Year 7, as depicted in the chart below, the average 
Scorecard 1.5 score for the developments was around 3.6. The 
developments’ average scores increased slightly over the course of 
Year 7, peaking at an average of 3.7 in the fourth quarter.

87 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT

a score between 3.0 and 4.0 when inspected in Year 7. A number of 
developments received scores below 3.0, demonstrating that some 
developments have common areas and grounds that are poorly 
maintained. WMD’s inspectors visit low-scoring developments more 
often, meaning that those developments receive more scores than 
better-maintained developments. The chart below represents the 
scores given to 197 separate developments over the course of 1,745 
inspections. Out of the 219 developments that NYCHA operates, 22 
do not receive Scorecard inspections because they are too small to 
justify the review or because they are set up as single-family homes.116

2. 
Inspect and Correct Negative Waste Conditions 
To further assess compliance with the Waste Management 
Requirement and to supplement NYCHA’s inspections, the Monitors’ 

89 
 
 
89 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
independent inspection team conducts evaluations of NYCHA 
properties, covering grounds, including curbside collection points, 
short-term storage areas, waste yards, walkways, and common areas, 
including lobbies, elevators, and stairwells. During Year 7, the 
Monitors’ team conducted 381 grounds inspections across 188 
developments 
and 
2,062 
building 
inspections 
across 
200 
developments. The team has shared the results of the inspections 
with NYCHA property management to help improve compliance at 
the development level. The Monitors also share the results of the 
inspections with WMD’s leadership to help improve compliance 
agency-wide. 
Based on these inspections, the Monitors conclude that 
NYCHA is in partial compliance with the Waste Management 
Requirement. The Monitors’ inspectors found that over half of the 
inspected 
developments 
complied 
with 
the 
requirement, 
demonstrating good waste management practices, with properly 
managed lobby areas and well-maintained outside entranceways that 
were clean and free of obstructions. On the other hand, the Monitors’ 
inspectors also observed some developments with issues such as 
trash on the grounds that was not picked up as well as bagged trash 
and debris near containers that had already been emptied. 
Additionally, the Monitors’ inspectors noted accumulated trash 
behind construction fences. In some developments, stairwells had 
stained walls and visible trash or substances on the floor. Finally, the 
Monitors’ inspectors noted that one development received a passing 
Scorecard 1.5 inspection despite hypodermic needles from 
intravenous drug users being present in the development’s hallway. 
Although the needles could have been left in the hallway after the 
development staff performed their cleaning duties for that day, the 
Monitors note that discarded needles pose a safety risk to residents, 
which should be reflected in the Scorecard 1.5 scoring. These 
deficiencies were all shared with NYCHA through the Monitors’ 
inspection reports, and the Monitors are working with NYCHA to 
improve their inspections standards. 
During the Monitors’ inspections, the Monitors’ team identified 
an issue related to NYCHA’s use of two-yard trash and recyclables 
bins—so named because they hold two cubic yards of material. In 
certain developments, trash and recyclables are temporarily held in 
two-yard bins that are placed outside of buildings, and which 
development staff must then move to the development’s waste yard 

90 
 
90 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
or pick-up point. The equipment to move the bins can either be front-
loading, which means that the bin is carried in front of the driver, or 
rear-loading, where the bin is held behind the driver. Since 2024, 
NYCHA has recognized that using front-loading equipment presents 
a safety risk because the driver’s view is obstructed by the bin. To 
address this risk, NYCHA requires that when its staff use a front-
loader, there is a second person who acts as a spotter and is 
responsible for identifying any obstructions and communicating with 
the driver.   
However, in one instance, the Monitors’ inspection team 
recently observed development staff not following those safety 
guidelines when using front-loading equipment. While performing a 
waste inspection at Isaacs Houses, the Monitors’ inspectors noticed 
development staff using front-loading equipment to service a two-
yard container without the accompanying spotter. 
 
As this did not follow the procedure and safety guidelines set 
out by NYCHA, the Monitors informed WMD and development staff 
of the issue. The property manager for Isaacs Houses reinforced the 
requirement to have a spotter when using front-loading equipment 
to service two-yard containers and confirmed that disciplinary 
measures will be taken if the spotter requirement is not followed. 
Because some developments lack the necessary rear-loading 
equipment or do not have sufficient staff to operate front-loading 

91 
 
 
91 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
equipment with a spotter, several developments have stopped using 
two-yard containers for the time being. At six developments,117 the 
two-yard containers were housed inside separate structures called 
bin enclosures, as seen in the photo below, which were part of a pilot 
program launched in August 2025 to mitigate the impact of those 
two-yard containers that are placed near a building. NYCHA had 
observed that the uncovered two-yard waste bins presented an 
eyesore. 
 
Because the bin enclosures cannot be emptied safely when a 
development does not have rear-loading equipment or enough staff 
to use a spotter with front-loading equipment, several developments 
closed off the bin enclosures. For example, the bin enclosures at 
Marcy Houses have been effectively closed since August 8, 2025. 
However, some residents continued to place trash beside them, as 
noted by the Monitors’ team during a site visit.  

92 
 
92 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
As a stopgap, NYCHA placed additional 50-gallon receptacles 
throughout the development, which are being serviced multiple 
times daily, though resident compliance with redirection instructions 
has been gradual. 
 
What these inspection visits have made clear is that waste 
management compliance is not solely a function of NYCHA’s WMD. 
While WMD sets protocols and oversees the pilot program, the day-
to-day operation and maintenance of waste containerization falls 
primarily on development maintenance staff, not WMD personnel. 
Ensuring safe and effective waste management therefore requires 
coordination across multiple parts of the organization, including 
Asset & Capital Management, Operations, Compliance, and EH&S, as 

93 
 
 
93 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
well as consistent training and reinforcement at the development 
level.  
NYCHA has made meaningful improvements in response to 
the concerns raised—including retraining staff, reinforcing spotter 
protocols system-wide, and working to finalize a comprehensive 
safety protocol for bin enclosure servicing—but significant work 
remains. The Monitors cannot yet find substantial compliance with 
respect to these issues. The Monitors will continue to closely track 
how WMD advances the pilot program, addresses the outstanding 
equipment compatibility issues, and ensures that development-level 
maintenance staff are adequately trained. 
To facilitate compliance with the Waste Management 
Requirement, the Monitors will continue to perform independent 
grounds and common area inspections and will share the results with 
NYCHA. 
3. 
Implement Waste Management Capital Projects 
Among other measures to reduce pest populations and 
improve sanitation in its developments, the HUD Agreement required 
NYCHA to install 50 rat slabs by December 31, 2020.118 As of January 
31, 2026, NYCHA has installed all 50 rat slabs, satisfying this one-time 
requirement. The Monitors commend NYCHA’s commitment to 
accomplishing this long-standing goal, albeit approximately five 
years behind the Agreement’s deadline. 
 The government has approved an amendment to the City 
Capital Action Plan (“CCAP”) that reallocates $50 million from waste 
management capital projects to heating and plumbing initiatives. The 
amendment reduces NYCHA’s CCAP commitments for new waste 
yards from 120 to 111 at 83 developments and interior compactors, 
down from 1,211 to 1,197 at 92 developments, but accelerates the 
timeline for delivery of these projects. NYCHA expects that the 
accelerated waste management project pipeline, now targeted for 
completion by the end of 2028 rather than 2030, will offset any 
resulting operational costs, and NYCHA will work with the Monitors 
to evaluate the condition of aging waste equipment and develop 
strategies to ensure continued operation at developments where 
projects have been deferred. 
 
 
City Capital Action Plan 
(“CCAP”): Under the 
HUD Agreement, New 
York City must provide 
$2.2 billion in capital 
funding to NYCHA over a 
ten-year period starting 
in 2019 ($250 million per 
year for the final six 
years). These funds are 
committed to capital 
projects and can only be 
spent pursuant to an 
Action Plan. The CCAP 
was approved by the 
First Monitor on May 8, 
2021, and was amended 
in 2026.

thin layers of concrete 
poured over areas of 
exposed sand and 
aggregate within 
basements and crawl 
spaces where rats 
burrow. They are 
intended to prevent 
rodents from 
establishing burrows 
inside buildings.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
I.4 
Mold & Leaks 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to timely remediate 
mold and leaks, prevent mold growth and recurrence, and stop 
NYCHA employees from improperly closing mold-related work 
orders. 119 The Agreement also requires NYCHA to comply with a 
separate set of obligations established by court orders in a federal 
class action lawsuit, Baez v. NYCHA, No. 13 Civ. 8915 (“Baez”), 
brought by NYCHA residents to address mold and leaks in NYCHA 
developments.120 
The subsections below provide an update on NYCHA’s 
compliance with the outstanding mold and leak requirements under 
the HUD Agreement in Year 7,121 as well as information about mold-
related capital projects. 
A. 
Mold Work Orders 
 NYCHA’s Mold Standard Procedure (“Mold SP”) prescribes 
the following steps for responding to mold conditions. The HUD 
Agreement establishes deadlines by which NYCHA must complete 
each applicable step, which are set forth in the following subsection.  
Mold Inspection. A mold inspection occurs in response to a 
resident’s complaint regarding mold in their apartment or when 
NYCHA staff identify mold during an unrelated visit to the apartment, 
such as during the mandated annual inspection. When NYCHA is 
alerted to a mold condition via resident complaint or by a staff 
member who is not qualified to perform a mold inspection, NYCHA 
sends a certified mold inspector to the resident’s apartment to verify 
that mold is present and to determine what work needs to be done 
to address the mold (the “mold inspection”). If the mold is identified 
by a NYCHA staff member who is qualified to perform a mold 
inspection, that staff member will perform the inspection. After the 
mold inspection, NYCHA sends the resident a work plan—a 
document explaining how the agency will address verified mold 
complaints and their root cause(s). The work plan has a “parent” work 
order, which contains multiple “child” work orders for each step of the 
remediation process—such as cleaning mold, fixing the root cause of 
the mold growth, and completing cosmetic repairs to restore the 
apartment to habitable condition. The parent work order is only 
closed after all related child work orders have been addressed. 
Baez v. NYCHA, No. 13 
Civ. 8915 (“Baez”): 
Baez is a federal class 
action lawsuit brought by 
NYCHA residents 
suffering from asthma 
and living in apartments 
with mold and excessive 
moisture. NYCHA settled 
Baez in 2014. The 
resulting consent 
decrees impose various 
requirements on NYCHA 
that are similar but not 
identical to the HUD 
Agreement obligations 
and aim to help NYCHA 
effectively remediate 
mold and moisture in a 
timely manner. 
Mold Inspector: A mold 
inspector is a Property 
Maintenance Supervisor, 
Assistant Property 
Maintenance Supervisor, 
Property Manager, or 
Maintenance Worker 
who is trained and 
authorized to perform 
initial mold inspections. 
A mold inspector must 
complete the Mold 
Inspector and Building 
Sciences Trainings 
before performing such 
inspections.  

101 
 
 
101 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
Mold Removal. The first step to addressing a mold complaint 
is to remove the mold in the resident’s apartment. Mold removal can 
be accomplished through mold cleaning or by removing fixtures 
with mold growth. To clean mold, NYCHA staff or vendors apply a 
mold-specific cleaning detergent and disinfectant and manually clear 
the mold with a brush.122 If the mold cannot be removed via cleaning, 
NYCHA staff will remove sheetrock, grouting, or fixtures like cabinets 
that contain mold.  
Mold Remediation. Once mold is removed, either through 
cleaning or fixture removal, NYCHA must remediate the mold 
condition. Remediation includes repairs that address the root cause 
of mold and restore the apartment to habitable condition. Each 
parent work order is categorized as needing either a simple or 
complex repair. Simple repairs can be completed by NYCHA 
property maintenance staff, such as cleaning bathroom vents to 
improve air circulation and reduce the incidence of mold growth. 
Complex repairs require skilled trades staff, such as plasterers and 
painters, and include repairing leaky pipes or replacing cabinets or 
sheetrock that were removed due to mold growth.  
The subsections below provide an update on NYCHA’s 
compliance with the HUD Agreement requirements governing mold 
work orders in Year 7. 
1. 
Work Plan Distribution (Requirement No. M5)  
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA must provide a resident 
with a work plan within five business days of a resident’s mold 
complaint at least 95% of the time (the “Work Plan Distribution 
Requirement”).123 To prepare a work plan, a NYCHA mold inspector 
verifies that mold is present in the resident’s apartment and 
determines the various child work orders needed to address the 
mold condition.  
To assess compliance with this requirement, NYCHA uses a 
proxy measurement approved by the First Monitor. NYCHA measures 
the time it takes to print the work plan, rather than the time it takes to 
deliver the work plan to the resident. The graph below illustrates that, 
for work orders created in Year 7, NYCHA printed work plans within 
five business days of a resident’s complaint 90% of the time, 
compared to 66% of the time in Year 6. For the first time since the

start of the monitorship, NYCHA is nearly in compliance with this

NYCHA’s improved performance with the Work Plan 
Distribution Requirement is partially attributable to NYCHA staff 
inspecting apartments more quickly after mold is first identified. 
Because staff generate work plans after inspecting and verifying mold 
complaints, a decrease in the number of days to inspect corresponds 
to a decrease in the number of days it takes to deliver work plans to 
residents. In Year 7, NYCHA’s median days to inspect an apartment 
was 1.9 days after a resident’s complaint, meaning that half of the 
mold inspections completed in Year 7 took less than 1.9 days and half 
took more than 1.9 days. This represents a substantial improvement 
over Year 6, when NYCHA’s median days to inspect an apartment was 
3.13 days after the resident’s complaint. 
2. 
Timely 
Removal 
and 
Remediation 
of 
Mold 
(Requirements No. M6) 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to remove or remediate 
mold and its root cause(s) within specified timeframes at least 95% of 
the time.124 NYCHA has two options to comply with this requirement. 
First, NYCHA can remove mold via cleaning or fixture removal within 
five business days of a resident’s complaint or identification by a 
NYCHA staff member.125 Second, if NYCHA does not complete mold 
removal within five business days, the agency can comply by 

103 
 
 
103 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
completing simple repairs within seven days of a resident’s complaint 
or completing complex repairs within 15 days of a resident’s 
complaint. NYCHA’s overall compliance with response times 
prescribed in the HUD Agreement is set forth in the blended rate, 
which measures how often NYCHA removed mold within five days, or 
failing that, how often the agency completed simple and complex 
repairs within seven and 15 days, respectively.  
In Year 7, NYCHA’s performance improved with regards to 
timely removal of mold conditions that were first reported during that 
year. As with the Work Plan Distribution Requirement, this 
improvement is largely attributable to faster inspection and mold 
removal times. Because mold inspection and removal are the first 
steps in mold remediation, faster inspection and removal increase the 
chances that NYCHA will also timely complete simple or complex 
repairs.  
a. 
Mold Removal  
To comply with the HUD Agreement via mold removal, NYCHA 
must remove mold within five business days of a resident’s mold 
complaint. 126  As explained above, removal can consist of mold 
cleaning or fixture removal, depending on the mold condition.  
As illustrated in the graph below, for mold work orders created 
in Year 7, NYCHA removed mold within five business days of a 
resident’s complaint 25% of the time. This is an improvement 
compared to Year 6, when NYCHA removed mold within five days of 
a resident’s complaint 7% of the time.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
 
The Monitors conducted further analysis of NYCHA’s average 
and median days to complete mold removal since the beginning of 
the monitorship. The average days to remove mold shows the sum of 
how many days it took NYCHA to remove mold for each work order 
where mold was removed divided by the total number of mold 
removal work orders completed in that quarter. As such, the average 
values above are more likely to veer higher or lower based on 
isolated cases where mold removal took much more or less time to 
complete than usual.127  
The graph below shows that NYCHA’s efforts have produced a 
consistent and commendable decrease in the median number of 
days to remove mold—from 95 days in the fourth quarter of Year 3 to 
just seven days in the fourth quarter of Year 7. NYCHA attributes the 
increase in median days to remove mold between Years 1 and 3 of 
the monitorship to the rollout of the Mold Busters program, which 
restructured the agency’s response to mold repairs and, among other 
things, resulted in an increase of mold work orders during the rollout 
period. According to NYCHA, the agency has since targeted mold 
and leak complaints through several initiatives, which helped to 
decrease the backlog of mold and leak work orders and, in turn, 
allowed NYCHA staff to more quickly address new complaints in a 
timely manner. While NYCHA still has a long way to go to ensuring 
that residents’ mold conditions are timely addressed, this trend 
demonstrates a meaningful improvement.

Moreover, when the average and median values move closer 
together, it signals that NYCHA’s response to mold work orders is 
becoming more consistent and that delays, bottlenecks, or outlier 
jobs are happening less often. The graph above shows that, in 
addition to NYCHA’s consistent decrease in mold removal times, the 
agency’s mold removal process is becoming more stable and that 
mold removal times for new complaints are becoming more 
predictable.  
b. 
Simple Repairs 
For mold conditions requiring simple repairs that can be 
resolved by NYCHA maintenance staff, if NYCHA does not remove 
the mold within five days of a resident’s complaint, the agency can 
comply with the HUD Agreement by completing all repairs necessary 
to close the parent work order within seven days of a resident’s 
complaint.128 Simple repairs are different from mold removal. Mold 
removal clears the mold hazard from the apartment by either 
cleaning the mold or by removing fixtures containing mold. Repairs 
include all steps needed to address the root cause of the mold 
growth and return the apartment to habitable condition after the 
mold has been removed. For example, if NYCHA removes mold six 
days after the resident’s complaint, that removal would not satisfy the 
Agreement’s five-day deadline. However, in cases where only simple 
repairs are needed, NYCHA could still comply with the overall 

106 
 
106 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
response-time requirement by completing those repairs within seven 
days of the resident’s complaint.  
In Year 7, NYCHA did not complete any simple repairs within 
seven days when it did not first remove mold within five days of a 
resident’s complaint. In other words, while there were some cases 
where NYCHA both removed mold within five days and completed 
simple repairs within seven days (included in the mold removal 
compliance graph above), there were no cases where NYCHA missed 
the HUD Agreement timeline for mold removal but met the timeline 
for completing simple repairs.  
NYCHA has made consistent progress toward timely 
completing simple repairs. As illustrated in the graph below, NYCHA 
completed simple repairs more quickly in Year 7 than during Years 2, 
3, 4, 5, and 6.129 In the last quarter of Year 7, NYCHA completed 
simple repairs in a median of eight days, down from a median of 15 
days in the last quarter of Year 6. Although NYCHA has not yet 
attained compliance with the HUD Agreement timeframes for simple 
repairs, these improvements reflect a promising trend towards timely 
treating resident’s mold conditions. Moreover, as with the mold 
removal data, the trend lines for NYCHA’s average and median days 
to complete simple repairs are moving closer together, indicating 
that NYCHA’s process for completing simple repairs is becoming 
more predictable.

c. 
Complex Repairs 
For mold conditions requiring complex repairs, if NYCHA does 
not remove the mold within five days of a resident’s complaint, the 
agency can comply with the HUD Agreement by completing complex 
repairs within 15 days. 130 The graph below illustrates how often 
NYCHA remediated mold and its root cause(s) via complex repair 
within 15 days of a resident’s complaint when NYCHA did not timely 
remove the mold. 
In Year 7, for work orders where NYCHA did not first remove 
mold within five days, NYCHA completed complex repairs within 15 
days of a resident’s complaint 5% of the time. There were also some 
cases where NYCHA both completed mold removal within five days 
and completed complex repairs within 15 days (included in the mold 
removal compliance graph above).  
  
NYCHA has also made significant progress in the number of 
days it takes to complete a complex repair. For work orders created 
in Year 7, NYCHA completed complex repairs more quickly than any 
year since the beginning of the monitorship.131 As shown in the graph 
below, in the fourth quarter of Year 7, the median number of days for 
NYCHA to complete complex repairs was 20 days, down from a 
median of 107 days at the end of Year 6. While 20 days is longer than 
the 15-day window prescribed by the HUD Agreement, this is a 
significant improvement and indicates that NYCHA’s efforts to reduce

the mold and leak backlog and more quickly inspect residents’ 
apartments are yielding promising results. Lastly, as with the trends 
for simple repairs and mold removal, the average and median days 
to complete complex repairs are moving toward convergence, 
suggesting that NYCHA’s process for complex repairs is becoming 
more stable and predictable. 
 
d. 
Blended Rate 
The rate at which NYCHA removes or remediates mold and its 
root cause(s) within the HUD Agreement timeframes is known as the 
“blended rate.” Although NYCHA’s performance with timely removal 
improved in Year 7, the graph below shows that the agency is still 
substantially out of compliance with the Agreement requirements. 
For work orders created in Year 7, NYCHA complied with the blended 
requirement 29% of the time, compared to 9% of the time in Year 6. 

109 
 
 
109 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
As discussed above, this improvement was largely driven by 
improvements 
in 
NYCHA’s 
timely 
mold 
removal. 
That 
accomplishment notwithstanding, there are several obstacles to 
NYCHA’s compliance with the HUD Agreement timelines for mold 
complaints. First, NYCHA reports that the large backlog of mold and 
leaks work orders compounds the agency’s resource constraints. 
According to NYCHA, the agency has enough staff to keep up with 
work orders created within each year. However, the large number of 
aged, open work orders means that NYCHA staff are often spread too 
thin.132 Skilled trade workers are necessary for some types of mold 
removal (including removing sheetrock or cabinets with mold 
growth) and for all complex repairs. As such, work orders requiring 
those skilled trades often cannot be scheduled within the compliant 
period, meaning that NYCHA cannot complete mold removal or 
complex repairs within the Agreement timeframes.  
Second, NYCHA staff do not always show up to complete work 
on the scheduled date. NYCHA and the Monitors developed a 
dashboard to track missed appointments for skilled trades workers. 
According to that dashboard, NYCHA skilled trades staff showed up 
on the original scheduled date 71% of the time in Year 7. In 9% of 
cases, NYCHA skilled trades staff rescheduled the appointment in 
advance. That means in 20% of cases, NYCHA skilled trades staff 
missed an appointment and did not reschedule the appointment 
ahead of time. NYCHA staff miss scheduled appointments for a 
variety of reasons, including unforeseen emergencies that require 

110 
 
110 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
staff to respond elsewhere, staff members missing work due to illness, 
or shortages of equipment needed to complete the work order. This 
contributes to noncompliance, as missing a scheduled appointment 
within the compliance window will almost certainly extend NYCHA’s 
timeline to complete mold removal or repairs beyond the HUD 
Agreement timelines. Moreover, residents often express their 
concern with missed appointments, both for the delay in addressing 
mold conditions and for the impact on the resident, who may, for 
example, have taken time off work. 
Third, NYCHA may not be able to access the resident’s 
apartment or the area where work is needed. Access issues 
encompass various situations, including circumstances where 
residents are not home to let NYCHA staff in or where residents refuse 
to let NYCHA into their apartments because they are home but 
unavailable or for various other reasons, such as prior work orders 
that were not properly completed. For mold and leak work orders, 
NYCHA staff are able to access residents’ apartments 87% of the time. 
However, work orders that have been open for 100 days or more have 
a 66% access rate.  
NYCHA reports that access issues are problematic because 
they prevent the agency from closing work orders on time 
(particularly in cases where the work was scheduled within the time 
periods prescribed by the HUD Agreement and NYCHA showed up 
on the scheduled day) and because NYCHA staff spend substantial 
time returning to apartments they were previously unable to access 
instead of addressing newly-opened work orders. According to 
NYCHA, although work orders with multiple unsuccessful access 
attempts represent a small percentage of open mold work orders, 
repeatedly returning to the apartments takes significant amounts of 
staff time that could be used to more quickly respond to new work 
orders. NYCHA, along with the Independent Data Analyst (“IDA”) and 
the Monitors, are considering strategies to address these issues.  
3. 
Existence of Large Mold Growths (Requirement No. 
M3)  
During a mold inspection, NYCHA staff measure the size of 
mold growths. Under the HUD Agreement, no more than 15% of 
verified mold complaints may be for mold covering ten or more 
square feet (“Large Mold Growths”).133 

111 
 
 
111 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
As illustrated in the graph below, NYCHA remained 
substantially out of compliance with this requirement through Year 7, 
reporting that 85% of verified mold complaints were for Large Mold 
Growths, a slight regression compared to Year 6, when 84% of 
verified mold complaints were for Large Mold Growths.  
 
As discussed in prior reports, multiple factors contribute to the 
prevalence of Large Mold Growths in NYCHA developments, 
including aging infrastructure—like plumbing lines and hot water 
systems—that cause recurrent leaks, NYCHA workers overestimating 
the sizes of mold growths during inspections, and delayed resident 
reporting.134 NYCHA has taken steps to address the prevalence of 
Large Mold Growths, including by educating residents about the 
importance of timely reporting mold, completing infrastructural 
repairs through capital programs like Comprehensive Modernization 
(“Comp Mod”) and the Building Line Initiative (“BLI”), and using the 
Mold and Leaks Restore and Renew Program (“MLRR”) to direct 
resources to developments with a high volume of open, aged mold 
work orders.  
Although NYCHA’s compliance with the HUD Agreement 
requirements for Large Mold Growths has stagnated in the last few 
years, an analysis of mold work orders shows that NYCHA has made 
some improvements in this area. First, as illustrated in the graph 
below, there were fewer work orders for Large Mold Growths in Year 
7 than during any previous monitorship year. In Year 7, there were 

112 
 
112 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
6,280 work orders for Large Mold Growths, compared to 6,455 work 
orders for Large Mold Growths in Year 6 and 7,299 work orders for 
Large Mold Growths in Year 5.  
 
To better understand Large Mold Growths at NYCHA 
developments, the Monitors’ analysis divided mold work orders into 
the following size categories: work orders for mold growths sized 1 
to 9 square feet (not Large Mold Growths), 10 to 19 square feet, 20 to 
49 square feet, 50 to 99 square feet, and 100 or more square feet. In 
Year 7, there were fewer work orders in almost all size categories. This 
decrease is likely the result of several factors, including faster 
inspections—which catch mold before it grows into larger size 
categories—and better resident communication regarding the 
importance of timely mold reporting. The only exception was work 
orders for mold growths between 20 and 49 square feet, which 
increased from 3,107 work orders in Year 6 to 3,390 work orders in 
Year 7. According to NYCHA, the increase in mold growths sized 20 
to 49 square feet is consistent with the decrease in other categories 
of Large Mold Growths, reflecting that NYCHA is addressing mold 
conditions before they grow to larger sizes.  
Second, the graph below shows different sizes of mold 
growths as a percentage of the overall number of work orders 
created in Year 7. The graph illustrates that, of all mold work orders, 
the percentage of work orders for mold growths sized 10 to 19 square 
feet, 50 to 99 square feet, and 100 or more square feet decreased. 

113 
 
 
113 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
Consistent with the graph above, the percentage of work orders for 
mold growths between 20 to 49 square feet increased from 40.7% in 
Year 6 to 45.9% in Year 7. As with the increase in the number of mold 
growths between 20 and 49 square feet, NYCHA attributes this 
change to faster response times.  
 
Third, in each year since the beginning of the monitorship, 
NYCHA staff identified resident-caused conditions as the leading 
cause of Large Mold Growths. Resident-caused conditions include 
situations where residents in apartments without mechanical 
ventilation did not open bathroom doors or windows to allow for 
proper air circulation, where residents improperly installed 
appliances like dishwashers and washing machines, or where 
residents blocked air vents in their apartments. In some of these 
cases, although resident activity may contribute to or worsen mold 
growth, the mold cannot be solely attributed to resident behavior. 
This is consistent with the fact that NYCHA staff can record multiple 
root causes for mold growths at a time, including “resident caused.”  
Approximately half of NYCHA developments do not have 
mechanical ventilation in bathrooms. In those developments, 
residents must open their bathroom doors or windows to manually 
ventilate apartments and prevent excess moisture that causes mold. 
A portion of the Large Mold Growths listed as “resident-caused” are 
attributable to apartments without mechanical ventilation where 
residents did not manually ventilate the room. The monitorship is 

114 
 
114 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
investigating the various causes of Large Mold Growths and will 
report on their findings in a subsequent report. 
That investigation will include circumstances in which NYCHA 
finds that the resident caused the Large Mold Growth to confirm that 
NYCHA is correctly applying the appropriate standards. In or about 
2020, NYCHA, the previous Monitor, and the Baez-appointed 
Independent Mold Analyst (“IMA”) found that NYCHA staff often 
incorrectly identified resident behavior as the root cause of mold 
growths. In response, NYCHA updated mold inspector trainings to 
specify that “resident caused” can only be selected as a root cause of 
mold if (1) there is proof that the resident’s behavior caused the 
condition and (2) if the condition could be prevented through 
resident education and a change in resident behavior. NYCHA also 
released an IT enhancement in December 2021 that clarified the 
language around root causes to eliminate potential ambiguity in the 
inspection 
process. 
Additionally, 
NYCHA’s 
Office 
of 
Mold 
Assessment 
and 
Remediation 
(“OMAR”) 
and 
Compliance 
Department, along with the IMA, conduct multiple root cause 
trainings each year for inspectors that have been flagged for not 
selecting the proper root causes of mold. Finally, the IMA regularly 
conducts Quality Assurance inspections for completed mold work 
orders. If an IMA inspection shows that a NYCHA inspector incorrectly 
identified mold growth as being caused by a resident, the inspector 
will be redirected to complete additional mold trainings and will be 
flagged for further follow-up and investigation with NYCHA’s 
compliance department.  
The second leading cause of Large Mold Growths was 
damaged or missing caulking on bathtubs and other bathroom 
fixtures, which accounted for 27% of all Large Mold Growths in Year 
7. Missing or damaged caulking allows water to seep into apartment 
walls and other areas that are not visible to residents, creating damp 
conditions where mold can grow undetected for long periods of time 
before being identified by a resident or mold inspector.  
Lastly, Leaks from Above accounted for 11.5% of all Large 
Mold Growths. Leaks from Above have multiple causes, including 
improperly installed appliances in upstairs apartments or leaking 
plumbing and waste lines. Even minor leaks can result in drastic mold 
conditions, including Large Mold Growths, if the leak is not identified 
or repaired for a long period of time.

Analyst (“IMA”): The

appointed under the

apartments on a

Leaks from Above:. 
Floods, leaks, or water 
penetration into a 
resident's apartment 
from another

4. 
Recurrence 
Mold conditions may recur after the parent work order has 
been completed. Multiple factors contribute to mold recurrence, 
including infrastructural issues that require capital investments to 
properly address or NYCHA staff improperly completing the initial 
repair.  
a. 
Recurrence of Verified Mold Complaints 
(Requirement No. M2) 
Under the HUD Agreement, there must not be a second 
verified mold complaint for the same apartment, common area room, 
or hallway within a 12-month period for 85% of verified mold 
complaints (the “Recurring Complaints”). 135  The Monitors cannot 
report with confidence on Recurring Complaints for Year 7 due to 
their concerns about the quality of the data that may have caused the 
recurrence rate for mold complaints to be understated.  
First, NYCHA’s work order reporting system limits the 
Monitors’ ability to track recurrence. This is because NYCHA’s system 
prevents residents from submitting new work orders for recurrence 
while there is an open mold work order in the same room in their 
apartment. While this was initially designed to prevent the creation of 
duplicate work orders, it is problematic in situations where, for 
example, NYCHA has completed the mold cleaning but has not yet 
completed all of the necessary repairs. In those situations, the child 
work orders associated with mold cleaning are closed but, because 
there are remaining open repair work orders—such as paint and 
plaster work orders—the overall parent work order remains open. 
Accordingly, if mold were to recur in the same room during this 
period, NYCHA’s system would block the resident from lodging the 
complaint and NYCHA’s data would not record the recurrence. This 
limitation does not apply to NYCHA staff and NYCHA’s system allows 
staff to report recurrence if they identify recurring mold conditions 
during an inspection or while completing another repair. NYCHA is 
working with the Monitors to identify solutions to this issue that will 
allow for better tracking of mold recurrence and improve resident 
experiences by allowing residents to report recurrences that occur 
after mold has been removed but before the parent work order has 
been closed.  

116 
 
116 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
Second, NYCHA discovered that a program used to identify 
unreported instances of mold recurrence yielded inaccurate results 
because of misrepresentations by a NYCHA employee. One of 
NYCHA’s efforts to improve its performance regarding mold 
recurrence is the Post-Close Survey program, in which NYCHA staff 
call residents after parent work order completion to verify that work 
orders were properly closed and that mold complaints have not 
resurfaced. The program was previously run by NYCHA’s Customer 
Contact Center (“CCC”) but was taken over by OMAR about one year 
ago. In November 2025, NYCHA discovered that the Post-Close 
Survey reported zero recurrences—a figure inconsistent with the 
recurrence rate reported under the Baez Consent Decree. This 
discrepancy prompted the head of the Mold Response Unit (“MRU”) 
to more closely review reported resident responses, revealing that 
one NYCHA staff member misrepresented that he was making the 
required calls. NYCHA immediately removed the staff member from 
the program and called all residents the staff member was 
responsible for contacting—approximately 5,000 individuals. As of 
February 4, 2026, NYCHA reports that it successfully completed 
1,928 calls, resulting in 628 reported recurrences. NYCHA also 
reports that it has since inspected 624 of those work orders—278, or 
44%, were closed as unfounded, meaning NYCHA did not identify 
mold during the inspection. For the 346 founded mold complaints, 
NYCHA created new work orders to address recurring conditions.  
NYCHA has also taken steps to improve accountability and 
prevent future incorrect reporting under the Post-Close Survey 
program, including by recording survey calls with residents, 
strengthening the internal audit process, and working with the 
Environmental Health and Safety department to develop additional 
program oversight. In light of this development and because 
NYCHA’s system blocks residents from reporting recurrences when 
there is an open work order in the same room, the Monitors are 
assessing the accuracy of available recurrence data. 
b. 
Occurrence of Mold (Requirement No. M4) 
Under the HUD Agreement, mold must not occur more than 
three times in a single apartment over a 12-month period.136 As with 
the Recurring Complaints, the Monitors cannot report on NYCHA’s 
performance with this requirement due to concerns about the 
accuracy of the underlying data. NYCHA and the Monitors are 

117 
 
 
117 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
working to identify solutions to these concerns and will provide 
updates on those efforts in future reporting.  
B. 
Leak Work Orders 
NYCHA’s response to leak conditions is governed by the Leak 
Standard Procedure (“Leak SP”), which NYCHA published and 
began rolling out in Year 7. As with mold work orders, the HUD 
Agreement requires that NYCHA timely address leak conditions.  
1. 
Abating Floods, Leaks from Above, and Other 
Moisture Conditions (Requirement No. M7) 
 
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA is required to abate 
floods, Leaks from Above, and other conditions that cause sustained 
or recurrent moisture in resident apartments or apartment walls 
(“Other Moisture Conditions”) within 24 hours of a resident’s 
complaint.137 NYCHA must also remove any resulting standing water 
within 48 hours of a resident’s complaint at least 95% of the time.138  
As illustrated in the graph below, NYCHA abated floods, Leaks 
From Above, and Other Moisture Conditions within 24 hours 72% of 
the time in Year 7, compared to 70% of the time in Year 6.  
  
In Year 7, NYCHA made progress on several projects intended 
to decrease the amount of time it takes to abate floods, Leaks From 
Above, and Other Moisture Conditions. These projects include hiring 
Leak Standard 
Procedure:. A 
standardized process for 
identifying, tracing, and 
addressing the root 
causes of leaks across all 
NYCHA developments. 

118 
 
118 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
more skilled trades staff,139 enhancing worker trainings, reducing the 
work order backlog through MLRR, implementing a standard 
procedure for responding to and remediating leaks, and updating 
aging infrastructure through large-scale capital renovations provided 
by the Comp Mod and BLI programs.140  
As discussed in the Monitors’ Sixth Report, the Leak SP allows 
NYCHA and the Monitors to track NYCHA’s removal of standing 
water. 141 The Leak SP also provides staff with standardized, best-
practice processes for identifying, tracing, and fixing leaks and their 
root cause(s).142 These standardizations are designed to help NYCHA 
to perform more consistent, higher-quality repair work, and reduce 
sustained moisture conditions, thereby decreasing the incidence of 
mold growth. 143  In Year 7, NYCHA implemented the Leak SP in 
Queens and Staten Island and trained staff at all developments in 
those boroughs. NYCHA is currently in the process of implementing 
the Leak SP in Brooklyn.  
The Monitors cannot provide an update on NYCHA’s 
compliance with the second part of the requirement—that NYCHA 
remove standing water within 48 hours of a resident’s complaint—
because, as set forth above, NYCHA is still in the process of 
implementing the Leak SP.144 As part of the Leak SP, NYCHA created 
an IT update that allows staff to track responses to standing water 
through their handheld devices, which NYCHA is currently releasing 
on a development-by-development basis when 80% or more of a 
development’s staff have completed Leak SP training. NYCHA, along 
with the Monitors and the IDA, are currently in the process of 
establishing business rules to calculate NYCHA’s compliance with this 
requirement.  
C. 
Mold and Leak Work Order Backlog  
NYCHA has approximately 65,000 open mold and leak work 
orders, which NYCHA refers to as the mold and leak backlog.145 The 
backlog includes different types of work orders related to mold and 
leaks and the substantial majority of these work orders are generated 
in response to resident complaints. As illustrated in the graph below, 
the backlog had 65,182 work orders as of the end of Year 7. This 
represents a decrease of 9,077 work orders since the end of Year 
6.146  

119 
 
 
119 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
 
NYCHA reports that the mold and leak backlog undermines its 
ability to comply with mold removal and remediation requirements 
because the large volume of aging work orders awaiting repair 
diverts resources from newer work orders, making it difficult to 
respond within required timeframes—and driving up both average 
and median response times.  
NYCHA has taken steps to reduce the mold and leak work 
order backlog, including through MLRR, which made substantial 
progress in Year 7. As discussed in previous reports, NYCHA, in 
partnership with the IDA, HUD, SDNY, and the monitorship, launched 
MLRR in August 2025. Building on the success of the Enhanced 
Oversight Program (“EOP”), which took a focused approach to 
address select mold and leak conditions in a two-month period at 
high-need developments, MLRR is a two-year program that provides 
skilled trades and administrative resources to 12 developments with 
a high volume of aged, open mold and leak work orders.  
At Sotomayor Houses, the first MLRR development, NYCHA 
closed 512 work orders between September 22, 2025 and December 
31, 2025—representing 83% of the MLRR-eligible work orders and 
falling short of MLRR’s goal to close all eligible work orders.147 The 
agency attempted to complete, but did not close, an additional 66 
work orders—meaning that 94% of MLRR-eligible work orders 
received some level of engagement from the program, including 

120 
 
120 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
attempted repairs, completed repairs, and work orders that were 
closed after NYCHA verified that work was no longer needed.  
NYCHA ascribes this shortfall to several factors associated with 
the program roll-out, including delays in the hiring process for MLRR 
skilled trade staff, which caused the program to be understaffed 
through November of 2025; challenges contacting residents, which 
subsequently resulted in access issues; lack of capacity for NYCHA 
planners; and delays in the work order verification process. NYCHA is 
working with the IDA and the Monitors to address these issues and 
improve performance at future sites, including by approving 
overtime for neighborhood planners, working with the MRU to 
contact residents and perform work order verification, continuing 
MLRR skilled trade hiring and using borough skilled trades workers 
for additional support, and increasing in-person visits to residents 
before work is scheduled to begin. 
NYCHA and the IDA began the second round of MLRR at 
Whitman Houses in December 2025, which is currently in progress. 
As of the end of Year 7, NYCHA and the IDA verified that 71 work 
orders that had been listed as outstanding in fact no longer required 
additional work, and completed work on an additional 461 work 
orders—representing 66% of MLRR-eligible work orders at Whitman 
Houses. Although the Whitman Houses MLRR is running behind 
schedule, NYCHA’s performance at Whitman Houses shows key 
improvements over the first round MLRR at Sotomayor Houses and 
suggests that the program is maturing and resolving initial challenges 
described above. NYCHA and the IDA also began work order 
verification at the third MLRR site, Ingersoll Houses, and MLRR skilled 
trades staff started work on eligible work orders in March 2026.  
Considering lessons learned during the MLRR project at 
Sotomayor Houses, NYCHA is making several enhancements to MLRR 
to improve resident experiences and program efficiency. Most 
notably, NYCHA is taking a more holistic approach to determining 
work orders that are included in MLRR’s scope. Rather than focusing 
on individual mold and leak work orders that have been open for 
more than 100 days, NYCHA will now focus on completing the parent 
work order to which the MLRR child is connected and completing 
some work orders generated by the Quality Assurance inspection of 
the original MLRR work order. In effect, NYCHA will complete the 
larger job associated with the mold or leak condition, rather than 
completing a single step of that job. This approach has the added 

121 
 
 
121 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
benefit of improving inter-departmental coordination to more 
efficiently complete MLRR-eligible work orders. NYCHA has also 
committed to conducting desktop reviews of MLRR work orders. 
Through desktop reviews, which are completed before NYCHA staff 
enter the apartment, NYCHA staff confirm that MLRR-eligible work 
orders have been properly sequenced and check for open mold and 
leak conditions that might impact the MLRR work orders—thereby 
improving the program’s efficiency. 
D. 
Mold-Related Capital Projects  
As discussed in the Monitors’ Sixth Report, NYCHA’s Comp 
Mod and BLI programs address infrastructural causes of mold growth 
and recurrence.148 Through the Comp Mod program, select NYCHA 
developments undergo large-scale renovations, including roof and 
façade repairs, plumbing replacements, ventilation upgrades, and 
kitchen and bathroom renovations, to resolve recurrent leaks and 
moisture conditions.149 Through the BLI program, NYCHA replaces 
high-leak plumbing lines that frequently impact apartments and 
common areas. 150  Building on the success of the BLI program, 
NYCHA will reallocate funds in the City Capital Action Plan (“CCAP”) 
to create a Waste Plumbing Initiative (“WPI”), which will replace 
severely corroded plumbing waste lines that cause frequent and 
repeated leaks in NYCHA developments. These programs are crucial 
to updating aging infrastructure that contributes to mold growth and 
recurrence, which cannot effectively be addressed through targeted 
repairs in individual apartments.  
1. 
Comprehensive Modernization  
Since the Monitors’ Sixth Report, NYCHA has continued to 
advance key Comp Mod projects. At Todt Hill Houses, Phase 1 of 
construction incurred an 11-week delay due to issues with Asbestos 
Containing Materials (“ACM”) filings, late delivery of elevator 
equipment, and delays with elevator testing. NYCHA does not expect 
that this delay will impact subsequent construction phases. With the 
delay, NYCHA’s design-build vendor completed interior construction 
for 142 apartments in Phase 1 and returned all residents to their 
apartment in January 2026. The vendor also completed façade 
compliance work for Phase 1 buildings and is awaiting final sign-off 
by the Department of Buildings. Phase 2, impacting an additional 144 
apartments, is progressing on schedule. The vendor completed 
resident relocations and began work on lead abatement. NYCHA 

122 
 
122 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
anticipates that the vendor will complete Phase 2 construction in 
September 2026. It further forecasts that all four phases of the Todt 
Hill project will be completed in March 2028.  
The Comp Mod project at Saint Nicholas Houses has 
experienced a cumulative 52-week delay, reflecting an additional 12-
week delay since the Monitors’ Sixth Report. NYCHA attributes the 
additional delay to several factors—revision of ACM filings for Phase 1 
buildings, delays in establishing temporary heating on site in 
November 2025 through February 2026, and a stop work order in 
February 2026. NYCHA is taking steps to get the design-build vendor 
back on track with the recovery schedule, including by resequencing 
activities and approvals, streamlining processes, and increasing shifts 
and manpower to complete construction work more quickly. To avoid 
similar delays in Phase 2, the vendor has updated its filing and 
abatement plan to account for the potential discovery of additional 
asbestos and lead materials and is considering options to mitigate 
the impact of Phase 1 delays on subsequent project phases and 
complete construction in October 2029 as originally planned.  
Despite these delays, the vendor has made progress on Phase 
1 buildings, which include Saint Nicholas buildings 10 and 12. As of 
the end of Year 7, the vendor completed lead and asbestos 
abatement in building 10 and is proceeding with window installation, 
rough plumbing, and rough electrical work. In building 12, NYCHA 
has completed the first phase of lead and asbestos abatement and is 
awaiting additional city agency approvals to further progress the 
work.  
2. 
Building Line Initiative 
In Year 7, NYCHA had two ongoing BLI projects—one at 
Tompkins Houses (85 Tompkins Avenue, A line), impacting 15 
apartments, and another at Red Hook East Houses (124 Bush Street, 
A and B lines), impacting 12 apartments. NYCHA has also advanced 
the pre-construction phase of the fourth BLI project at Carleton 
Manor, impacting ten apartments. NYCHA has selected additional 
plumbing lines at Red Hook East Houses for the fifth BLI project and 
is conducting inspections at Red Hook West Houses for the sixth BLI 
project. 
NYCHA completed the BLI project at Tompkins Houses in Year 
7, with general construction completed on January 31, 2026, and final 

123 
 
 
123 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
electrical and plumbing permits approved early in February 2026—
approximately ten months later than originally forecasted. As 
discussed in prior reports, NYCHA attributed this delay to 
complications retaining a subcontractor for electrical work, which 
pushed the projected completion date back by nine months, and 
unanticipated field conditions that caused an additional one-month 
delay.151 As of this report, the Tompkins Houses BLI project passed its 
quality assurance inspection, and NYCHA has begun returning 
residents to their apartments.  
At Red Hook East Houses, NYCHA is advancing two BLI 
projects—an A-line project providing bathroom renovations and 
waste line replacements at six apartments, and a B-line project 
delivering more extensive renovations to six apartments. NYCHA 
completed resident relocation for the B Line in December 2025, with 
asbestos and lead abatement complete and demolition in progress. 
The project is pending City approval of NYCHA’s tenant protection 
plan before plumbing work can proceed. NYCHA forecasts 
completing the B-line project in December 2026. NYCHA anticipates 
completing A-Line relocation by the end of March 2026, with 
construction expected to take approximately four weeks thereafter, 
and will finalize construction in spring 2026.  
At Carleton Manor, NYCHA has defined the scope of work and 
is reviewing the vendor’s proposal. 152  Resident relocation is in 
progress, with eight of ten apartments relocated in Year 7.  
As discussed in the Executive Summary, the government has 
approved an amendment to the CCAP that reallocates $50 million 
from waste management capital projects to heating and plumbing 
initiatives. Of this funding, $10 million is allocated to the heating 
system renovation at Pomonok Houses, which will free up $10 million 
in federal funds to support additional BLI projects. The remaining $40 
million is allocated to a new Waste Plumbing Initiative (“WPI”) that 
draws on the success of both the BLI and Comp Mod programs to 
replace severely deteriorated waste plumbing lines and related 
branch plumbing, as well as kitchen and bathroom components, at 
one to two developments with approximately 300 impacted 
apartments. The Monitors will track NYCHA’s progress in 
implementing WPI and report on these projects in future reporting.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
E. 
Compliance with Baez (Requirement No. M8) 
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA must comply with the 
terms of court orders entered in Baez, a federal class action lawsuit 
brought by NYCHA residents suffering from asthma and living in 
apartments with mold and excessive moisture.153  
As of January 31, 2026, the IDA’s reporting shows that NYCHA 
is compliant with one of the requirements imposed by the Baez 
Consent Decree, that it repairs simple leaks within seven days. 
NYCHA is not compliant with the other Baez requirements. NYCHA’s 
obligations to respond to leaks under the HUD Agreement are 
materially different from the obligations imposed by Baez and, as 
noted above, NYCHA does not yet have the data to assess its 
compliance under the Agreement.  
The Monitors will continue to collaborate with the IMA, IDA, 
and Baez Plaintiffs to address NYCHA’s mold and leak issues in a 
coordinated manner.

I.5 
Lead 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to comply with federal 
lead regulations, including compliance with lead safe work practices 
and timely completion of interim controls to limit the risks posed by 
existing lead-based paint. 154  The Agreement ultimately requires 
NYCHA to abate all interior lead-based paint in its developments by 
2039.155 This subsection provides an update on NYCHA’s progress on 
its outstanding lead-related obligations under the Agreement for 
Year 7.156 Agreement obligations that NYCHA has completed may be 
found in Appendix A.  
A. 
Testing 
and 
Abatement 
Progress 
in 
Apartments 
(Requirement Nos. L6, L20-L22) 
 The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to abate 50% of 
apartments by 2029, 75% of apartments by 2034, and 100% of 
apartments by 2039. 157  To identify the complete universe of 
apartments containing lead-based paint and requiring abatement, 
NYCHA has undertaken a campaign to conduct X-Ray Fluorescence 
(“XRF”) testing in all apartments not previously tested at the current 
NYC definition of lead paint (0.5 mg/cm2). 158 As described further 
below, NYCHA is nearing completion of its testing initiative.159  
1. 
XRF Testing in Apartments  
As of January 31, 2026, NYCHA reports that it has completed 
substantially all XRF testing for apartments. Approximately 3.2% 
(4,693 apartments) of all apartments in the testing universe have been 
attempted but not yet tested because of access issues, down from 
5.9% (8,645 apartments) as of January 31, 2025. Untested apartments 
remain in scope and are subject to NYCHA’s ongoing, documented 
access efforts as NYCHA continues to focus on resolving persistent 
access issues. This includes initiating administrative tenancy actions 
for apartments where residents repeatedly refuse access or where 
apartment conditions have made testing impractical (such as 
hoarding). NYCHA is also considering other options for apartments 
where residents are not home for multiple access attempts, including 
a progressive access protocol whereby NYCHA provides 48-hour 
notice that it will enter an apartment to address health and safety 
conditions after repeated documented attempts and escalation 
through established administrative process. NYCHA also reports that, 
as of January 31, 2026, it identified lead-based paint in 61,253, or 
X-Ray Fluorescence 
(“XRF”) Testing: XRF 
testing is a non-
destructive analytical 
technique that uses X-
rays to determine the 
elemental composition 
of a material, including 
to determine whether 
lead is present.

45%, of tested apartments and found no lead-based paint in 75,196,

While NYCHA continues to make progress in its abatement

Still, based on abatement totals to date and NYCHA’s current

assuming that NYCHA remains at or above its average rate of

common area abatement plan NYCHA is developing will enable the
