A. 
Lead Abatement Vendor Oversight 
The Monitors are reviewing NYCHA’s oversight of its lead 
abatement vendors. As part of its responsibilities in administering its 
Lead Abatement Program, NYCHA procures and retains abatement 
vendors through a competitive process that requires compliance with 

134 
 
134 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
applicable EPA, HUD, OSHA, New York State and New York City 
requirements for lead-based paint activities. Vendors must hold 
appropriate firm certifications, and individual supervisors and 
workers must maintain current federal, state, and city certifications 
and training credentials, which NYCHA verifies prior to assignment of 
work. NYCHA also reviews vendors’ compliance history, including 
prior performance on NYCHA projects and, where available, 
regulatory or enforcement history, before issuing work and 
periodically thereafter. 
Beyond prequalification, NYCHA maintains ongoing oversight 
of abatement vendor performance. NYCHA authorizes and initiates 
abatement work through formal Notices to Proceed and associated 
work orders that establish defined response times, mobilization 
requirements, work practices, and documentation obligations. 
NYCHA program management staff and field inspectors monitor 
compliance with containment procedures, supervision requirements, 
EPA notification protocols, and clearance documentation. Where 
deficiencies are identified (whether through field observation, 
compliance audits, or agency review), NYCHA issues written notices 
of non-compliance or Letters of Direction, requires corrective action 
plans, and may mandate retraining. Escalation measures can include 
suspension of work orders, withholding or reduction of payments, or 
contract termination. Collectively, these procurement controls, field 
oversight practices, and independent compliance reviews form the 
core of NYCHA’s vendor governance framework for lead abatement 
activities. NYCHA reports that consistent application of these 
oversight mechanisms has improved vendor adherence to 
mobilization, notification, and supervisory requirements over time. 
1. 
EH&S Compliance Assurance Inspections 
Pursuant to the HUD Agreement, EH&S conducts independent 
compliance assurance inspections of both vendors and NYCHA 
personnel performing lead-related work, including abatement, risk 
assessment, repair and repainting, dust wipe clearance, and visual 
assessments.161 These inspections are carried out by the EH&S Lead 
Oversight Team, which applies established Corrective Action and 
Escalation Protocols when noncompliance is observed. Such 
protocols may include immediate cessation of noncompliant work, 
supervisory notification, documented corrective measures, follow-up 
inspections, and formal escalation where warranted. 

135 
 
 
135 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
2. 
NYCHA “Pause Inspection” and Quality Assurance 
Inspections 
As part of its vendor oversight, NYCHA conducts a “Pause 
Inspection” during every abatement project to ensure the work meets 
standards and is completed according to the issued scope. NYCHA 
began performing its Pause Inspections program in 2024. During a 
“Pause Inspection,” which occurs after abatement work is completed 
but before clearance testing, NYCHA inspectors examine the work 
area to confirm that all required paint removal has been performed 
and that underlying surfaces are properly exposed and prepared for 
repainting. This inspection allows NYCHA to identify any remaining 
paint, incomplete work, or other issues while the abatement vendor 
is still at the job site, before containment is removed and final 
clearance dust testing occurs, ensuring that deficiencies can be 
corrected before the job is closed out. After the inspection is 
complete, the abatement vendor is given approval to remove the 
containment and proceed with cleaning. After cleaning, a final dust 
wipe clearance test is completed, which ensures that cleanup after 
the abatement was adequate and that dust lead levels are below 
Federal and NYC clearance levels. 
In addition to Pause Inspections, NYCHA implemented an 
additional inspection for apartments that have already been abated, 
including apartments abated before NYCHA implemented Pause 
Inspections in 2024. NYCHA reports that it has completed post-
abatement inspections for approximately 78% of apartments abated 
prior to the Pause Inspection implementation. For the remaining 22% 
of abated apartments, NYCHA intends to conduct post-abatement 
review and inspection work in tandem with the next ordinary course 
work order issued for an apartment to minimize disruptions to 
residents.  
3. 
Vendor-Related Complaints and Nonconformance 
Reports 
In addition to the vendor oversight measures described above, 
NYCHA also collects data on resident complaints and vendor 
nonconformance. Between February 1, 2025, and January 31, 2026, 
NYCHA’s lead abatement vendors completed 4,958 abatements. 
During that same period, NYCHA issued 171 nonconformance 
reports documenting instances where vendor performance did not 
meet program requirements. These reports are generated when 

136 
 
136 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
NYCHA identifies a specific issue through its oversight, which 
includes schedule monitoring, field inspections, and compliance 
reviews. The chart below summarizes the types of nonconformance 
reports issued during this period. The most common issue was 
vendors not beginning work when scheduled (referred to as a “failure 
to mobilize as scheduled”), followed by abatements that took longer 
than expected. Other types of issues, including defective or 
nonconforming work, clearance failures, and health and safety 
violations, were reported far less frequently. 
 
Although 
NYCHA 
reports 
a 
relatively 
low 
rate 
of 
nonconformance relative to the 4,958 abatements completed 
between February 1, 2025, and January 31, 2026, nonconformance 
reports enable NYCHA to identify vendor performance issues and 
adjust its vendor management and deployment accordingly. When 
issues arise, NYCHA takes steps to address them, such as requiring 
immediate correction, increasing oversight of the vendor’s work, 
requiring the vendor to submit corrective action plans, or adjusting 
work assignments. For example, NYCHA reports that it has previously 
lowered the number of jobs assigned to a vendor that was struggling 

137 
 
 
137 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
to complete work on schedule and reassigned those jobs to vendors 
with greater capacity.  
Additionally, for certain material or persistent vendor 
nonconformance issues, NYCHA sends vendors Letters of Direction 
or Notices of Non-Compliant Performance. Between February 1, 
2025, and January 31, 2026, NYCHA issued twelve such letters. The 
letters address issues including failure to mobilize work crews as 
scheduled, inadequate supervision at job sites, and failure to comply 
with regulatory notification requirements. In these communications, 
NYCHA identifies the contractual provisions at issue, explains the 
operational or safety implications of the nonconformance, and 
reminds vendors that continued violations may lead to escalation 
measures such as suspension of work orders, withholding or 
reduction of payments, or contract termination. These letters 
therefore serve both as corrective instructions and as part of NYCHA’s 
formal escalation framework for vendor performance management.  
In one case, NYCHA identified an administrative deficiency in 
the EPA notification process during routine oversight of the Lead 
Hazard Control Department (“LHCD”)’s abatement program, 
determining that a single vendor had been filing amended EPA 
notifications in the EPA Central Data Exchange (“CDX”) after 
abatement work had already been completed, rather than at least five 
business days prior to the actual start date as required. Upon 
confirming the issue, LHCD conducted a corrective call with the 
vendor to clarify the proper sequencing and timing of both initial and 
amended EPA filings, and formalized expectations through a Letter 
of Direction reinforcing mandatory USEPA notification and site 
posting requirements. In addition, LHCD implemented a program-
wide requirement that the EPA notice posted at each job site be a 
printed copy generated directly from the EPA CDX, enabling 
inspectors to verify compliance in real time and prevent recurrence 
of the issue.

(Requirement Nos. L23-L25)  
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA is required to comply with 
various federal and local regulations when it becomes aware of a 
child under age six (“CU6”) with an elevated blood lead level 
(“EBLL”) in its developments.162 In Year 7, as of February 2, 2026, 27 
EBLLs 163  were reported by the health department to NYCHA for

Level (“EBLL”): EBLL is a 
confirmed concentration 
of lead in the blood of a 
child under age six equal 
to or greater than 3.5 
micrograms per deciliter 
or higher.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
children under age 6 living in NYCHA properties and required 
abatement, as compared to 49 and 48 for approximately the same 
period in Years 5 and 6, respectively.164 
 
Throughout Year 7, NYCHA was in substantial compliance with 
its Agreement requirements to notify the Monitors and the U.S.

reports the delays were attributable to resident availability and 
refusals for access. 
C. 
Testing and Abatement of Childcare Centers   
As previously reported, in response to a 2024 increase in EBLLs 
connected to childcare centers located on NYCHA properties, 
NYCHA has committed to prioritizing childcare centers as the next 
phase of its comprehensive XRF testing initiative. Since the end of the 
current reporting period on January 31, 2026, NYCHA received one 
COTA for a childcare center at Mariner’s Harbor on March 9, 2026. 
NYCHA is in the process of conducting additional testing and scoping 
the location for abatement. The Monitors will continue to work with 
NYCHA and report on progress in the next report.  

139 
 
 
139 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
In 2025, NYCHA reported that it conducted visual assessments 
in 75 out of 87 identified childcare centers as part of its regular annual 
visual assessment process and determined that the remaining 12 
locations are not currently operating as childcare centers and will not 
be part of NYCHA’s childcare center focused testing and abatement 
efforts. NYCHA further reports it identified deficiencies in 88% of 
completed visual assessments. Deficiencies may include peeling or 
chipping paint. Deficiencies are particularly dangerous if they contain 
lead-based paint and must be addressed through either abatement 
or interim controls. NYCHA reports that only 9% of deficiencies 
identified in childcare centers had a positive XRF test result. Where it 
identified deficiencies or deteriorated paint with a positive XRF result, 
NYCHA reports that it implemented interim controls, on average, 15 
days after performing visual assessments. Where deficiencies tested 
negative for lead-based paint, a work order was entered to repaint, 
as the deficiency was not considered a lead-based paint hazard.  
In 2026, NYCHA will conduct XRF testing in all childcare 
centers on its properties, submit a budget proposal, and formalize an 
abatement plan. NYCHA was able to get an early start on this initiative 
in December 2025 while centers were closed for holiday break. Ten 
childcare centers were comprehensively XRF tested. Of these, two 
were positive, and interim controls were put in place to remediate the 
deficiencies until abatement can begin. The Monitors will continue to 
report on these efforts and urge NYCHA to commence abatement in 
childcare centers as soon as possible. 
D. 
Apartments That Tested Negative Prior to Receiving an 
EBLL COTA 
Of the 27 remaining COTAs in Year 7, nine involved 
apartments whose XRF testing had previously indicated that they 
were negative for lead-based paint. Five of these nine cases involved 
dust COTAs rather than paint COTAs, meaning that the New York City 
Department of Health and Mental Hygiene (“DOHMH”) identified 
elevated lead levels in dust within the apartment but did not identify 
lead-based paint as the source. In such cases, NYCHA resolves the 
violation by cleaning the unit and performing follow-up dust wipe 
testing until clearance is achieved. The remaining four apartments 
involved paint COTAs based on DOHMH XRF readings on wall or 
ceiling components. NYCHA reported that in several cases, the 
DOHMH XRF readings exceeded NYCHA’s earlier XRF results 
obtained during prior inspections, but even so, would have been 

140 
 
140 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
considered inconclusive under federal performance standards but 
were nevertheless treated as positive and abated. 
Several factors, common across the lead-testing industry and 
not specific to NYCHA, may help explain why an apartment that 
previously tested negative for lead-based paint could later be 
identified as the source of an EBLL case and ultimately require 
abatement. One issue involves how XRF testing works. XRF 
instruments are widely used and generally reliable, but some level of 
inaccurate or inconclusive results is inherent in XRF testing, in part 
because lead is being measured through the entire surface scanned 
and results are not isolated to paint. In certain building materials such 
as reinforced concrete ceilings, plaster with metal lath, or other 
substrates that contain metal, the instrument may detect signals from 
the material beneath the paint rather than from the paint layer itself. 
In those situations, the reading may reflect benign characteristics of 
the building material rather than lead in the paint film. At the same 
time, inspections typically test specific components of a surface rather 
than every square inch, and paint layers can vary across a surface. 
Dust lead testing can also reflect dust that entered the apartment 
from other sources unrelated to paint. 
Federal guidance attempts to manage these issues through 
quality control procedures for XRF devices and trained inspectors. 
Even with those safeguards, both XRF testing and laboratory paint 
chip sampling can produce variable results in certain situations. 
Importantly, the goal of these tests is to determine how much lead is 
present in the paint layer itself (not misleading readings due to the 
underlying building material). In some circumstances, however, 
NYCHA’s ability to perform confirmatory paint chip testing is 
constrained by local policies even if otherwise permitted under 
federal regulations. Paint chip testing can sometimes help distinguish 
whether a reading is coming from the paint or from the substrate 
underneath. When that additional testing is not permitted, NYCHA is 
required to proceed based on XRF results even if questions remain 
about whether the paint itself is the true source of lead. 
The goal of the program is to eliminate the source of lead 
exposure for NYCHA residents, particularly children, and abatement 
is an essential tool in achieving that objective. At the same time, some 
abatement work (particularly on concrete or other structural surfaces) 
may require chemical removal methods that carry additional safety 
considerations for workers. Ensuring that abatement is directed at the 

141 
 
 
141 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
true source of lead exposure helps protect NYCHA residents as well 
as NYCHA workers and allows resources to be focused on apartments 
requiring abatement. 
For these reasons, the Monitors and their lead expert believe 
this area warrants further analysis of the impact of testing limitations 
on DOHMH’s approach to NYCHA buildings, including the 
interaction between XRF testing and certain building substrates, and 
whether confirmatory paint chip testing might be appropriate under 
a broader set of circumstances than is currently permitted under local 
regulations. The Monitors have asked their lead expert to conduct 
additional technical analysis and engage in discussion with both 
NYCHA and the DOHMH to assess these topics and make 
recommendations regarding how best to protect NYCHA residents 
and workers.  
E. 
Conducting Visual Assessments and Remediating 
Identified Paint Deficiencies (Requirement Nos. L28-
L29) 
Under the HUD Agreement and relevant federal and local lead 
regulations, NYCHA is required to conduct visual assessments of 
apartments known or presumed to contain lead-based paint and 
must remediate any deficiencies identified during those assessments 
within certain federally mandated time periods.166 In 2025, NYCHA 
reports that it attempted visual assessments for 47,137 apartments, 
representing 100% 167  of apartments subject to visual assessment 
requirements (i.e., apartments in buildings constructed prior to 1960 
and certain apartments built between 1960 and 1978 where lead-
based paint is known to be present), excluding units that have already 
been abated to the 0.5 mg/cm² standard or previously tested 
negative for lead-based paint. NYCHA reports that it was able to 
complete visual assessments in approximately 76% of such 
apartments. In the remaining 24% of cases, inspectors were unable to 
gain access, most commonly because residents were not home at the 
time of the visit or declined entry. Residents receive advance notice 
of scheduled visual assessment inspections, and where access is not 
obtained, NYCHA typically conducts follow-up visits and documents 
multiple attempts to complete the inspection. NYCHA also reports it 
has attempted visual assessments for 100% of common areas for 
2025. NYCHA was able to complete 98.2% of the visual assessments, 
and 1.8% were unable to be completed because of lack of building 
access, typically due to temporary access constraints, e.g., at certain 

142 
 
142 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
locations where the building or structural layout is such that common 
areas that can only be accessed through resident homes.168  
NYCHA 
conducts 
paint 
remediation 
stemming 
from 
deficiencies identified through visual assessments and XRF testing. 
As previously reported, NYCHA has a backlog of paint remediation 
work orders in apartments and common areas that has accumulated 
since 2018. 169 In December 2024, NYCHA identified 19,835 work 
orders from this backlog that it is endeavoring to complete by the end 
of Year 7.170 As of January 31, 2026, NYCHA has closed 9,412 (47.5%) 
of these work orders.171 NYCHA has additionally cancelled 610 (3.1%) 
of work orders in the relevant universe.172  
Of the remaining 9,813 open work orders, NYCHA has 
attempted to address 99% of these work orders. 181 have not been 
attempted at all, and NYCHA has unsuccessfully attempted 2,481 
work orders once and 7,151 two or more times.  
 
The Monitors will continue to support NYCHA as it endeavors 
to clear its backlog of remediation work orders and increase the pace 
at which it completes paint remediation work orders.

SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
I.6 
Inspections 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to (1) comply with 
HUD’s physical condition standards, (2) annually self-inspect its 
apartments, (3) complete timely, industry standard repairs, and (4) 
prevent deceptive practices with respect to HUD’s inspections. The 
section below provides an update on NYCHA’s progress with respect 
to outstanding inspection-related obligations covering the year 
ended December 31, 2025, unless otherwise specified below.   
A. 
HUD’s Guidance and Standards (Requirement Nos. I5 and 
I7) 
1. 
NSPIRE Inspections 
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA is required to maintain 
housing that is “functionally adequate, operable, and free of health 
and safety hazards” and make all repairs in accordance with HUD’s 
physical condition standards, NSPIRE. 175  HUD regularly conducts 
inspections of NYCHA’s developments to determine NYCHA’s 
compliance with the NSPIRE standards.176 NSPIRE inspections include 
a random sampling of units and interior and exterior common areas.  
Health and safety deficiencies identified are categorized as 
“life-threatening,” “severe,” “moderate,” or “low.” “Life-threatening” 
and “severe” are weighted more heavily and result in higher point 
deductions. To receive a passing score, a development must receive 
an overall score of 60 or greater and have fewer than 30 points 
deducted for in-unit deficiencies.177 If 30 or more points are deducted 
for such in-unit deficiencies, but the development otherwise scores a 
passing grade of 60 or above, that score will be adjusted down to 59, 
and the development will receive a failing grade. 
Between January 1, 2025, and December 31, 2025, NYCHA 
has received NSPIRE results for 89 inspected developments. 178 Of 
these: 
• 
64 developments ultimately received passing scores, 
and 
• 
25 developments ultimately received failing scores. 
Inspections Calendar 
Year (2025): Section 1.6 
of the Monitors’ April 
2026 Report details 
NYCHA’s efforts to 
comply with its 
inspection-related 
obligations for the 
period covering January 
1, 2025, through 
December 31, 2025. 

152 
 
152 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
For all 2025 inspections, NYCHA has received passing scores 
for 71.9% of 89 NSPIRE inspections. This is a significant improvement 
from 46% of 138 inspections in 2024.   
2. 
Common Deficiencies 
In 2025, NYCHA reported that HUD’s inspectors most routinely 
identified the deficiencies illustrated in the graph below: 
 
As shown above, close to 18% of the points lost during NSPIRE 
inspections in 2025 resulted from electrical deficiencies in 
apartments such as damaged or missing electrical conductors, outlet 
covers, and switches. In 2024, electrical deficiencies also represented 
the largest category of points lost (30%). These deficiencies primarily 
fall under the “life-threatening” and “severe” deficiency categories, 
significantly impacting overall property inspection scores. The 
electrical deficiencies are both significant and simple to repair. The 
Monitors have emphasized to NYCHA the importance of addressing 
these deficiencies during every annual self-inspection both for 
resident safety and to improve NYCHA’s NSPIRE results. In 2026, 
NYCHA included training on improving electrical deficiencies as part 
of the Maintenance Muster program designed to train maintenance 
workers.  
The second most common deficiencies were egress violations. 
This represented 15% of all points lost in 2025, as compared to 12% 

153 
 
 
153 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
in 2024. These violations typically involve resident-installed-locks or 
deadbolts on doors that could prevent residents from exiting quickly 
in case of fire or other emergencies. NYCHA is taking steps to address 
egress violations in its self-inspections. While blocked egress has 
always been a deficiency under UPCS and NSPIRE, NYCHA did not 
include resident-caused blocked egress in its Maximo inspection 
template used for self-inspections until recently. Starting in June 
2025, NYCHA implemented an IT upgrade to add resident-caused 
egress questions to the handheld devices used by maintenance 
workers during the annual self-inspection. By prompting NYCHA’s 
employees to check for egress deficiencies in the self-inspection 
process, NYCHA should see improved scores in the NSPIRE 
inspections, and the Monitors will assess how NYCHA performs in 
future reporting.179  
Smoke alarms were also a common deficiency noted during 
NSPIRE inspections, accounting for 14% of all deficiencies identified. 
For resident safety, smoke alarms are required to be placed in every 
room of an apartment. While HUD inspectors identified missing 
smoke alarms as deficiencies in 2025, they did not result in points 
being deducted from NSPIRE scores. The Monitors emphasized to 
NYCHA that ensuring proper smoke alarms are in all rooms of NYCHA 
apartments should be a key focus of its annual self-inspections in 
2026.180  
3. 
60 Day Survey 
NYCHA is required to take specific steps to remediate possible 
issues with respect to units that fail NSPIRE inspections. Specifically, 
under the HUD regulations, NYCHA must conduct a survey of the 
entire development for any property that scored less than 60 on the 
NSPIRE inspection, including reinspecting all units, common areas, 
and exteriors for any deficiency.181 NYCHA has historically struggled 
to comply with this regulation within 60 days because of resource and 
capacity constraints, as well as the size of some of its developments. 
With HUD’s approval, NYCHA and the Monitors created a protocol 
for seeking an extension to the 60 day survey requirement, as a 
temporary measure until NYCHA improves its processes to allow for 
compliance with HUD guidelines. The protocol requires NYCHA to 
make a reasonable attempt to complete its survey and correction 
obligations. If NYCHA cannot complete the survey in time, it can 
request an extension, provided it complies with certain requirements.  

154 
 
154 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
In total, NYCHA requested extensions for 11 developments, 
which were granted on the condition: (1) that all open health and 
safety work orders be closed out within 30 days; (2) that NYCHA work 
with development staff to ensure any health and safety issues not 
reflected in work orders were being addressed; (3) that NYCHA work 
with development staff to ensure the quality of the follow up self-
inspections; and (4) to work with the Monitors to create an extension 
protocol to be finalized by the end of Q1 of 2026. 
The chart below 182  lists each property governed by an 
extension.  
 
The Monitors will continue to consider extensions on a case-
by-case basis provided HUD continues to agree and NYCHA 
demonstrates it will address health and safety issues during 
pendency of the extension.   
B. 
Annual Self-Inspections (Requirement No. I3) 
1. 
Inspections Completed 
Under HUD regulations (as incorporated into the HUD 
Agreement), NYCHA is required to inspect 100% of its apartments 
annually. 183  For the year ended December 31, 2025, NYCHA 

155 
 
 
155 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
completed inspections in 95% of occupied apartments for the year, 
as depicted in the chart below.  
 
The 2025 inspection rate of 95% represents an increase in 
NYCHA’s completed inspections from December 31, 2025, when 
NYCHA completed 91% within the same period in 2024. To achieve 
this increase, NYCHA expanded its vendor usage and prioritized 
completing more annual self-inspections earlier in the year. The 
earlier timeline gave NYCHA more time to return to apartments that 
it could not access on first attempt, as well as to avoid access issues 
at the end of the year during holidays. 
Throughout the year, the Monitors’ team conducted testing of 
the quality of NYCHA’s performance of its annual self-inspections and 
provided NYCHA with feedback on the results.  
In some cases, the Monitors’ testing identified improvements. 
For example, beginning in 2025, regulations required smoke 
detectors in each bedroom, outside each sleeping area, and on every 
level of the unit. The 2025 NSPIRE inspections identified many 
deficiencies related to smoke detectors, indicating that these had not 
been installed timely as part of the NYCHA self-inspections. However, 
in January 2026, the Monitors’ team accompanied NYCHA’s self-
inspections at six developments and noted a significant decrease in 
the number of missing smoke detectors.  

156 
 
156 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
In other cases, the Monitors’ testing identified gaps in NYCHA’s 
self-inspection process. As discussed in a previous report, the 
Monitors’ team used Farragut Houses as a case study to review the 
efficacy of NYCHA self-inspections. This was inspired by the 
particularly low initial score at Farragut Houses on NSPIRE inspection 
grades. 184 This review revealed several issues which should have 
been caught by NYCHA’s self-inspection, including missing or 
damaged smoke detectors and GFCI outlets.185  
Similarly, the Monitors are in the process of reviewing NYCHA’s 
self-inspections at Brevoort Houses. This review was prompted after 
Monitors’ mold team and NYCHA’s Office of Mold Assessment and 
Remediation (OMAR) identified a unit with significant leak issues 
where the residents had submitted numerous complaints over 
multiple years. Due to the severe conditions, the OCC recommended 
NYCHA relocate the residents, which NYCHA did in December 2025. 
NYCHA is in process of working to remediate these issues.  
The Monitors’ team reviewed NYCHA’s self-inspection records 
for the unit and found that these severe conditions were not identified 
in recent self-inspections. The Monitors’ team then compared the self-
inspection results at Brevoort Houses as a whole against verified mold 
complaints in the development. Since 2023, two units were identified 
at Brevoort Houses as having a mold or mildew issue in a self-
inspection. All other inspection results were otherwise marked as 
“satisfactory” or as “unsatisfactory,” although those marked as 
“unsatisfactory” were because of a defect that was not related to mold 
(such as an electrical issue). The Monitors and NYCHA are in the 
process of determining root cause and remediation going forward.  
2. 
Failures to Attempt Inspections 
In 2025, NYCHA did not attempt HUD-required self-
inspections on approximately 3% of apartments, down 2% from 2024, 
when NYCHA failed to attempt to inspect approximately 5% of 
apartments. NYCHA reported that two-thirds of the apartments that 
were not inspected were either move-outs or off rent-roll. 186 Since 
residents are not actively living in these apartments, the move-out or 
off rent roll inspections are less extensive than the HUD-required self-
inspections but still involve checking for issues such as active leaks or 
broken windows. NYCHA should continue to work to reduce and 
eliminate the number of apartments where no annual inspection is 
attempted.  

157 
 
 
157 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
3. 
Resident Communication 
a. 
Resident Engagement Plan 
In past years, NYCHA identified communication with residents 
was, at times, a barrier to access for inspections. In 2025, as part of 
NYCHA’s commitment to improving access, it created a new resident 
engagement plan to implement new communications strategies. The 
plan outlines all the communication methods to reach residents, 
including fliers, emails, calls, rent inserts, newsletters, text messages, 
an informational video, and social media. Additionally, NYCHA PMO 
and the Resident Services, Partnerships, & Initiatives (“RSPI”) 
Department created resident presentations, conducting three 
informational sessions, which highlight the importance of inspections 
and the need for access. The presentation also addresses steps that 
residents can take, such as keeping smoke detectors installed and not 
installing non-NYCHA locks on doors, to keep their apartments safe 
and ready for inspection. The Monitors have observed many of these 
communication strategies, including fliers, informational video, and 
resident presentations. NYCHA’s efforts have positively improved the 
channels of communication, and likely contributed to the increase in 
NYCHA’s overall self-inspection rate from 91% in 2025 to 95% in 
2026.  
b. 
Second Attempts 
Earlier this year, the Monitors’ team observed that some 
residents were not receiving notifications about the scheduled 
second annual self-inspection attempt, i.e., when NYCHA scheduled 
a time to conduct an inspection of a unit after it was unable to access 
the unit the first time. The Monitors recommended that NYCHA 
standardize the process across developments to inform residents 
about second attempts to access apartments. NYCHA updated their 
Annual Inspections Standard Procedures to include formal directives 
that second attempts should proceed in the same manner as the first 
attempts, including by placing notices under the doors of NYCHA 
residents 21 and 7 days before the next scheduled inspection. The 
Monitors will continue to assess whether this directive improves 
NYCHA’s ability to access apartments on second attempts. 
C. 
Repairs of Deficiencies (Requirement No. I4) 

158 
 
158 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA is required to complete 
minor repairs of deficiencies discovered during annual apartment 
inspections. As part of its process, NYCHA maintenance workers 
identify if a repair is required. If it is, it is either completed at the time 
of inspection or deferred. A repair may be deferred if, for example, 
the repair needs to be sequenced to skilled trades, would be too 
time-consuming, or the maintenance worker does not have the 
proper equipment available. A repair that is deferred will be 
scheduled for repair at a later date. 
1. 
Minor Repairs 
During 2025, NYCHA reported that maintenance workers 
completed minor repairs in 18% of all inspected apartments for the 
year to date, as illustrated in the graph below: 
 
In total, NYCHA reported that maintenance workers identified 
a total of 84,762 deficiencies that could be fixed with minor repairs 
during the annual self-inspections. Of those, NYCHA reported that 
the maintenance worker completing the inspection performed the 
minor repairs for 59,536 deficiencies and deferred 25,226. NYCHA 
reported that maintenance workers deferred minor repairs in 
approximately 9% of all inspected apartments through December 31, 
2025. As described in the Monitors’ previous reports and below, 
NYCHA is working to improve its documentation and oversight of 
minor repairs.  

159 
 
 
159 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
2. 
Documentation of Minor Repairs 
As a priority for 2025, NYCHA improved its documentation of 
minor repairs during annual apartment inspections. First, NYCHA 
automated the list of required minor repairs to pre-populate in the 
handheld device used by Maintenance Workers during the annual 
inspection. NYCHA completed this initiative and implemented it in 
October 2025. Second, in June 2025, NYCHA added additional 
egress related questions to its electronic tracking process to help 
maintenance workers identify, repair, and track these issues during 
the annual self-inspections since these issues were often noted on 
NSPIRE inspections in prior years.  
3. 
High Priority Repairs 
As described above, NYCHA also tracks the status of “high-
priority” subsequent repair requests that are identified during self-
inspections. These include work to address flooding or leaks, high 
severity pest infestations, inadequate heat, and other emergency 
conditions. For these repairs in 2025, NYCHA reported that it 
completed more than 94% of high-priority repairs within 24 hours, as 
illustrated in the graph below, a decrease in performance of 2% from 
2024. Per NYCHA, the decrease was attributable to the lack of 
availability of skilled trades workers to make certain repairs. 
 
As also illustrated above, NYCHA reported completing high-
priority requests in over 98% of instances by year-end. Of the high 
priority requests left incomplete by year-end, the majority require 

160 
 
160 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
NYCHA to fix damaged or missing combination smoke and carbon-
monoxide alarms. There are also a number of remaining repairs that 
require skilled trades such as electricians and carpenters. 
4. 
Maintenance Cares 
In 2025, NYCHA developed a new initiative to escalate critical 
repairs that present serious health and safety conditions, including 
loss of power, significant leaks, damaged ceilings, open walls, or 
excessive hoarding. The program helps maintenance workers 
immediately report repairs to a dedicated email address for 
escalation if the work orders involve these types of conditions. The 
program has been highlighted to developments through posters and 
postcards. It is also promoted at the maintenance musters, which are 
short morning meetings of maintenance workers at each 
development to discuss key topics impacting their work. The 
Maintenance Cares team ensures that when critical repairs are 
reported, they are prioritized and closed out timely. Neighborhood 
Administrators monitor each step of the process, including creating 
tickets as needed and engaging the appropriate stakeholders, such 
as maintenance workers or skilled trades workers. The team 
ultimately reviews photo evidence of the final repair before closing 
out the case to ensure a satisfactory resolution for the resident. 
NYCHA has closed out 650 priority work orders through this program 
since its inception in the first quarter of 2025. The Monitors will 
continue to support NYCHA in their efforts to formalize this program.  
D. 
Industry Standards (Requirement No. I6)  
1. 
QAU Review 
The HUD Agreement requires NYCHA to ensure all 
maintenance repairs are performed to established industry standards 
and workmanship.187 NYCHA complies with this obligation through 
two main methods.  
First, NYCHA leverages its Quality Assurance Unit (“QAU”) to 
monitor the quality of repairs. The QAU team conducts between 
6,000 to 9,000 corrective maintenance inspections annually. This 
amounts to about one half to one percent of total annual work orders 
completed at NYCHA. To maximize the impact of this sample size, 
QAU uses a risk-based sampling approach. This approach applies a 
bias to work orders with a higher degree of risk based on HUD 

161 
 
 
161 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
Agreement metrics. As a result, QAU’s samples are intended to 
capture a broader sample of work orders and ensure high risk work 
orders are not ignored. 
2. 
Supervisory Inspections 
Additionally, NYCHA requires the supervisors of skilled trades 
and maintenance workers to perform routine inspections of work 
orders closed by the employees they supervise. These supervisory 
inspections serve two roles. First, they provide an opportunity for 
supervisors to promote accountability and improve general work 
outcomes. Second, these additional inspections represent a critical 
quality assurance function. Because QAU is able to review only a small 
sample of total work orders, this work by the front-line supervisors is 
critical. 
NYCHA has had success in increasing compliance with skilled 
trade supervisory inspection requirements, which it tracks on its 
Inspection STAT dashboard. NYCHA completed 73.4% of the 
assigned inspections in December 2025, a significant improvement 
from 57% in December, 2024. As demonstrated in the chart below,188 
NYCHA has drastically improved their monthly percentage of skilled 
trade supervisory inspections completed compared to the previous 
year. NYCHA intends to continue to make improvements above its 
current level and has worked with IT to enhance the assignment 
process by development, with expected Maximo deployment by July 
2026. 

162 
 
162 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
  
However, NYCHA is not yet able to track the number of 
supervisory inspections done by the supervisors to maintenance 
workers. Maintenance worker supervisory inspections currently exist 
as an exclusively paper process, and there is no consistent, agency-
wide process for recording and reviewing these inspections. NYCHA 
has explained to the Monitors that at least some developments 
record these inspections on the paper inspection form itself. In the 
absence of aggregated completion data, it is unclear how many 
supervisory inspections are occurring and whether NYCHA is 
appropriately prioritizing this important check on maintenance 
workers’ work. This means that the vast majority of corrective 
maintenance work orders occur without a clear record that can be 
reviewed centrally. That is, unless the work order is within the small 
set reviewed by QAU, many of these work orders are likely 
unreviewed entirely.  
NYCHA has had delays in its process of improving its inability 
to track the amount of supervisory inspections done by maintenance 
workers. In 2025, NYCHA initially agreed to launch an IT project that 
would enable it to track maintenance supervisory inspections in 
NYCHA’s IT software, Maximo. NYCHA then paused the IT project 
because it believed maintenance worker supervisory inspections 
were duplicative of the risk-based sampling inspection of work orders 
that QAU was already doing. After additional discussion with the 
Monitor, NYCHA agreed to continue to pursue this priority.189 NYCHA 
has now completed the business requirements document to facilitate 

163 
 
 
163 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT 
an IT upgrade to track maintenance worker supervisory inspections 
data with estimated deployment in July 2026.  
E. 
Deceptive Practices (Requirement No. I7-I10) 
The HUD Agreement also requires NYCHA to prevent 
deceptive practices in connection with HUD’s inspections. More 
specifically, the Agreement requires NYCHA: (a) not to use deceptive 
practices with request to PHAS requirements (Requirement No. 60); 
(b) to design internal controls to prevent deceptive practices 
(Requirement No. 61); and (c) to require its Chief Compliance Officer 
to take a series of steps designed to prevent deceptive practices 
(Requirement No. 62(a)-(f)). The Monitors continuously assess 
NYCHA’s controls to prevent deceptive practices in connection with 
HUD’s inspections, and based on their review to date, NYCHA is in 
compliance or provisional compliance with these provisions. 
As a key control for quality inspections and to prevent 
deceptive practices, NYCHA requires development staff to complete 
NSPIRE Readiness Training. The training consists of a two-day 
classroom training and a half-day site-based training. NYCHA 
additionally offers an annual refresher training for staff. The training 
comprehensively reviews the NSPIRE standards, including what 
qualifies as life-threatening or severe deficiencies and how quickly 
these must be remediated. The training also discusses the prohibition 
against deceptive practices before or during a NSPIRE inspection, 
such as “performing work in units or common areas after a REAC-
NSPIRE inspection begins.” The training encourages reporting of any 
known deceptive practices and provides reporting options, including 
a hotline. 
In October 2025, NYCHA received a deceptive practice 
complaint. With the oversight of the Monitors and in consultation with 
SDNY and HUD, NYCHA’s Compliance Department investigated the 
allegations. The investigation determined that repairs were made the 
morning of the NSPIRE inspection in the common areas in a particular 
development. The repairs were completed before the inspection 
began. The repairs were deemed to be not deceptive because they 
were completed before the inspection began and were permanent, 
industry-standard fixes, not last-minute work intended to hide 
conditions. There were no repairs in the units selected for inspection. 
The Monitors assessed the process used to identify and investigate 
the complaint and found Compliance’s investigation to be 

164 
 
164 
SECTION I: PROPERTY MANAGEMENT  
considered and thorough. The Monitors are working with NYCHA to 
leverage the lessons from the investigation and formalize 
investigative 
procedures 
for 
any 
future 
deceptive 
practice 
allegations.

173 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
Under the HUD Agreement, NYCHA is required to make broad 
management and organizational reforms across the agency that will 
improve its overall functioning and its delivery of services to 
residents. As discussed in the Monitors’ prior reports, NYCHA’s plan 
for doing so is contained in its “Transformation Plan,” which lays out 
broad principles for improvement and core values for the 
organization—e.g., “creating a culture of service,” “strengthening 
NYCHA’s partnership with its residents,” and “using data to drive 
decision making”—as well as 47 specific initiatives intended to 
improve NYCHA’s processes and performance. Consistent with those 
broad principles, NYCHA also works with HUD, SDNY, and the 
Monitors to identify additional initiatives that will build on those 
specific projects in order to continue to improve itself as an 
organization. 
In this report, the Monitors provide updates on significant 
developments over the last year regarding NYCHA’s progress in 
achieving organizational change, followed by a table summarizing 
the status of NYCHA’s progress under each of the 47 initiatives under 
the Transformation Plan as of the end of Year 7 of the monitorship. 
A. 
Training 
A key goal under the Transformation Plan is to enhance 
NYCHA’s processes for training its personnel across the agency to 
better deliver resident services. This report focuses on two training 
improvements that have taken place over the last year: (1) enhanced 
training programs for NYCHA’s maintenance workers, including 
improvements to the “muster” program; and (2) the creation of a 
“Learning Leaders” training program, which expands NYCHA’s 
capacity to provide training to its employees on important topics such 
as customer service and conflict de-escalation. 
1. 
Maintenance Worker Training and Musters 
NYCHA’s maintenance workers provide critical services to 
NYCHA residents. They interact with residents daily, enter their 
homes to conduct inspections, assess what repair work is needed, 
make a variety of repairs themselves, and are often the first to 
respond to emergencies and other resident needs. To perform the 
job well, maintenance workers need consistent and high-quality 
training. 

174 
 
174 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
In the last year, NYCHA improved its maintenance worker 
training, with support from the Monitors. This included the 
development of a new “muster” training program for maintenance 
workers. Musters are daily meetings NYCHA hosts for property 
caretakers and maintenance workers that focus on a variety of 
consistent themes, such as customer service and accountability. As 
reported in the past, NYCHA rolled out the Caretaker Muster 
Overhaul Program, improving the training for caretakers, who 
maintain NYCHA grounds and conduct minor repairs as needed. 
Leveraging those successes, NYCHA launched, with the support of 
the Monitors, the Maintenance Worker Muster Overhaul Program, 
which focuses on strengthening daily maintenance operations, 
communication, accountability, and a culture of professionalism and 
continuous improvement. 
Maintenance Musters have a theme for each of the three 
weekly sessions: Maintenance Cares Tuesdays, Wrench & Reflect 
Wednesdays, and Tool Time Thursdays. Maintenance Cares 
Tuesdays began in January of 2026 and focuses on service, safety, 
and resident well-being through various customer service and de-
escalation techniques. Wrench & Reflect Wednesdays will begin in 
early 2027 and will focus on productivity, especially through process 
improvements informed by analysis of real-time worker productivity 
data. Tool Time Thursdays will begin in late Fall 2026 and will provide 
a hands-on training day to develop technical skills. For Wrench & 
Reflect Wednesdays and Tool Time Thursdays, NYCHA and the 
Monitors are developing robust training content that can cover the 
52 meetings per topic that occur each year. This ensures each muster 
meeting offers fresh information and will be delivered consistently 
across developments.  
In addition to the launch of Maintenance Cares Tuesdays, 
NYCHA established, with the support of the Monitors, the 
Maintenance Worker Recognition Program, which is incorporated 
into the meetings. The goal of this program is to highlight and elevate 
the essential contributions of NYCHA’s frontline workforce. In 
addition to celebrating high performers, this program serves to 
strengthen morale, reinforce organizational values, and promote a 
culture of pride in workmanship, responsibility, integrity, and 
exceptional service.  
The Monitor team is evaluating the performance of 
Maintenance Cares Tuesdays and Maintenance Worker Recognition 

175 
 
 
175 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
Program. To this end, the Monitor team will observe each program 
and distribute surveys to gather feedback. With the results of this 
evaluation, the Monitors and NYCHA will refine Maintenance Cares 
Tuesdays and provide recommendations regarding the rollout of 
Wrench & Reflect Wednesdays and Tool Time Thursdays. 
NYCHA also improved the training for new maintenance 
workers when they join NYCHA, including on issues like tracing the 
origin of leaks and fixing stoves, refrigerators, and electrical 
problems. NYCHA is also working to fix deficiencies with its 
onboarding process that cause delays in maintenance workers 
obtaining basic items that they need to perform their jobs, such as 
“handhelds” (the term for their work mobile phones), and uniforms 
for employees in training so that they can enter resident-occupied 
units. 
Finally, NYCHA is working to improve the organization of its 
L&D unit and physical infrastructure to provide trainings. NYCHA L&D 
re-organized its Maintenance Training Unit under new leadership by 
promoting a former maintenance trainer and hiring three new trainers 
from within NYCHA who have experience in the tasks and subject 
matters maintenance workers must understand to be effective. 
NYCHA continues to build the Bronx River training facility, a new 
facility which will allow for better training for common maintenance 
orders, such as window and door repair, in conditions that 
approximate on-site training. However, the facility’s opening is 
delayed from 2025 to the end of 2026. NYCHA explained that the 
delay was caused by delays in the permitting process, which have 
now been resolved, as well as cost saving measures, such as using in-
house resources as opposed to relying on vendors.  
2. 
Learning Leaders Program 
As part of its Transformation Plan, NYCHA committed to 
provide timely customer service and to institute a “customer service 
mindset” to standardize service to residents. Changing mindsets in a 
large organization like NYCHA takes time and consistent effort, and it 
is clear that more work is required, including with regard to training 
employees. One of the most frequent concerns that the Monitors hear 
from residents is the need to improve NYCHA’s customer service.  
Since the start of their term, the current Monitors have 
supported NYCHA’s efforts to improve the customer service 

176 
 
176 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
delivered to residents. During Year 7 of the monitorship, the Monitors 
worked with NYCHA to expand and improve its customer service 
training, which spurred the creation of the Learning Leaders program 
discussed below. A challenge that a large organization like NYCHA 
faces is the capacity to train a large number of employees with a 
comparatively small number of training staff. The Learning Leaders 
program aims to help build that training capacity. 
The Learning Leaders program trains NYCHA workers who 
already excel at their jobs to train other NYCHA workers. By 
leveraging its employees in this manner, NYCHA is able to deliver 
training to far more employees than if it relied solely on employees 
within its L&D department. Moreover, the training is expected to be 
more effective than other commonly used options for expanding 
training, such as hiring an outside vendor, because the trainers are 
employees who have practical, real-world knowledge of the 
challenges and opportunities other NYCHA employees in similar 
roles face. Importantly, the training delivered to employees, titled, 
“Be a Customer Service Superhero,” focuses on improving the 
customer service delivered to NYCHA residents. The goals, process, 
and initial results of the Learning Leaders program are discussed 
below.  
Goals. Beginning in January 2025, the Monitors collaborated 
with NYCHA L&D to create a team of internal NYCHA trainers made 
up of workers who are identified as excellent at their job and qualified 
to train others. NYCHA ultimately dubbed these internal trainers its 
“Learning Leaders.” The goals of the program were to provide 
NYCHA with the following benefits: 
• 
Increase capacity to provide training. 
• 
Improve each individual’s ability to do their job. 
• 
Improve team performance and accountability. 
• 
Improve service to residents. 
• 
Create opportunities for individuals to take courses. 
• 
Engage, motivate, and retain talent. 

177 
 
 
177 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
• 
Ensure that employees are trained as they are promoted 
and transition from one title to another title. 
Process. With these goals in mind, NYCHA and the Monitors 
designed a process whereby high performing NYCHA employees 
would receive training regarding how to train other employees and 
then deliver to other employees a newly reimagined course on how 
to deliver excellent customer service. 
1. 
Selection Process 
To become Learning Leaders, candidates had to pass a pre-
screening workshop and be in good standing, receive a supervisor 
recommendation, and complete three days of training. One hundred 
and forty-nine employees applied, 85 were screened, 57 were 
selected, 15 withdrew, and 42 graduated to become Learning 
Leaders. 
The first cohort of Learning Leaders represented a cross-
section of NYCHA employees: 10% from Leased Housing, 19% from 
Operations, 32% from Central Office, and 39% from Property 
Management. All boroughs were represented in the Property 
Management sector. 
2. 
Required Courses 
The coursework for the program was designed to equip 
trainers with the skills, knowledge, and methodologies needed to 
deliver training sessions and facilitate positive learning experiences. 
The main topics were: 
• 
Facilitation Techniques 
• 
Engagement 
• 
Retention, Relatability, and Real-World Applications for 
Training 
• 
Methods for Stress Management 
• 
Delivering an Effective Presentation 
After completing the two-day course, the Learning Leaders 
would be able to:  

178 
 
178 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
• 
Effectively 
deliver 
training 
while 
demonstrating 
preparation and professionalism. 
• 
Appreciate different learning styles and use varied 
instructional 
techniques 
to 
increase 
participant 
retention. 
• 
Engage participants through storytelling and real-world 
application of course content. 
• 
Communicate effectively with words, tone, and physical 
expression. 
• 
Apply course concepts to include, engage, and manage 
participants. 
As the Learning Leaders were being trained on how to train 
other NYCHA employees, NYCHA simultaneously worked with the 
Monitors to develop content for the customer service training course 
that the Learning Leaders would deliver. NYCHA developed a robust 
training course titled “Be a Customer Service Superhero.” In this one-
day course, Learning Leaders would teach the following topics:  
• 
Strategies for delivering memorable service; 
• 
Real-world practice in active listening and positive 
language; 
• 
Defusing hostile and difficult conversations; and 
• 
Owning the “customer service superhero” mindset: 
resilience, empathy, and ready for anything. 
After the course, NYCHA employees would be able to: 
• 
Improve the quality of interactions with internal 
customers (other NYCHA employees) and external 
customers (residents); 
• 
Define good and bad customer service; 
• 
Use words and actions that signal “I care, I understand, 
and you can trust me to take care of this”; and 

179 
 
 
179 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
• 
Use words and actions that de-escalate anger before it 
becomes aggression. 
After taking these two courses, the Learning Leaders 
participated in a peer evaluation group session where they presented 
the “Be a Customer Superhero” course and were critiqued by their 
peers and members of the L&D staff. As of November 2025, the initial 
group of 42 Learning Leaders were trained and ready to teach the 
“Be a Customer Superhero” class to hundreds of NYCHA employees 
over the following months. 
3. Initial Results 
The program has greatly expanded NYCHA’s training capacity 
for an area that is core to resident service. To date, 986 employees 
have taken the “Be a Customer Superhero” course, and NYCHA 
expects that approximately 1,200 individuals will have taken the 
course by June 2026. NYCHA noted at a recent Community Advisory 
Committee meeting that, through this expanded capacity, NYCHA’s 
goal is to train all employees agency-wide in the improved customer 
service course by the end of 2027. Moreover, the training and 
promotion of internal trainers, including coaching and feedback 
training, add broader benefits to NYCHA employees’ professional 
development and abilities. NYCHA employees reported benefits 
from their expanded interactions with other NYCHA workers with 
whom they do not usually interact. The Monitors plan to assess the 
extent to which this program is translating into on-the-ground 
improvements in NYCHA employee and resident interactions. And 
the Monitors will work with NYCHA L&D as it begins planning for a 
second cohort of trainers, and the expansion of this “Train-the-
Trainer” function to other training topics. 
B. 
IT System Overhaul Projects 
NYCHA undertook several major IT projects during Year 7, 
including efforts to improve the Resident Case Management and 
Human Capital Management systems (“RCMS” and “HCMS,” 
respectively) and to upgrade the Maximo Application Suite. NYCHA’s 
efforts to modernize its IT infrastructure are essential to its 
transformation and the improvement of its resident services, and the 
Monitors have provided updates on these projects in multiple prior 
reports. Despite significant time, effort, and expense, however, these 
projects continue to be delayed and have not yet been completed.  
Resident Case 
Management System 
(“RCMS”): An IT system 
that handles numerous 
functions involving 
resident services and 
communication under a 
single platform. For 
example, an RCMS 
would include 
application processing 
for potential residents, 
communications about 
inspections, and 
managing moveouts. 
Human Capital 
Management System 
(“HCMS”): A centralized 
IT system for all 
employee data that 
supports HR functions, 
including recruitment, 
onboarding, and 
workforce planning.  

180 
 
180 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
The Monitors have been evaluating how NYCHA has managed 
these IT projects, supported by a team of consultants with expertise 
in IT strategy and project management. Here, the Monitors report on 
their assessment of the RCMS project as a case study in NYCHA’s 
attempts to modernize IT infrastructure.   
Under the Transformation Plan, NYCHA committed to 
implement an RCMS. An RCMS is an IT system that handles numerous 
functions, including, for example, eligibility and application 
processing for potential residents; communications with current 
residents about leases, inspections, work orders, and rent; and the 
conclusion 
of 
tenancy, 
including 
move-outs 
and 
vacancy 
management. By bringing together these functions into a single 
system, a new RCMS would improve the efficiency of NYCHA’s 
operations and delivery of services to residents. 
Moreover, NYCHA needs to replace certain existing IT systems 
that provide components of what an RCMS would provide because 
they are past their useful life. For example, one component of 
NYCHA’s existing IT systems uses a more than forty-year-old legacy 
system which is difficult to adapt to modern workflows, has limited 
integration capabilities, and has major user interface constraints. 
Given the range and importance of the functions an RCMS serves, 
NYCHA identified several years ago that it needed to develop a more 
comprehensive, reliable, and integrated solution. 
Accordingly, NYCHA engaged in a procurement process via 
an open request for proposals (“RFP”) for the new RCMS system from 
June 2022 to January 2023, prior to the start of the current Monitors’ 
term. NYCHA received six submissions from prospective vendors as 
part of this process. Four submissions proposed a completely custom 
solution that was not typically used for public housing agencies, and 
the other two bidders proposed customizing a commercially 
available product used by other public housing agencies to meet 
NYCHA’s needs. 
In January 2023, NYCHA selected a vendor (the RCMS Vendor) 
that proposed a completely custom solution. NYCHA selected the 
RCMS Vendor for a variety of reasons, including cost and it scoring 
better than competitors across several metrics, such as technical 
implementation experience with similar projects. Under NYCHA’s 
agreement with the RCMS Vendor, the vendor was to provide several 
services, including project management, requirements validation, 

181 
 
 
181 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
design and implementation, and training, while NYCHA would 
provide NYCHA-specific housing expertise, requirements definition, 
and final testing approval, as well as historical knowledge of existing 
systems and data. This division of responsibilities is common in public 
sector system implementations: The vendor leads execution and 
technical delivery, and the agency provides business expertise, 
oversight, and formal approvals.  
1. 
Project Timeline 
The RCMS Vendor began work in June 2023, and NYCHA and 
the RCMS Vendor originally estimated that the project would be 
completed in April 2025. By July 2024, internal deadlines were not 
being met. In November 2024, NYCHA sent the RCMS Vendor a letter 
detailing its “poor performance,” and the parties met at length to 
develop a revised, agreed-upon schedule. After these meetings, 
NYCHA and the RCMS Vendor agreed to a revised schedule that 
included three releases with an estimated completion date of August 
2025 for the first release.   
Project delays continued and the delivery date for the first 
release was extended to January 2026. In April 2025, NYCHA 
engaged a platform vendor, Salesforce, to conduct a technical 
assessment of the work done to date. The assessment generally 
indicated that the product would be successfully implemented and 
launched if the vendor remained on a proper course of development, 
while flagging certain areas as requiring additional discussions with 
the vendor. Shortly thereafter, an important subcontractor that was 
critical in ensuring compliance with HUD regulations and notices, 
disengaged with the project, leaving the issue of compliance solely 
to NYCHA. Throughout the summer and fall of 2025, project 
milestones continued to slip. NYCHA and the RCMS Vendor 
exchanged letters accusing each other of causing the delay. The 
RCMS Vendor suspended its work in November of 2025, and NYCHA 
ultimately terminated the contract with the RCMS Vendor, based on 
its claim that the RCMS vendor had failed to perform its obligations 
under the contract.  
NYCHA engaged Salesforce for a second technical assessment 
which began in January 2026. It recently completed its assessment, 
which revealed significant limitations in usability, scalability, and data 
modeling, indicating that substantial architectural improvements and 
overhauls are necessary to meet NYCHA’s needs. The platform 

182 
 
182 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
vendor presented multiple options for process improvement and 
data strategy, which are currently under review. As of this date, the 
RCMS Project, an essential NYCHA transformation project that was 
originally projected for its first phase to be operational in April 2025, 
remains incomplete, without a projected launch date for the first 
phase. NYCHA is currently engaged with the underlying platform 
provider to formally evaluate the available options for resuming the 
RCMS project. 
2. 
The Monitors’ Assessment  
The Monitors conducted an assessment of NYCHA’s 
implementation and management of its RCMS project, as informed 
by the IT subject matter experts on the Monitors’ team. That 
assessment made the following observations regarding the project to 
date, and recommendations for improving NYCHA’s IT projects in the 
future. 
a. 
Vendor Selection 
Picking the right vendor is an important part of a successful IT 
implementation. Generally, NYCHA must select a vendor with 
consideration of cost, as well as other factors, such as proven 
experience delivering comparable implementations at similar scale 
and regulatory complexity, demonstrated public housing and HUD 
compliance expertise, established methodologies for enterprise data 
migration and integration, strong project governance and staffing 
stability, and a clear understanding of NYCHA’s operational 
environment. Because NYCHA is the largest public housing authority 
in the country by far, it faces a particular challenge identifying vendors 
who have public housing experience and the ability to deliver IT 
projects at the scale NYCHA requires. 
When NYCHA hired the RCMS Vendor, it was aware of this 
challenge and sought to address it. Specifically, it selected a vendor 
with experience with large IT projects but without significant public 
housing experience. NYCHA required the RCMS Vendor to hire a 
subcontractor with public housing experience, but the RCMS Vendor 
did not comply with this requirement. As a result, at times, sample 
work product prepared by the RCMS Vendor did not reflect HUD 
regulation compliance requirements or other specifications needed 
for a public housing authority. NYCHA and the RCMS Vendor 
participated in business requirements sessions, which were meetings 

183 
 
 
183 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
where NYCHA informed the RCMS Vendor of the requirements of a 
public housing authority. In the Monitors’ assessment, NYCHA should 
either select vendors with more public housing experience or take 
quicker action to enforce contractual provisions that require the 
vendor to acquire public housing expertise itself.  
b. 
Customization 
While a certain amount of customization of IT solutions is 
necessary for an agency of NYCHA’s size and complexity, IT experts 
recommend limiting customization, because less customization leads 
to a simpler and more effective process for changing IT systems. 
Indeed, NYCHA’s 2022 plan for its IT projects recommended 
purchasing applications “out-of-the-box” or “off the shelf” with limited 
customization, to align with the practices of other housing agencies 
across the country and make NYCHA’s IT systems more easily 
serviceable. 
However, the RCMS Vendor proposed a software solution that 
required substantial customization, not only to NYCHA’s unique 
specifications, but also customization to meet HUD regulatory 
requirements, such as requirements relating to rent collection. Other 
public housing providers use different software platforms that are 
designed to comply with those requirements by default and do not 
require such customization. NYCHA explained that it determined 
these other platforms not to have the capability to meet NYCHA’s 
size, operational demands and complexities. As it plans next steps to 
implement a new RCMS, NYCHA should consider how it could 
minimize customization to the greatest extent possible, particularly 
taking into account HUD requirements that are common across 
public housing agencies. 
c. 
Vendor Management 
The Monitors identified several ways in which NYCHA could 
improve its vendor management. While issues and disputes are a 
common aspect of implementing large projects with vendors, more 
active vendor management practices and improved communication 
can help to resolve them more quickly and with better results.  
NYCHA and the RCMS Vendor disputed the RCMS Project 
scope throughout the project. These scope disagreements have 
many causes, including the vendor’s lack of subject area expertise 

184 
 
184 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
discussed above and insufficient alignment at the outset of the 
project. For example, in NYCHA’s view, the contract required the 
RCMS Vendor to transfer seven years of NYCHA’s data from one 
location and additionally all the data pertaining to previous years 
from a separate location to the new system, but the RCMS Vendor 
refused to do this without additional compensation, because it 
claimed that the original contract only required limited data 
migration. Ultimately, better communication at the outset could help 
address and resolve potential disputes about what the project 
requires. 
There were other communication issues throughout the span 
of the project. As one example, the RCMS Vendor was unable to 
explain to NYCHA the ramifications of a technical design choice that 
would impact how users search the system. This caused a month’s 
delay as the parties turned to a third-party platform company to 
present the options. At times, the RCMS Vendor announced a delay 
without estimating the delay’s length or proposing ways to reduce the 
delay, requiring follow up from NYCHA and contributing to delays in 
decision-making.  
NYCHA should consider opportunities to adopt improved 
vendor performance measures earlier, including enforcing the 
contractual requirements of IT projects more proactively. For 
example, as discussed above, the RCMS Vendor did not engage a 
subcontractor with public housing experience, as required by its 
contract, which was only revealed when NYCHA requested the 
subcontractors begin joining meetings with NYCHA and the RCMS 
Vendor. NYCHA sent the RCMS Vendor a letter setting forth the 
performance deficiencies and formally requesting a corrective action 
plan in November 2024 and exchanged letters about the project with 
the RCMS Vendor again in fall 2025. However, earlier action 
potentially could have improved overall performance and better 
equipped the project to maintain its schedule. 
3. 
The Effects of the RCMS Delays 
Delays in the implementation of the new RCMS have had 
several negative impacts. First, as described above, the new RCMS 
was intended to improve a variety of ways in which NYCHA interacts 
with residents, and thus delays to improving that system also delay 
improving those communications. To its credit, NYCHA is examining 
ways to move forward on improving resident communications 

185 
 
 
185 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
independently while the RCMS project is delayed. For example, 
NYCHA has agreed to begin a pilot project aimed at expanding the 
use of text messaging in communicating with residents, and 
decoupling that program from the delayed RCMS implementation. 
Second, NYCHA has incurred additional costs, both monetary and 
organizational. As described above, NYCHA twice engaged 
Salesforce to conduct reviews of the project. The first engagement 
was to assess the architecture of the system being configured and 
built by the vendor, at no cost, and the second engagement was to 
assess the work completed in RCMS and outline a roadmap to a fully 
implemented RCMS solution, at a cost of $350,000. NYCHA also has 
devoted organizational resources to onboard, educate, evaluate, and 
ultimately exit the relationship with the RCMS Vendor. Third, NYCHA 
will need to undertake a second procurement process for a new 
vendor, which will result in further delay and may result in higher costs 
as market rates for IT projects have generally risen since 2023. 
Depending on the proposal NYCHA ultimately selects, there may also 
be additional costs in the form of changes to the RCMS solution itself, 
or wasted efforts toward the former solution. As discussed above, the 
initial assessment of the work performed by the RCMS Vendor 
suggests that NYCHA will need to incur new costs to develop a new 
solution.  
4. 
Common Issues in NYCHA IT Overhauls 
NYCHA’s RCMS implementation is not the only project to have 
experienced delays. NYCHA’s Human Capital Management System 
(“HCMS”) was scheduled to be operational in November 2024, but 
NYCHA now projects that will not happen until May 2026. NYCHA’s 
Maximo Application Suite (“MAS 9.0”) was scheduled to be 
operational in early 2026, but now is estimated for completion in June 
2026, because NYCHA discovered an issue that may have caused the 
software to be slow and unresponsive, and which required the 
procurement of additional services from Microsoft to resolve. 
Leadership within NYCHA’s IT department is currently 
changing. Former leadership has left the organization, and NYCHA is 
searching for new leadership. As part of the hiring process, the 
Monitors recommend, and NYCHA has agreed, to share these 
observations with prospective candidates to ensure they are 
prepared to address them beginning on day one and take action to 
complete these IT overhauls as quickly as possible. 
Maximo Application 
Suite (“MAS 9.0”): A 
planned upgrade of the 
Maximo IT system that 
NYCHA uses for its 
maintenance work 
orders. This system 
allows NYCHA to track 
data on development 
and unit repairs across 
the agency. It plays a 
central role in residents’ 
day-to-day housing 
experience by 
supporting the 
timeliness, coordination, 
and accountability of 
repair services.
