SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
C. 
NYCHA Human Resources Initiatives 
NYCHA and the Monitors collaborated on several initiatives 
during Year 7 to improve NYCHA’s Human Resources processes, 
particularly as they impact NYCHA personnel performance and 
accountability agency-wide. This Report discusses two of these 
initiatives: the development of a performance evaluation process and 
improvements to onboarding new employees. NYCHA has made 
strides to improve its HR processes, and the Monitors appreciate 
NYCHA HR’s collaborative approach. At the same time, NYCHA’s HR 
processes have important gaps that must be addressed. 
1. 
Performance Evaluations 
In the Transformation Plan, NYCHA committed to implement 
performance evaluations for its staff. Housing agencies across the 
country use performance evaluations to enable employees to 
improve their skills and productivity and to better deliver services to 
residents. By the end of the first five-year term of the monitorship, 
NYCHA had announced that it would implement performance 
evaluations for its managerial staff, which make up a small percentage 
of its overall workforce, but had not yet begun those evaluations. In 
Year 7, NYCHA completed its first performance evaluations for 
managerial employees and began to plan for a performance 
evaluation system for all of its employees across the agency. 
a. 
Performance 
Evaluations 
for 
Managerial 
Employees 
By mid-2025, NYCHA completed its inaugural managerial 
performance evaluation cycle for the 2024 calendar year. NYCHA’s 
managerial performance evaluation process applied to over 500 
employees within NYCHA, including a variety of administrative 
positions such as administrative housing superintendents and 
administrative housing managers; key neighborhood roles such as 
neighborhood administrators; NYCHA’s strategic planning roles, 
such as individuals within NYCHA S&I; and executive positions, such 
as the Executive VP of Property Management Operations. The 
Monitors commend NYCHA for successfully executing its first agency-
wide managerial evaluation cycle and achieving a participation rate 
of 97.6%. 

187 
 
 
187 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
Upon completion of the cycle, NYCHA initiated a survey for its 
managers that had participated in the evaluation process to gauge 
their experience and identify opportunities for improvement. There 
were 73 responses, and the evaluation process received a 7.1 out of 
10 score. This survey produced recommendations from staff such as 
decreasing the number of employee competencies evaluated, 
increasing training and guidance throughout the process, focusing 
goals on professional development, and allowing for more narrative 
responses within the evaluation form. 
In July 2025, the Monitors conducted their own review of the 
evaluation process and developed recommendations to guide future 
performance evaluation cycles. First, the Monitors recommended 
improving and simplifying the evaluation process to allow for clearer 
and more consistent feedback, including clarifying rating definitions 
with examples and reducing the ratings scale from five choices to 
four; and adding sample comments and comment rules as guidance 
for raters. Second, the Monitors recommended adding a secondary 
layer of review to evaluations to ensure fairness and proper 
documentation. Third, the Monitors recommended that there be 
more concrete, objective metrics tied to the employees’ job 
responsibilities worked into their review, including relevant Key 
Performance Indicators (“KPIs”). Finally, the Monitors recommended 
that additional steps be taken to connect the performance 
evaluations to future corrective action, including requiring action 
plans for low ratings and addressing gaps between self-evaluations 
and supervisors’ evaluations.  
NYCHA 
agreed 
to 
implement 
certain 
of 
these 
recommendations in the next round of managerial performance 
evaluations, which began in January 2026, including a four-point 
rating scale and the review of evaluations by next-level supervisors. 
NYCHA is developing KPIs, so these are not part of the current round 
of evaluations, but NYCHA plans to use KPIs in evaluations in 2027. 
Finally, NYCHA did not agree to implement improved training, which 
the Monitors had recommended particularly for individuals with low 
ratings. In response to a draft of this report, NYCHA stated that it did 
not need to implement this recommendation “as this type of follow-
up for performance-based issues is part of the more traditional 
progressive management of staff, which is already in place at 
NYCHA.” 
Providing 
training 
to 
low-performing 
staff 
is 
a 
commonsense measure to address performance issues, as it ensures 

188 
 
188 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
a common understanding of the requirements of good performance 
and equips staff with the tools to meet that standard. NYCHA has not 
yet shown that its current approach toward ensuring that low-
performers receive the necessary training is working, and the 
Monitors continue to contend that NYCHA should implement training 
specifically for employees who receive poor evaluations. 
b. 
Performance Evaluations for Nonmanagerial 
Employees 
NYCHA plans to implement performance evaluations for 
nonmanagerial employees, but this has moved forward more slowly 
than managerial performance evaluations. Despite committing to 
negotiate performance evaluations for nonmanagerial staff with the 
local union as part of its February 2023 implementation plan for the 
Transformation Plan, the Monitors found that NYCHA had done little 
to advance its implementation or even planning towards this goal by 
the start of their term. However, with support and urging from the 
Monitors, NYCHA committed in early 2025 to developing a pilot 
program 
for 
nonmanagerial 
performance 
evaluations 
in 
collaboration with leadership for the Union representing much of the 
NYCHA Property Management workforce. The Monitors supported 
NYCHA in establishing a working group with the Union on 
nonmanagerial performance evaluations.  
Through this working group, NYCHA, the Union, and the 
Monitor have developed a pilot program for nonmanagerial 
performance evaluations. The Monitor team provided a framework to 
include the Union and NYCHA staff in the process of developing 
these evaluations, and trained NYCHA and the Union on effective 
evaluations. The pilot program will involve evaluations that are tied to 
the employee’s job description, and NYCHA and the Monitor team 
will train participants in giving and receiving feedback. The central 
focus of the program is to establish evaluations that enable NYCHA 
to create a system of professional development, including providing 
corrective training to individuals identified as performing below 
expectations. 
The working group will soon discuss a detailed presentation 
regarding the pilot program, and the Union will provide feedback on 
the program. Following this feedback session, the working group will 
use the job descriptions and materials from the managerial 
evaluations to develop the nonmanagerial evaluations. Then the 

189 
 
 
189 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
working group will create the specific calendar for the pilot program, 
including training and other implementation steps. The Monitors 
commend the Union’s partnership during this process, and the 
Monitors will continue to support NYCHA and the Union in 
collaboratively working on this important program, which is expected 
to begin in the first quarter of 2026. 
2. 
Onboarding 
Onboarding is the process by which new employees learn 
about NYCHA and their role within the organization. Through 
collaboration with NYCHA’s Chief Administrative Officer, the Monitor 
team documented NYCHA’s current onboarding process to identify 
gaps and areas for improvement.  
The Monitor team found that the current onboarding process 
is not formally documented and no standard operating procedures 
exist. Instead, NYCHA’s onboarding process today depends on the 
undocumented institutional knowledge of individual employees and 
“manual” or ad hoc information distribution from one employee to 
another. The Monitor team also found that NYCHA’s onboarding 
process is fragmented across several departments, including HR, IT, 
General Services, Safety & Security, Learning & Development, and 
hiring managers. This distribution of responsibility introduces delays 
and the possibility of miscommunications, as well as a lack of clarity 
about who owns an employee’s onboarding process. Furthermore, 
even if process ownership was clear, the distribution of responsibility 
makes it challenging to supervise the entire process. Finally, the 
Monitor team found that fostering greater cross-departmental 
collaboration and communication within NYCHA would further help 
address some of these challenges. 
The Monitors surveyed new hires and hiring managers with the 
goal of gathering information from those most directly affected by the 
onboarding 
process. 
New 
hires 
identified 
problems 
with 
communicating with HR and getting timely access to tools and 
resources. New hires also expressed a desire for more structured 
training and better access to training materials that do exist. Last, new 
hires indicated that shadowing opportunities and clearer job 
expectations would also improve their onboarding experience. 
Hiring managers expressed similar needs. In particular, hiring 
managers said that HR struggled to communicate in a timely fashion, 

190 
 
190 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
and new employees needed immediate access to tools, systems, and 
training upon starting their roles. Hiring managers suggested that HR, 
IT, and hiring managers needed to coordinate better toward this end. 
Beyond these needs, hiring managers said that NYCHA departments 
might benefit from onboarding experiences customized by 
department. Finally, hiring managers stated that onboarding 
checklists would be a useful tool for tracking progress. 
In Year 8, the Monitors will pivot to the next phase of the 
project: designing a new onboarding process in collaboration with 
NYCHA HR. NYCHA and the Monitors have already begun this work. 
For example, NYCHA ran a pilot program for the issuance of 
handheld devices to maintenance workers as part of their training. 
This pilot program addressed a longstanding issue in which new 
maintenance workers were not given their own handheld devices 
(used, among other things, to manage work orders for resident 
repairs) prior to and even after being trained on how to use those 
devices. The pilot program has successfully led to the early 
distribution of those handheld devices thus far. NYCHA stakeholders 
including the CAO, IT, HR, and Learning & Development are working 
to build on this pilot, and the Monitors are providing support. 
D. 
A&CM Transformation Plan Overview 
In 2022, NYCHA established the Asset & Capital Management 
Division (“A&CM”) to restructure, scale, and improve the effectiveness 
and efficiency of the agency’s capital projects, real estate 
development, and comprehensive modernization functions. As part 
of that effort, A&CM launched a multi-year program intended to 
transform organizational culture and values, policies, processes and 
systems, and staff capabilities. According to A&CM, execution of 
these changes has allowed the division to accelerate and complete 
construction on over 500 capital projects in the last four years, 
expending over $4 billion in capital funds. Below, the Monitors 
summarize what A&CM has reported regarding its progress to date 
and the expected impact of these changes. The Monitors are 
continuing to track NYCHA’s progress towards implementing these 
improvements.   
A&CM reports that its change initiatives have been focused on 
several key areas: 

191 
 
 
191 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
• 
building a customer-service oriented, delivery-focused, 
and collaborative culture;  
• 
strengthening transparency and quality of engagement 
with NYCHA Operations, resident leaders, and other 
stakeholders;  
• 
implementing strategic capital investment planning, 
agency-wide design standards, and project scoping 
and budgeting processes;  
• 
addressing major policy bottlenecks and streamlining 
partner agency coordination;  
• 
improving vendor diversity and quality, as well as 
management and oversight;  
• 
embedding data-driven, risk-based project, program, 
and performance management; and  
• 
launching 
expansive 
training 
and 
professional 
development programs.  
A&CM has reported several operational changes in pursuit of 
these goals.  
1. 
Agency-Wide and Interagency Coordination 
A&CM worked with the CEO’s Office to establish the Strategic 
Asset Management Committee (“SAM Committee”), a monthly 
agency-wide executive committee to strengthen coordination across 
divisions on major capital investment planning and asset 
management. According to A&CM, these meetings provide an 
opportunity to more transparently and systematically make decisions 
on major capital project investments and asset management. A&CM 
also reports that it has also implemented joint policy and process 
improvements with the New York City Office of Management and 
Budget (“OMB”), New York City Comptroller’s Office, and New York 
City Department of Buildings (“DOB”). According to A&CM, these 
efforts reduced City funding approval times by 48% for OMB and 76% 
for the Comptroller’s Office, and led to a reduction in DOB permit 
approval times. A&CM reports that these improvements support 

192 
 
192 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
stronger schedule management for capital projects and facilitate 
timely project delivery.  
In addition, A&CM has rolled out e-Comply, a new prevailing 
wage and Section 3 reporting and compliance validation system, to 
all vendors and has redesigned its vendor evaluation system to better 
track vendor performance across all A&CM projects. According to 
A&CM, these reporting systems provide improved information on 
vendor compliance and performance.  
2. 
Project Delivery 
A&CM has updated its project delivery processes to improve 
on-time completion and minimize delays. This includes revamping 
project schedule management through new schedule templates, 
policies, processes, controls, and training; and expanding use of 
alternative project delivery models (including the Design-Build 
model) that allow A&CM to select vendors based on best value rather 
than lowest cost, and realize time and cost savings, including for the 
largest and most complex capital projects. A&CM has also 
established dedicated teams to strengthen oversight of construction 
management consultants and to rapidly respond to capital project 
emergencies, such as structural or façade issues. According to 
A&CM, these organizational changes allow for more efficient project 
management and greater responsiveness to the agency’s most 
pressing capital needs. A&CM has also built out an extensive staff 
training 
program—including 
onboarding, 
construction 
project 
management, and other technical and soft skills offerings—to enhance 
staff members’ ability to effectively execute their work.  
3. 
Data and Technology 
A&CM has made several improvements to its data and 
technology systems that are intended to improve on-time delivery of 
projects. The division has refined over 60 existing workflows in 
eBuilder, its construction project management system, and has 
introduced over 20 new workflows to better standardize and 
automate project management processes and controls. A&CM has 
also integrated dashboards and key performance indicators into day-
to-day management and decision-making at the project, team, and 
portfolio levels. According to A&CM, these tools help ensure that staff 
and managers utilize data to operate more effectively and make 
better decisions.  

193 
 
 
193 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
4. 
Planned Initiatives 
Looking ahead, A&CM expects to continue working on change 
initiatives started last year, including completing the roll-out of 
consultant onboarding and oversight resources, new interagency 
coordination and escalation processes, an updated capital projects 
procedure manual and several topic-specific manuals, A&CM’s 
program-level 
risk 
management 
approach, 
project-specific 
stakeholder surveys, asset handover process improvements, and 
enhanced environmental management processes.  
New initiatives will be focused on scaling capacity for the 
delivery of major new capital programs—including heat pumps for 
space heating and domestic hot water and waste plumbing 
rehabilitation—as well as identifying opportunities to streamline 
procurement processes and to strengthen project cost management. 
A&CM expects that these efforts will support its goal of completing 
construction on 130 projects, expending over $1 billion in capital 
funds, and scaling new capital and sustainability innovation programs 
in 2026. 
Additionally, the Monitors have begun two projects with 
NYCHA’s cooperation to help improve how A&CM plans and delivers 
capital work. In the first, the Monitors are examining why capital 
projects experience delays and what changes NYCHA can make to 
complete them more quickly. That review focuses on three areas that 
A&CM has identified as contributors to the delays: how NYCHA vets 
and approves vendors, how schedule management responsibilities 
are divided among A&CM staff and contractors, and how A&CM 
procures and oversees performance of program management 
services consultants who are tasked with managing certain capital 
programs for NYCHA. In the second, the Monitors have launched a 
pilot project to explore how NYCHA can incorporate “whole life cost” 
analyses into decisions about capital and operating investments. A 
whole life cost analysis considers the full, long-term cost of 
maintaining a building system over its lifetime, including the upfront 
price as well as the ongoing maintenance, operational, and 
replacement costs, so that investments options can be evaluated 
based on total cost of ownership. By taking these factors into account, 
NYCHA can most effectively utilize the funds it has available to it now 
and which it anticipates having in the future. The findings from this 
pilot are intended to inform decision-making by the SAM Committee, 
where NYCHA assesses the organizational impact of capital 

194 
 
194 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
investments and makes decisions on major capital investment 
planning and asset management. The pilot will focus on plumbing 
systems and heat distribution systems. The Monitors will report on the 
findings and recommendations from both projects in future 
reporting. 
*  *  * 
Several of the initiatives described above correspond to 
specific Transformation Plan milestones, which are tracked in the 
Asset and Capital Management category (initiatives 3.1 through 3.11) 
in the table below. 
E. 
Transformation Plan Chart 
In addition to the initiatives described above, NYCHA also 
committed to other efforts to reform itself in its Transformation Plan. 
The table below describes NYCHA’s progress toward completing the 
47 initiatives that NYCHA proposed in the Transformation Plan. 
Consistent with how NYCHA groups the initiatives, the table 
categorizes each initiative into five groups: Strategy and Innovation 
Initiatives; Administrative Initiatives; Asset and Capital Management; 
Information Technology; and Procurement. The table includes 
language derived from the milestones as defined in the 
Transformation Plan, followed by NYCHA’s own report on the status 
of each project and comments from the Monitors. The Monitors are 
continuing to assess both NYCHA’s progress in completing these 
projects and the impact of these projects on NYCHA’s organizational 
transformation.  
At the conclusion of Year 6, the Monitor reported that of the 
337 “milestone activities” that NYCHA identified as necessary for 
completing these 47 projects, 80% were completed according to 
NYCHA. By the end of Year 7, NYCHA reports that it has completed 
81.5% of its milestone activities. While NYCHA’s attempt to quantify 
its progress gives some sense of what has been accomplished, there 
are three reasons it might not give an accurate picture of how much 
NYCHA has done and how much remains to be done to realize the 
Transformation Plan’s goals. First, NYCHA’s reports measure 
progress by the total number of completed milestones. While each 
incremental milestone does indicate progress, not all milestones 
involve similar amounts of work or similar challenges. Second, the 
Monitors are still in the process of verifying the completion status of 

195 
 
 
195 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE 
various milestones. In some cases, NYCHA and the Monitors may 
disagree as to the meanings of key terms and operative phrases in 
the milestone as described in the Transformation Plan. Some such 
cases will be noted in the Monitors’ comments below, while others 
may not yet have been identified. Third, the table below should not 
be taken as an exhaustive list of work remaining to complete NYCHA’s 
transformation. The framework provided by the Transformation Plan 
could not have foreseen every organizational reform that would be 
needed to achieve the Plan’s goals, and the plan must therefore be 
supplemented and refined as it is implemented. Thus, completed 
milestones will not always reflect the end of work in an area. As an 
organization that delivers services to NYCHA residents on a daily 
basis, NYCHA cannot implement reforms and consider them 
completed for the future. Rather, work is required to sustain those 
reforms through time. 
The following chart notes each of NYCHA’s Transformation 
Plan initiatives, the status reported by NYCHA for the initiative, and 
the Monitor’s assessment. With respect to NYCHA, once it determines 
that it has completed a project by completing all of the specific tasks 
or “milestones” associated with the project, it formally “closes out” the 
project in a presentation to the Monitor. While this close-out means 
that NYCHA believes it has completed the necessary tasks for this 
project, the Monitors will continue to assess the project’s impact and 
how NYCHA is performing in the area. In the right-most column, the 
Monitors assess NYCHA’s progress into one of the following:  
Complete: NYCHA has completed all milestones associated 
with this initiative and accomplished the underlying goals of the 
initiative. 
Additional Monitoring Required: NYCHA reports that it has 
completed all milestones associated with the initiative, but the 
Monitors continue to assess the extent of completion and whether the 
underlying goals have been met. 
More Work Required: NYCHA acknowledges that additional 
milestones or other work is necessary for this initiative, or NYCHA 
reports that it has completed its milestones, but the Monitors view 
those efforts as incomplete. 
Cancelled/Deferred: NYCHA has not completed the initiative 
because it cancelled or deferred the initiative, such as because the 

196 
 
196 
SECTION II: ORGANIZATIONAL CHANGE  
initiative no longer served NYCHA’s underlying goals or due to 
limited resources. 
Across all four categories, the Monitors detail the basis for their 
assessment.

(“ITSM”) Process 
Standardization –

Technology 
Infrastructure Library 
(ITIL®) framework for IT 
Service Management 
(ITSM).  
Complete; 
project

More Work Required.  
NYCHA IT has made 
meaningful progress 
establishing an IT 
Service Management 
(“ITSM”) foundation 
through adoption of 
the ITIL® framework 
and deployment of the 
ServiceNow platform.  
 
However, ITSM maturity 
remains limited. 
Inconsistent ticket 
categorization, 
premature closures, 
redundant workflows, 
and unreliable data 
continue to skew 
performance indicators 
and hinder accurate 
assessment of 
workload, service 
quality, and resource 
needs.  
 
ITSM has a central role 
in managing IT 
operations and 
enabling enterprise 
transparency. NYCHA 
should prioritize 
strengthening 
governance and 
establishing clear 
ServiceNow ownership 
early in Year 8 to 
ensure operational 
reliability, 
accountability, and 
data-driven oversight 
across IT services.

SECTION III: THE MONITORS’ BUDGET AND STAKEHOLDER ENGAGEMENT 
This section describes the Monitors’ budget for work on the 
monitorship and summarizes the Monitors’ engagement with 
residents and other stakeholders since the Monitors’ December 2025 
Report. 
A. 
Monitorship Budget 
The HUD Agreement requires the Monitors to prepare an 
annual budget for the monitorship, including staff, expert consultants, 
or other third-party contractors engaged by the Monitors. HUD, SDNY, 
and the City approved the Monitors’ budget for Year 7, and the City 
is required to pay the costs of the monitorship as set out in the 
approved budget. This subsection describes the Monitors’ approved 
budget for Year 7, including how it was allocated for various areas of 
work and how it was allocated among the Monitors’ team.  
The monitorship is comprised of nine workstreams focusing on 
NYCHA’s obligations under the HUD Agreement related to heat, 
elevators, pests and waste, lead, mold, inspections, capital projects, 
and organizational change, as well as the Monitors’ stakeholder 
engagement work. For the period covering February 28, 2025, to 
February 27, 2026, the total approved budget for the monitorship was 
$19,902,453.90. Those funds were allocated among the various 
monitorship areas, as follows:194 
• 
Heat: This component of the monitorship focuses on 
NYCHA’s compliance with its heat-related obligations 
under the HUD Agreement. The Year 7 budget allocated 
$1,818,797.50 to this work.  
• 
Elevators: This component of the monitorship focuses 
on NYCHA’s compliance with its elevator-related 
obligations under the HUD Agreement. The Year 7 
budget allocated $2,007,857.50 to this work. 
• 
Pests and Waste: This component of the monitorship 
focuses on NYCHA’s compliance with its pests and waste 
management obligations under the HUD Agreement. 
The Year 7 budget allocated $2,175,686.50 to this work.  
 

232 
 
232 
SECTION III: THE MONITORS’ BUDGET AND STAKEHOLDER ENGAGEMENT 
232 
• 
Lead-Based Paint: This component of the monitorship 
focuses on NYCHA’s compliance with its lead-based 
paint-related obligations under the HUD Agreement. 
The Year 7 budget allocated $1,909,401.50 to this work.  
• 
Mold: This component of the monitorship focuses on 
NYCHA’s compliance with its mold-related obligations 
under the HUD Agreement. The Year 7 budget allocated 
$2,181,308.50 to this work.  
• 
Inspections: This component of the monitorship 
focuses on NYCHA’s compliance with its inspection-
related obligations. The Year 7 budget allocated 
$1,663,542.70 to this work.  
• 
Capital Projects: This component of the monitorship 
focuses on NYCHA’s compliance with its obligation to 
provide systemic and long-lasting improvements to 
residents’ quality of life through capital projects, 
including replacing outdated heating systems, elevators, 
and plumbing infrastructure, installing measures to 
combat 
pest 
infestations, 
and 
improving 
waste 
management systems. The Year 7 budget allocated 
$2,296,746.60 to this work.  
• 
Organizational Change: This component of the 
monitorship focuses on NYCHA’s compliance with its 
obligations to change its management, organizational, 
and workforce structure in a manner that will ensure 
sustained compliance with the HUD Agreement’s other 
requirements. 
The 
Year 
7 
budget 
allocated 
$4,904,782.50 to this work.  
• 
Stakeholder Engagement: This component of the 
monitorship 
focuses 
on 
communication 
and 
engagement with residents and external stakeholders, 
including members of the public, SDNY, HUD, the City, 
elected and public ofﬁcials, as well as the means 
through which residents and stakeholders can report 
issues to the Monitors. The Year 7 budget allocated a 
$691,233 to stakeholder engagement work.

SECTION III: THE MONITORS’ BUDGET AND STAKEHOLDER ENGAGEMENT 
Additional detail on the work of each of these teams is 
contained in the preceding pages of this report, as well as the 
Monitors’ previous reports, available on the monitorship website. 
The budget for each workstream was allocated among the 
various members of the monitorship team. The monitorship is 
comprised of co-monitors Neil Barofsky and Matthew Cipolla, 
Jenner & Block LLP attorneys and staff, Turner & Townsend Inc. 
employees, Quadel employees, FTI Consulting, Inc. employees, and 
two individual consultants. The approved budget noted above was 
allocated between these groups as follows: 
• 
Jenner & Block LLP (“Jenner”): The Monitors are 
partners at Jenner, a law firm with offices in New York, 
other U.S. cities, and the United Kingdom. The Monitors 
and nine other Jenner partners lead the monitorship. 
The Monitors and all Jenner partners are contributing 
their time pro bono. Calculated based on hourly billing 
rates, the value of donated partner time was 
approximately $5 million. Jenner also has, as of the date 
of this report, 13 associates as well as one paralegal and 
one 
legal 
assistant, 
providing 
services 
to 
the 
monitorship. Under the terms of the Monitors’ 
agreement, the Monitors agreed to limit the amount 
billed to the City for associate and staff time to $6.3 
million. 
• 
Turner & Townsend Inc. (“T&T”): T&T is a global 
professional services company specializing in program 
management, 
project 
management, 
and 
cost 
management consulting within the property and real 
estate 
sector. 
The 
Year 
7 
budget 
allocated 
$8,658,221.20 for the work of T&T. T&T bills its fees on 
an hourly basis for the work of its staff, which as of the 
date of this report included 21 full-time employees and 
7 half-time employees. The budget also allocated 
$30,000 for T&T’s expenses, such as travel, and 
$105,000 for software licenses and data hosting fees.  
• 
Quadel Consulting & Training, LLC (“Quadel”): 
Quadel is an affordable housing consultancy trained 
and certified in HUD regulations, compliance, and 
housing authority operations. Quadel also specializes in

inspection standards, local building codes, lead 
remediation, and pest and waste management. The 
Year 7 budget allocated $4,043,470.00 for Quadel’s 
work. Quadel bills its fees on an hourly basis for the work 
of its staff, which as of the date of this report included 
the equivalent of 11 full-time employees, based on the 
total number of hours worked. The budget also 
allocated $194,000 for Quadel’s expenses, such as 
travel. 
• 
Individual Consultants: The monitorship also employs 
two individual consultants, who work closely with the 
monitorship team, and one of whom is a NYCHA 
resident. Both consultants support the Monitors’ 
stakeholder engagement efforts. One consultant also 
works with the various NYCHA departments responsible 
for providing services in the areas of heating system 
management, elevator services and maintenance, lead 
remediation, 
mold 
remediation, 
pest 
& 
waste 
management, and capital projects. The Year 7 budget 
allocated $250,000 for the work of these individuals, 
who bill on an hourly basis.  
• 
FTI Consulting, Inc (“FTI”): FTI is a business consulting 
firm. FTI’s Construction, Projects, & Assets, and Data & 
Analytics advisory practices assisted the monitorship by 
assessing NYCHA’s capital expenditure and project 
management processes. In Year 7 of the monitorship, 
FTI performed this work on a pro bono basis, for which 
the Monitors are extremely grateful. 
B. 
Stakeholder Engagement  
In Year 7, the Monitors have continued to engage with 
residents and other stakeholders, and the Monitors have 
incorporated those engagements into their work and priorities. 
Those activities have included but are not limited to: 
• 
Over 800 monitoring and inspection visits across 
NYCHA developments in all five boroughs, including 
building and grounds inspections, annual inspections, 
and mold inspections;

Sotomayor Houses in the Bronx;

Regular engagement with tenant association leaders,

government as well as members of the New York

Brooklyn, and Dyckman Houses in Manhattan; and

from residents through the Monitors’ email and phone 
lines. 
The Monitors appreciate the leadership of NYCHA residents, 
government officials, and community organizations to drive progress 
at NYCHA, and they welcome the opportunity for further 
engagement with all interested stakeholders.

distribution system, such as vertical risers and shared infrastructure, help buffer apartments from 
the immediate effects of equipment failures. As a result, according to NYCHA, even when a 
component of the end-to-end heating system requires servicing or is temporarily out of service, 
apartment temperatures may not fall below legal minimums for several hours. See id., at 20, 148

based on who manufactured them and if they have been subject to regular maintenance, with 
NYCHA noting that properly maintained steel boilers can last for over 50 years with proper 
welding. NYCHA will need to increase the pace of boiler replacements beyond what is required 
by the HUD Agreement to keep up with the rate at which its boilers are operating beyond their

27 Annual overhaul consists of preventive maintenance, inspections, and corrective maintenance 
on NYCHA’s central heating equipment. NYCHA aims to perform preventive maintenance and 
inspections each summer on all its central generation and distribution equipment, such as its 
 
 
 

242 
 
 
 
242 
ENDNOTES 
 
boilers and tank rooms. Through inspections, NYCHA also identifies equipment needing 
corrective maintenance, which it divides into three priority categories based on what is required 
to safely operate the heating system. Priority 1 comprises the highest priority issues that prevent 
equipment from being turned on for the upcoming season. NYCHA aims to complete 90% of its 
Priority 1 corrective maintenance work orders by October 1, and all of them by October 31. 
While NYCHA may not complete all maintenance by the start of the heating season, it tries to 
ensure that all developments are heat ready before October 1. Monitors’ Report, Dec. 19, 2025, 
at 16-17. 
28 NYCHA completed a higher percentage of the highest priority corrective maintenance work 
orders before the 2025-2026 heating season than it completed before the 2024-2025 heating 
season. Through inspections, NYCHA identified 65 Priority 1 corrective maintenance work 
orders during the 2025 annual overhaul. Of these, NYCHA completed 43, or 66%, by October 
1, 2025. As of October 31, 2025, NYCHA completed 63, or 97%, of Priority 1 corrective 
maintenance work orders. During the 2024 annual overhaul, NYCHA identified 114 Priority 1 
corrective maintenance work orders, completed 91, or 80%, by October 1, 2024, and 100, or 
88%, by October 31, 2024. Monitors’ Report, Dec. 19, 2025, at 17. NYCHA also completed 
preventive maintenance and inspections at a quicker pace and earlier in the summer during the 
2025 annual overhaul than during the 2024 annual overhaul, which reduced strain on NYCHA’s 
employees and allowed them to focus more on corrective maintenance work orders as the 
heating season approached. Id. at 17.

redundancies in its heating systems, NYCHA declared all developments were heat ready for

51 See Monitors’ Report, Sept. 30, 2025, at 41-44. Con Edison’s voltage reductions are one 
example of low voltage conditions that can result in an elevator service outage. Notably, these 
voltage reductions affect multiple buildings and/or developments at once, causing widespread 
outages. Sometimes, a single elevator car, a multi-elevator bank, or a handful of cars will go out 
because of a local electricity issue, like a blown transformer. Previously, NYCHA tracked elevator 
outages due to low voltage conditions inconsistently in its work order software, Maximo. 
Inconsistent tracking made it difficult for NYCHA to fully assess the impact of such outages on 
its assets and operations. Thus, in 2023, NYCHA standardized its data by adding a dedicated 
field in Maximo to flag and track low voltage outages. This change allows NYCHA and the 
Monitors to better understand the impact of low voltage outages, including those caused by 
Con Edison’s voltage reductions. However, NYCHA does not track the source of the low voltage 
condition, so the data does not differentiate between outages caused by Con Edison voltage 
reductions and those resulting from issues with NYCHA's own electrical infrastructure. 
52 When AVRs are installed in elevator motor rooms, they allow for elevators to continue to 
operate despite voltage reductions. In 2023, NYCHA installed AVRs in some developments to 
reduce the impact of voltage reductions, also known as “brownouts.” NYCHA Now, NYCHA 
Begins Installing Voltage Technology for Improved Elevator Service (Oct. 26, 2023), 
https://nychanow.nyc/nycha-begins-installing-voltage-technology-for-improved-elevator-
service/.

54 NYCHA shared that it cost between $60,000 and $90,000 to install each of the first 13 AVRs, 
and that prices have increased for the AVR units, materials, and labor costs. NYCHA also 
explained that it prioritizes one AVR unit per building regardless of the number of elevators 
servicing a building so at least one elevator can remain in service during low voltage conditions. 
The Monitors determined that low voltage outages impacted approximately 586 NYCHA 
buildings during Year 7. Escalating the average historical cost of installing an AVR to current 
prices, the Monitors estimate that installing one AVR would cost approximately $76,500, for a 
total cost of nearly $45 million to install for the 586 buildings impacted by low voltage outages 
in Year 7. 
55 There are fewer elevator banks with multiple elevators in this report as compared to the 
previous report. See Monitors’ Report, Dec. 19, 2025, at 37. When developments are transferred 
to a third-party through the PACT program, the elevators in those developments are no longer 
included in measuring performance for the HUD Agreement obligations. The same applies to 
other elevators, not just elevator banks with multiple elevators. Changes are applied 
retroactively, so the same elevators currently serviced by NYCHA are assessed across years. 
Accordingly, measures of performance indicated in previous reports may vary slightly based on 
the retroactive exclusion of these transferred elevators.

service. Id. ¶ 23(a). Beginning in Year 5, this requirement was superseded by the requirement 
that NYCHA ensure at least 85% of elevator banks with more than one elevator have no more 
than one instance per year where all elevators are out of service. Agreement, Ex. B ¶ 24(a).

159 As previously reported, NYCHA divided its XRF testing initiatives into two phases, with Phase

160 The total XRF testing universe consists of 147,184 apartments. At present, there are 2,425 
apartments that NYCHA is not required to XRF test at the 0.5mg/cm2 threshold (this includes 
apartments that are decommissioned, off rent roll or RAD/PACT apartments). RAD/PACT 
apartments are tested by the PACT partner. For those apartments that are currently off rent roll, 
they will only require an XRF test if the apartment goes back on the rent roll. Apartments 
classified as “off rent roll” are apartments approved as offline by HUD, but which may be used 
for another purpose such as for employee office space. NYCHA tracks these apartments but 
does not proactively attempt to test them.

turnover work.

192  While NYCHA does have policies and software-based controls in to place to prevent

instances where such work was nevertheless completed during an NSPIRE inspection. The 
Monitors understand that these work orders were previously scheduled for reasons 
unconnected to the NSPIRE inspections and were, based on the Compliance department’s 
investigation, neither scheduled nor completed during an inspection with the purpose of 
deceiving HUD about conditions at NYCHA properties. The Monitors plan to engage the parties

GLOSSARY

GLOSSARY

GLOSSARY 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

259 
 
 
 
GLOSSARY 
259 
The list of certain terms used throughout the 
Monitors’ report appear below. Each entry notes 
the section in which it appears.  
Annual Overhaul: Annual overhaul refers to 
NYCHA’s yearly preventive and corrective 
maintenance performed on heating service 
equipment 
starting 
around 
February 
and 
finishing by October, with most of the work 
taking place in the summer. The annual overhaul 
involves performing maintenance on all heating 
equipment (e.g., cleaning, lubricating, and 
replacing 
worn 
components), 
conducting 
thorough equipment inspections to identify all 
needed repairs and replacements, and then 
completing this work. (Heat) 
Apply: Under the definition agreed to by the 
First Monitor and NYCHA, this is the time from 
the moment a complaint is verified, to the 
moment that NYCHA staff completes the initial 
work order for inspection and initial treatment. 
Typically, the same NYCHA staff member who 
responds to the initial work order applies the 
initial pest control treatment during the same 
visit. Application of pest control methods should 
take, on average, 45 minutes. (Pests & Waste) 
Baez v. NYCHA, No. 13 Civ. 8915 (“Baez”): 
Baez is a federal class action lawsuit brought by 
NYCHA residents suffering from asthma and 
living in apartments with mold and excessive 
moisture. NYCHA settled Baez in 2014. The 
resulting consent decrees impose various 
requirements on NYCHA that are similar but not 
identical to the HUD Agreement obligations and 
aim to help NYCHA effectively remediate mold 
and moisture in a timely manner. (Mold & Leaks) 
Building 
Management 
Systems: 
The 
computerized controls that provide automation, 
remote monitoring, and remote control for 
building mechanical systems such as boiler 
plants. (Heat) 
Cause and Repair Codes: The standardized 
codes used in NYCHA's Maximo system to 
categorize the cause of a heat outage (cause 
code) and the work performed to resolve it 
(repair code). Accurate coding helps NYCHA 
identify patterns, target preventive maintenance, 
and track the effectiveness of repairs. (Heat) 
City Capital Action Plan (“CCAP”): Under the 
HUD Agreement, New York City must provide 
$2.2 billion in capital funding to NYCHA over a 
ten-year period starting in 2019 ($250 million per 
year for the final six years). These funds are 
committed to capital projects and can only be 
spent pursuant to an Action Plan. The CCAP was 
approved by the First Monitor on May 8, 2021, 
and was amended in 2026. (Pests & Waste) 
Consolidation: A group of developments 
managed by the same property management 
office.  (Pests & Waste) 
Corrective 
Maintenance: 
Corrective 
maintenance consists of repairing or replacing 
damaged or malfunctioning components such 
as burners, pumps, valves, control systems, and 
piping; performing hydrostatic tests to detect 
leaks; and restoring full operational capacity to 
ensure safe and reliable boiler function. (Heat) 
EH&S Findings: A finding is an issue identified 
by EH&S through its root cause failure 
investigations that requires NYCHA to take 
corrective action. For example, EH&S findings 
have led HMSD to create borough-specific 
storerooms in Manhattan and the Bronx for faster 
access to repair materials, and to improve 
staffing levels on days forecasted to have below-
average temperatures. (Heat) 
Elevated Blood Lead Level (“EBLL”): EBLL is a 
confirmed concentration of lead in the blood of 
a child under age six equal to or greater than 3.5 
micrograms per deciliter or higher. (Lead) 
Fixture Removal: Fixtures, including cabinetry, 
sheet rock, or floor tiles, that have been 
damaged by mold must be removed. (Mold & 
Leaks) 
Heat Ready: Heat ready refers to NYCHA’s 
assessment that the boilers at a development are 
capable of providing sufficient heat to the 
residents of the development in the upcoming 
heating season. Because many developments 
have boiler rooms with more than the necessary 
number of boilers, not all boilers must be 
operational in a development’s boiler room 
before NYCHA considers the development to be 
heat ready. (Heat) 

260 
 
 
260 
GLOSSARY 
Heating Degree Days (“HDDs): A measure of 
how cold it is over a period of time, calculated 
based on the number of degrees the daily 
average temperature falls below a set baseline 
(typically 65°F). Higher HDD values indicate 
greater demand for heat. (Heat) 
Heating Outage: A failure of the central heating 
system that prevents the system from delivering 
heat to multiple apartments. It does not include 
instances when heat is not being provided to an 
individual apartment, such as when a single 
radiator malfunctions. (Heat) 
Heating Season: The heating season runs from 
October 1 in one year to May 31 in the next year. 
This is the period when resident-occupied 
buildings in New York City are required to be 
provided with heat from a central heating 
system. (Heat) 
Heating Service Interruptions: This term 
consists of heating outages and in-apartment 
heating failures. (Heat) 
Heating System: The full set of equipment and 
infrastructure required to deliver heat to 
occupied apartments. This end-to-end system 
includes central generation equipment (such as 
boiler 
plants), 
distribution 
components 
(including tank rooms, pipes, risers, and valves), 
and in-apartment equipment (such as convectors 
and radiators). A failure or condition requiring 
servicing within these components can affect the 
delivery of heat to apartments. (Heat) 
Human 
Capital 
Management 
System 
(“HCMS”): A centralized IT system for all 
employee data that supports HR functions, 
including 
recruitment, 
onboarding, 
and 
workforce planning. (Organizational Change) 
In-Apartment Heating Failures: Conditions 
where the heating system inside an apartment 
does not function properly due to issues with in-
apartment components such as convectors, 
distribution pipes or related parts, like clogged 
traps, that require cleaning to restore proper 
heating. An in-apartment heating failure can 
impact a single room in the apartment or the 
entire apartment. (Heat) 
Independent Mold Analyst (“IMA”): The IMA is 
an entity appointed under the Baez Consent 
Decree 
to 
perform 
quality 
assurance 
by 
inspecting a certain number of randomly 
selected apartments on a quarterly basis. The 
IMA also reports on its findings and makes 
recommendations for improved compliance. 
(Mold & Leaks) 
Inspections Calendar Year (2025): Section 1.6 
of the Monitors’ April 2026 Report details 
NYCHA’s efforts to comply with its inspection-
related obligations for the period covering 
January 1, 2025, through December 31, 2025. 
(Inspections) 
Integrated Pest Management (“IPM”): An 
environmentally 
friendly, 
commonsense 
approach to pest control. Unlike traditional pest 
management, 
which 
involves 
the 
routine 
application of pesticide, IPM focuses on the 
prevention of pests and uses pesticide only as 
needed. It involves multiple forms of pest 
controls and has a four-tiered approach: (1) 
identify pests and monitor progress, (2) set 
action thresholds, (3) prevent pests, and (4) 
control pests. (Pests & Waste) 
Leak Standard Procedure:. A standardized 
process for identifying, tracing, and addressing 
the root causes of leaks across all NYCHA 
developments. (Mold & Leaks) 
Leaks from Above:. Floods, leaks, or water 
penetration into a resident's apartment from 
another apartment(s) above or adjacent to it, or 
the roof. (Mold & Leaks) 
Low 
Voltage 
Conditions: 
 
Low 
voltage 
conditions refer to when an elevator is not 
receiving enough voltage to continue operating. 
Many elevators (including NYCHA’s) cannot 
operate at lower voltages. Elevators need a 
steady supply of electricity to run safely. When 
the electrical voltage drops too low, the 
elevator’s motor does not have enough power to 
move the elevator car, and the safety systems 
automatically shut the elevator down to prevent 
damage or unsafe operation. Low voltage 
conditions can occur from widespread (e.g., a 
grid goes down) or local (e.g., a transformer 
blows) electrical issues. Low voltage conditions 
generally require an electrician to resolve the 
issue at the source of the problem. In responding 
to all outages, NYCHA mechanics will take 
voltage readings to confirm if the outage is due 
to low voltage conditions. (Elevators) 
Mandatory Minimum Temperature: From 
October 1 to May 31 (the “Heating Season”), 
during the day (6:00 a.m. to 10:00 p.m.), 
apartments must be at least 68°F when the 

261 
 
 
 
GLOSSARY 
261 
outside temperature is below 55°F, and at least 
62°F at night (10:00 p.m. to 6:00 a.m.) regardless 
of the outside temperature. (Heat) 
Maximo Application Suite (“MAS 9.0”): A 
planned upgrade of the Maximo IT system that 
NYCHA uses for its maintenance work orders. 
This system allows NYCHA to track data on 
development and unit repairs across the agency. 
It plays a central role in residents’ day-to-day 
housing 
experience 
by 
supporting 
the 
timeliness, coordination, and accountability of 
repair services. (Organizational Change) 
Modernization:  Modernizing elevators refers to 
replacing key equipment and electronics and 
refurbishing the elevator cabs. (Elevators) 
Mold Cleaning: To clean mold, NYCHA staff or 
a vendor apply a mold-specific cleaning 
detergent and disinfectant with as-needed 
manual mold removal with a brush. (Mold & 
Leaks) 
Mold Inspector: A mold inspector is a Property 
Maintenance Supervisor, Assistant Property 
Maintenance Supervisor, Property Manager, or 
Maintenance Worker who is trained and 
authorized to perform initial mold inspections. A 
mold inspector must complete the Mold 
Inspector and Building Sciences Trainings 
before performing such inspections. (Mold & 
Leaks) 
No-Service Condition: The HUD Agreement 
defines “no-service conditions” to include only a 
situation where all elevators are out of service at 
one building. However, NYCHA, HUD, and SDNY 
have agreed that it is consistent with the purpose 
of the Agreement for “no-service conditions” to 
also include a situation where all elevators that 
serve an apartment are out of service, which 
generally means all elevators in one bank are out 
of service. For example, a single building may 
have one bank of elevators that serves the 
northern half of the building, and a second bank 
of elevators that serves the southern half of the 
building. If all of the elevators that serve the 
northern half of the building stop working, that 
would be considered a no-service condition 
because the apartments on the northern half of 
the building cannot be accessed by elevator, 
even though other elevators in the building 
continue to function. (Elevators) 
Pest Standard Procedure (“Pest SP”): The Pest 
SP provides instructions to NYCHA staff on how 
to implement and comply with preventative and 
responsive measures to control pest infestations. 
It also creates protocols to protect the health of 
residents, 
employees, 
and 
vendors 
when 
removing and reducing pest activity using IPM 
techniques. (Pests & Waste) 
Pests: Under the HUD Agreement, the term 
“pests” refers to rats, mice, cockroaches, and bed 
bugs. (Pests & Waste) 
Preventive 
Maintenance: 
For 
heating, 
preventive maintenance consists of cleaning, 
lubricating, adjusting, repairing, and replacing 
worn components, and ensuring equipment and 
mechanical areas are in satisfactory operating 
condition. (Heat) 
Rat Slabs: Rat slabs are thin layers of concrete 
poured over areas of exposed sand and 
aggregate within basements and crawl spaces 
where rats burrow. They are intended to prevent 
rodents 
from 
establishing 
burrows 
inside 
buildings. (Pests & Waste) 
Remediate: 
Remediating 
mold 
means 
addressing 
mold 
and 
its 
root 
causes. 
Remediation might include repairing a section of 
leaky pipe and replacing the mold-covered wall 
containing the pipe. (Mold & Leaks) 
Remote Elevator Monitoring Systems:  REMS 
provide continuous real-time monitoring by 
tracking elevator performance through sensors 
that detect faults and generate fault codes. The 
local Elevator Motor Room (“EMR”) receives the 
fault codes in a fault log, then transmits the codes 
to either physical servers at the central office of 
NYCHA’s 
Elevator 
Service 
and 
Repair 
Department (ESRD) for older REMS devices 
(“server-based”), or to a cloud-based monitoring 
system for newer REMS devices (“cloud-based”), 
where the data can be accessed and used to 
coordinate responses, generate work orders, 
and monitor elevator performance offsite. 
(Elevators) 
Resident 
Case 
Management 
System 
(“RCMS”): An IT system that handles numerous 
functions 
involving 
resident 
services 
and 
communication under a single platform. For 
example, an RCMS would include application 

262 
 
 
262 
GLOSSARY 
processing 
for 
potential 
residents, 
communications 
about 
inspections, 
and 
managing moveouts. (Organizational Change) 
Section 3: NYCHA’s Section 3 Program requires 
NYCHA’s vendors to hire NYCHA residents or 
other low-income workers or document their 
attempts to do so. (Organizational Change) 
Snap Trap: A mechanical trap for rodents with a 
spring-activated mechanism. (Pests & Waste) 
Transformation Plan Project “Close-Out”: 
Once NYCHA determines that it has completed 
a project by completing all of the specific tasks 
associated with the project, it formally “closes 
out” the project in a presentation to the Monitor. 
While this close-out means that NYCHA believes 
it has completed the necessary tasks for this 
project, the Monitors will continue to assess its 
impact and how NYCHA is performing in this 
area. (Organizational Change) 
Verified Heating Complaints: Resident heating 
complaints where a NYCHA employee must 
perform work within an apartment to resolve the 
condition. (Heat) 
Voltage Reductions:  During heat waves, 
demand for electricity spikes as the use of 
cooling 
devices, 
such 
as 
fans 
and 
air 
conditioners, increases. If demand exceeds 
available capacity, equipment powering the 
electrical grid can overheat and fail, leading to 
blackouts. Reducing the voltage provided to the 
grid reduces overall electricity use, which lowers 
the risk of equipment failure or blackout. 
(Elevators) 
Work Plan: A work plan is a document that a 
mold inspector generates after their inspection, 
containing a description of the inspection and 
root cause findings, a list of next steps to address 
the mold and its root cause, and instructions on 
how to prevent mold and its root cause in the 
future. Under the Mold Standard Procedure, a 
work plan is automatically generated following a 
verified mold inspection. (Mold & Leaks) 
X-Ray Fluorescence (“XRF”) Testing: XRF 
testing is a non-destructive analytical technique 
that uses X-rays to determine the elemental 
composition 
of 
a 
material, 
including 
to 
determine whether lead is present. (Lead)
